❯ The 11/19 chat concluded with the following set of questions:

  ````
  - The five facts for the road to December 7–8
      (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/README.md):
      - Is your work code under git or TFS version control?
      - Can the work machine reach the npm registry?
      - What may leave that machine, and where will you present from?
      - Roughly how much code is in the largest repository?
      - Which browser, and does it have a graphics card?
    - Git: a branch per dev day, merged into main only at verified landings —
  yes
      or no? The guide's Git rule is unchanged until you answer.
    - Work items: does the form serve?
  ````

  I have answers to all of them, but still feel like my lack of replies
  yesterday has left the trajectory steering a little bit askew. Let me open
  some stuff up and try to start responding.

  >Is your work code under git or TFS version control?

  Both. I have 2 git repos I work on and then several other code projects I
  either work on or my software interacts with which are part of TFS. The two
  git repos correspond to two public-facing websites: one for legal clients and
  one for consumers to make payments. The TFS repos correspond to basically
  anything that talks to anything on-prem.

  >Can the work machine reach the npm registry?

  Sure but if I don't have to, that's much better. Like downloading an HTML
  file off of the Live Documentation hosted github pages site would be way
  cooler. Each copy of Live Documentation Explorer should be capable of writing
  a new fresh blank copy of Live Documentation Explorer, eagerly ready to take
  in its first scan!

  >What may leave that machine, and where will you present from?

  Nothing leaves the machine and I would just share my screen, which would
  mostly just be viewing my remote dev workstation. It's NOT heavily formal.
  This is just a "nice to have" for when I explain my portions of the
  cumulative applications estate of the enterprise because -- well -- that's
  the point of the software we're building!

  >Roughly how much code is in the largest repository?

  Any given scan-sized object is reasonable in size. I would scope to the
  solution level for most world map objects, where each world map object is a
  recursively scanned directory. No scanned object would even remotely approach
  a million lines. The t3 code scan is likely far more complex than any single
  scan I could do at my work. The engineers have separated out the
  applications really nicely in my opinion. Indeed, that's another thing about
  this software: it's clear to me that the more seasoned employees can see a
  cumulative architecture in their minds and it is sensible. But nobody has
  ever had the time to really draw it. And it changes frequently enough that we
  don't want to put in the effort to do so sometimes.

  >Which browser, and does it have a graphics card?

  Absolute crap VM. No idea on graphics card, but we're talking about a VM that
  has 8GB of RAM and a handful of GB of remaining storage available. We want
  this to be able to run on absolute garbage, even if some amount of
  precomputation time with reasonable progress indicators is required to do so.


  >Git: a branch per dev day, merged into main only at verified landings — yes
      or no? The guide's Git rule is unchanged until you answer.

  I'm fine with a branch per dev day. At my day job, we typically make a git
  branch per work item that we'd plan to get a code review on (PR or TFS code
  review request). But those repos are getting messy too. I am no git ninja,
  and I like to do the most vanilla thing and merge into Dev without squashing
  or rebasing, and when prod time comes, we merge into main without squashing
  or rebasing there either. So truthfully, I'm somewhat early in my journey
  towards git hygeine (despite using git for over 10 years), but I will say
  that the old-tech of TFS has rather impressed me with the simplicity of its
  mental model.

  >Work items: does the form serve?

  It'll do for now. Not madly in love with it but I think it's a reasonable
  starting point. Whether or not it should be as simple as markdown files is
  not yet clear to me.

  ---

  But let me zoom out here and say that I suspect I've accumulated quite a few
  decisions/questions that were raised for me and have failed to answer them or
  allowed defaults (your educated choices) to take effect. It would be very
  helpful to me if you could dispatch some builders to mine the November 15th,
  16th, 17th, 18th, and 19th chat logs to attempt to bubble up to _you_
  everything I failed to answer (you can use quite small models for that) and
  then you mine the bubbled-up list for what has actually been superseded
  versus what is still relevant. (If the bubbled-up might-be-relevant list is
  too large, you can request a builder to work through that might-be-relevant
  list and narrow it down to the actually-relevant list, but I wouldn't choose
  something smaller than Opus 5.5 High for that bit). Finally, on the list of
  what is still relevant and worth investigating, I want you to investigate
  yourself first. Whether or not you choose to offer a preferred approach or
  choice is up to you, but I want to see you engage with these questions in a
  fresh context and I want you to see how much churn occurs in these questions,
  with new findings superseding old ones often. A sampling of this churn
  firsthand will, I think, help ground your expectations and hopefully shake
  out just a tad of the (in my opinion) overcertainty which made it into the
  work item markdown files (which again, are a good start, but we should still
  be open to something better if it appears for a similar level of simplicity).


  I see that the last chat led to a fair bit of certainty, but I've seen "blind
  playtests" with builders come back with feedback that can change our
  trajectory in ways that supersedes what we've discussed. Our software
  crystallizes further, but when you use it, you can still feel rough edges
  innumerable. Much remains to be done and much uncertainty still remains on
  the horizon. That uncertainty is not a bad thing -- it's just the possibility
  space remaining open to us.

  ---

  Oh, should we mark the dev day as 11/20 or 11/21? It's like 8:40 PM eastern
  for me. I'm happy to go either way.

● Update(AGENTS.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      30  - **Clean workspace.** Remove your temporary outputs, and a bui
          lder's. A questionable file met during the work is investigated
           through its contents, consumers, git and chat provenance, and
          removed when no justified purpose remains; missing provenance a
          lone does not prove a file disposable. Preserve source captures
           and unique findings, and record consequential deletions.
      31  - **Roots and builders.** The root holds the vision, the conver
          sation with the owner, the design forks, the judgment of pictur
          es and the review of every handback. It delegates work whose ev
          idence is a long run of tool calls, and as a rule the writing a
          nd debugging of permanent code and tests, where a builder's dil
          igence is the more trustworthy; the root's part there is the id
          ea that spares the drudgery, the brief and the review. Builders
           run in parallel wherever the work divides into pieces that own
           different files (the owner, 2026-11-19: "We are in the age of
          parallel builders"); the number is bounded by what the root can
           brief, review and merge well, not by a count, and never by a d
          ate. A builder (Opus at high or extra-high effort, never max; a
           model that sees well where the result is judged by eye) takes
          a bounded brief that states the owner's preferences in their ow
          n words and what has already been tried, owns a named set of fi
          les, verifies what it builds (lint, the type-check, the audits
          and the tests of what calls the changed code, not its own cases
           alone), and hands back changed files, numbers, decided forks a
          nd unfinished work as text. In a worktree its first step is to
          reset to the commit the brief names, since worktrees have start

  >1. Does the page come before the package?

  Maybe this is naive of me, but this question surprises me a little:
  1.  I have aspired to have a page that can write a page for a fair while and
  spoken it a fair few times.
  2. With really awesome architecture, this shouldn't feel like a death
  sentence to switch between one and another (CLI versus browser window).
  Explore the possibility space that enables both if possible and elegant with
  great architecture. If not possible, explore the possibility space of what
  can be done with a page. If not enough is possible, then fall back to CLI.

  >2. Which browser is on that VM? If it is Firefox, the page can read a folder
  but not write beside the code.

  Chromium-based. Edge or Chrome.

  >3. Which shapes does your work code have most of? Scripts calling each

  a good idea of the kind of possibility space you need to accommodate by
  permuting combinations of the ingredients I've described. I feel that if I
  allow this to be too specified, I will allow for early victory celebration
  well before we've achieved what we've set out to do. It is not in my best
  interest to make only my use case look pristine and then look in disbelief at
  others who somehow can't get the same level of quality out of the software.
  Trying to tackle a wide variety of codebase shapes and languages and
  connectivities was a big big part of the Copilot era of this workspace, and
  it was _incredibly_ worthwhile. So many shapes I did not imagine (as they
  came in languages I'd never used) appeared and presented with interesting
  important design challenges. Their constraints helped shape considerably what
  we do and don't support, how deep or extensive our data model is, etc. Put
  another way, exploring a wide variety of possibility space enabled (forced,
  really) more parsimonious and simple design to accommodate the ability to
  measure and detect honestly in more contexts.

● I've taken your two messages as direction: both surfaces from one engine, the
  page alone if that fails, the command line alone last. Putting it to you as a
  fork was my mistake; the mining I'd just done showed you asking for the page
  on October 3, November 19 and today. Nothing below is waiting on you.

  The architecture I'm exploring

  There are three ways to get both, and I lean to the second:

  1. Swap Node's parts out when the page is built. This is what the November 19
     probe did, and the engine needs no change. But the engine does not know it
     has two homes, and I found today that it drifts (see the measurements).
  2. One small interface, two hosts. The engine asks its host for three things:
     list the folder, give me this file, write this. Designers call this "ports
     and adapters". About fifty places in the engine change.
  3. A scan as a pure function over a snapshot. The host loads everything into
     memory and the engine touches no disk at all. It is the simplest to reason
     about, but it would read your packages folders whole.

                a folder of code
                       │
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
      Node host                browser host
     (disk, git)           (the folder you pick)
          └────────────┬────────────┘
     list → choose what to leave out → read
                       │
                  the engine
                       │
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
    Markdown on disk         facts kept in the page
          └────────────┬────────────┘
               one Explorer page
       (shows the map; saves a blank or filled copy)

  - One page, two ways to fill it. The command line would write the same HTML
    file the page saves, already filled. Either copy can scan again or save a
    blank one.
  - The listing comes before any reading. Your ignore patterns need that step
    anyway.
  - What a page cannot do is anything that runs git: a repository's history and
    changed-only runs. Your two git repositories would get history from the
    command line, written into the same page. TFS folders have none on either
    surface.
  - What would overturn it: a reader that can't take its files through the
    interface without a second copy of its logic, or a scan too slow on your VM
    to bear even with a progress indicator.

  What I measured today

  I rebuilt the November 19 scanning page from today's engine and timed it (the
  write-up (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/sca
  n-in-a-tab/README.md)). "Slowed" is my machine imitating a weak one, not your
  VM.

  ┌────────────────────┬───────┬───────────┬────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │       Folder       │ Files │ Scan in a │ Slowed 4×, two │ Tab's peak   │
  │                    │       │    tab    │      cores     │    memory    │
  ├────────────────────┼───────┼───────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Estate sample      │    90 │     0.3 s │          1.6 s │       234 MB │
  ├────────────────────┼───────┼───────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ DNN Modules (C#,   │   845 │     3.9 s │         15.9 s │       299 MB │
  │ web forms)         │       │           │                │              │
  ├────────────────────┼───────┼───────────┼────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ DNN Library (C#)   │ 1,413 │    20.9 s │         80.2 s │       586 MB │
  └────────────────────┴───────┴───────────┴────────────────┴──────────────┘

  - The page probe broke within two days. It failed three times before it ran,
    each on something new the engine asked of Node, and nothing had flagged it.
  - The page draws nothing while it scans. The tab would look hung for the whole
    scan, so the scan must hand control back between files for a progress
    indicator to move.
  - Each file is read four to six times. One reading would cut the work most on
    the weakest machine.
  - Time looks bearable at solution size: seconds to a minute or two. Under
    heavy load the slowed 845-file scan took 59 s instead of 16.

  Your other points

  - Shapes. I won't ask again. What the readers mend next is ranked by breadth
    across real codebases, each measured against its language's own parser.
  - Ignore patterns before a scan. This is a new task: list the folder without
    reading it, show what would be scanned by folder with counts and which rule
    leaves each thing out, let you change it, then scan. The patterns would be
    the same on both surfaces, with .tfignore read where one exists. It will
    come to you as pictures and a trial.
  - 100k files. Today's probe page reads every file before scanning, which would
    choke on your packages folders. That is why the choice has to come before
    any reading.

  The work items now say this, mainly how the tool arrives
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/tool-arrives-on-windows.md) and what a scan leaves
  out (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/scan-leaves-no-trace.md). Your words are in
  Memory, and everything is committed on the day branch.

  ## What was built
  - **`packages/explorer/src/client/views/webgl-support.ts`** (new):
    - `drawsWebgl` asks a canvas for a WebGL 2 context with the renderer's
  attributes (`antialias`, `alpha`), then releases it with `WEBGL_lose_context`.
  A null answer or a throw counts as no.
    - `webglOffered()` asks once per page and remembers the answer.
    - Two pure helpers: `perspectiveShown(requested, offered)` and
  `perspectiveRemembered(shown, before, offered)`.
  - **`index.ts`**:
    - The question is asked at start, before any view.
    - `perspectiveShown` is applied at every place that sets the perspective:
  the initial state (a link or the remembered choice), `changePerspective` (the
  3D control and every other caller), and `restoreFromUrl` (Back/Forward). An
  entry that named 3D is rewritten to 2D there.
    - The remembered navigation writes `perspectiveRemembered`, so a stored 3D
  is never overwritten where WebGL is refused.
    - The 3D control gets `aria-disabled="true"`, not `disabled`, so it keeps
  its focus stop. Its name stays "3D". Its title, which is also its accessible
  description, is "3D needs WebGL, which this browser has turned off".
    - The amber "backward connections" cue on the 3D control stays unlit where
  3D is not offered.
  - **`styles/local.css`**: the 3D control's label fades by colour (55%) rather
  than by opacity. With opacity the focus ring faded to 2.52:1 on the toolbar's
  ground, under 3:1. At full strength it is 5.84:1 (Today's palette, computed).
  The look is otherwise the same as the toolbar's other disabled buttons.
  - **The toolbar had no pattern for "present but unavailable".** Its buttons
  use native `disabled` only briefly, during a perspective change. I chose
  `aria-disabled`: the design system already accepts `[aria-disabled="true"]` as
  a disabled-control selector, and native `disabled` would remove the focus
  stop.
  - **Design system** (`interaction-and-accessibility.mdmd.md`):
    - New rule in 10.2, with the owner's words and the probe as its reason: a
  view the browser cannot draw is not offered, its control says why, and a
  request for it shows the subject in a view the browser can draw.
    - The 10.2 browser rule no longer requires WebGL.
    - The 3.1 Disabled row now names `aria-disabled` for this case.
  - **Live Docs**: regenerated; three new docs carry authored Purpose and Notes.
  Generated regions were not hand-edited.

  ## Every place that needs WebGL, and what it does now
  - **`SpatialRenderer` / `forceGraphView.render()`**: never reached.
  `state.perspective` cannot become 3d. Every other entry into the 3D view is
  either guarded by `forceGraphInstance` or reachable only from 3D-only
  controls.
  - **`forceGraphBatches.ts`, `containedSphere.ts`**: run only inside the
  renderer, so never reached.
  - **`perspectiveTransition.ts`, `card-reading.ts`**: use 2D canvas contexts
  only, which work without WebGL. The transition never runs, since no 2D-to-3D
  change happens.
  - **Connections, Structure, Hops**: hidden in 2D, so absent.
  - **History**: a 3D link with a commit shows that moment in 2D, with the
  scrubber at its step (tested).
  - **Tuning's 3D group (Settle Hold etc.)**: still openable. Its levers change
  the stored 3D settings and draw nothing; no WebGL is asked for. It is left as
  is and noted as a doubt.
  - **Contact-sheet tool** (`scripts/`, outside my bounds): launched with the
  card, so it is unaffected. In a no-WebGL browser its "3d" rest wait on
  `aria-busy` would time out. Reported only.
  - **Layout lab**: drives the 2D page only.
  - **`webglcontextlost` today**: Three calls `preventDefault`, stops drawing,
  and draws again on restore. `forceGraphBatches.ts` already forces a redraw on
  `webglcontextrestored`. A context never restored leaves the 3D map blank with
  its controls standing, and nothing throws. Not mended; it is written into the
  rule's status.

  ## Decided forks
  - `aria-disabled`, not `disabled`, for the reasons above.
  - A remembered 3D stays 3D in storage: where nothing was stored, 2D is
  written.

  - The history in a 3D link opens at its moment in 2D.

  screen readers as the description; this is the toolbar-wide state today.

  ### 4. Task 4 (`a0da03f59`)
  - **Comments:** the two comments now say they hold only with `--keep-workers`:
  the description of `work()` (and "what a kept worker keeps") in
  `summarizeChange.ts`, and `RegenerationContext.memory` in `regenerate.ts`.
  - **Closing line, default mode:** it now reads "N of M changes regenerated
  were summarized again in a fresh worker: …". Kept workers keep "decided by a
  fresh process". The test asserts the new line, and that "decided by a fresh
  process" is absent by default.
  - **The "had summarized earlier changes" branch:** I left it. It is reachable
  only with kept workers, and its words are true there.
  - **`glob`:**
    - It is now in the generator's manifest and the lock. `npm install
  --package-lock-only` changed only that one line.
    - Its entry is removed from `LOCK_ROOTS`.
    - The guard now holds that, with no roots at all, only `tsx` is found
  undeclared; its can-fail case uses `tsx`.
    - The keyed-entries fixture now has the generator declaring `glob`.
    - A dated note is in the decisions log.
    - `history-lens-lock.test.ts` (a real worker under the permission audit)
  passes.

  **Left for you to decide, with the rule I would give each:**
  - **`--remove-superseded` across modes.** The default mode and
  `--keep-workers` key different lenses for the same code. `supersededCaches`
  takes every cache whose key is not the current lens's as superseded, so a run
  in either mode deletes the other mode's cache, which supersedes nothing. Rule:
  a cache counts as superseded only by a lens that differs from it in something
  other than the mode. Concretely, also compute the current code's key with
  `keepWorkers` flipped (`lensOf`) and never remove that cache.
  - **`pool.json` takes the last mode's lens.** There is one record per history
  folder. `nextPoolRecord` resets it whenever the lens differs, so alternating
  modes size each run by the other mode's peak memory, which genuinely differs
  (a kept worker grows, a one-commit worker does not), and the change count
  restarts. Rule: one record per lens (for example `<lens>/pool.json` inside its
  own cache folder, or a map keyed by lens), so that each mode is sized by its
  own measurement.

  - Not committed: "Export on demand, I'd expect."
  - The stored form: "Don't just settle for JSON by default", chosen by research
    and proved by measurement, with HDF5 among the candidates.
  - Documents as nodes: with the mirror gone, a Markdown document can itself be
    a node of the map.

  None of that is built. It was ordered to follow the readers' landing, and the
  readers are now joined and under independent review.

  The corrected picture:

                a folder of code
                       │
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
      Node host                browser host
     (disk, git)           (the folder you pick)
          └────────────┬────────────┘
     list → choose what to leave out → read
                       │
                  the engine
                       │
       a scan's facts, as data, in one stored form
          ┌────────────┴────────────┐
    kept in a folder that       kept inside
    ignores itself              the page
          └────────────┬────────────┘
               one Explorer page
       (shows the map; saves a blank or filled copy)

     Markdown: exported from either, when you ask

  Your catch makes the design simpler in two ways:

  - The store's trial and the page are one move. A page can only hold a scan's
    facts as data inside itself, so both hosts would write the same stored
    facts.
  - The format research gains one requirement. The form must be writable by a
    page and carried inside the one HTML file it saves. Any candidate, HDF5
    included, has to be shown to work there.

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Review of `readers-gathering` at a0da03f59 (range a5d29b90f..a0da03f59)

  I worked in the worktree at
  /workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-a2a353021a0cccd0b,
  reset to a0da03f59. I changed nothing and committed nothing; `git status` is
  clean. The scratch folder readers-reviewer/ has been removed. One subagent
  (general-purpose, Opus, "Run PowerShell and SQL graders") ran the graders
  listed at the end while I read the code. Unless a finding says "by reading", I
  showed it by running the generator CLI (`npx tsx --tsconfig
  ./tsconfig.base.json scripts/live-docs/generate.ts --workspace <case>`) and
  reading the Dependencies section it wrote.

  ### Findings

  **1. Wrong connection: a C# nested type inherited from a base class.**
  - **Where:** `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/csharp.ts`,
  `containersOf` (~392–405) and `resolveSegments` (~432–452). Shown by running.
  - **Input:** one SDK project `App/App.csproj` holding three files:
    - `Base.cs`: `namespace App { public class Base { public class Options { } }
  }`
    - `Other.cs`: `namespace App.Other { public class Options { } }`
    - `Derived.cs`: `using App.Other; namespace App { public class Derived :
  Base { public Options Settings { get; set; } } }`
  - **The reader gives:** `Derived.cs → Base` (correct) and `Derived.cs →
  Other.cs (Other.Options)` (wrong).
  - **Right:** `Options` binds to `Base.Options`. In C#, looking up a simple
  name inside a type searches that type's members, inherited ones included,
  before any namespace or `using`. The reader's containers are the enclosing
  types, the namespaces and the usings; base types are never searched.
  - **Oracle:** the C# compiler. I compiled the same code with `public
  Base.Options Check() => Settings;` added (dotnet 10): it builds with 0 errors,
  so `Settings` is `Base.Options`.
  - The order was already like this on main. The branch's header now claims
  "resolved the way the compiler resolves it".

  **2. Wrong connection: a C# alias to the non-generic type links every arity.**
  - **Where:** `csharp.ts` `resolveChain` (~457–470). It sets `counts` to
  `undefined` for an alias's segments, and `oneType` (~350–357) then admits
  every arity. Shown by running.
  - **Input:** one project, `P/P.csproj`, holding:
    - `Helper.cs`: `namespace Lib { public class Helper { } }`
    - `HelperOfT.cs`: `namespace Lib { public class Helper<T> { } }`
    - `Use.cs`: `using H = Lib.Helper; namespace App { public class Use { public
  H Item { get; set; } } }`
  - **The reader gives:** `Use.cs → Helper.cs` and `Use.cs → HelperOfT.cs`.
  - **Right:** only `Helper.cs`. The alias target `Lib.Helper` has no
  type-argument list, so it names the arity-0 type (C# spec, using alias
  directives). The arity can be read from the alias text, which the code comment
  says cannot be done.
  - **Lesser, same function:** `oneType`'s doc comment says "The reader does not
  yet match a use's type arguments to an arity", but `resolveSegments` now
  filters by arity. The comment is stale.

  **3. Wrong connection: a commented-out `ProjectReference` is read as real.**
  - **Where:**
    - `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/project.ts:25`
  (`PROJECT_REFERENCE`). This was already on main.
    - `dotnetProjects.ts:30`, new on this branch. It feeds C# name reach.
    - The same comment-blindness applies to `PACKAGE_REFERENCE`,
  `ASSEMBLY_REFERENCE` and the `.slnx` regex in `solution.ts:25` (by reading).
  - Shown by running.
  - **Input:**
    - `A/A.csproj`: `<Project Sdk="Microsoft.NET.Sdk"><ItemGroup><!--
  <ProjectReference Include="../B/B.csproj" /> --></ItemGroup></Project>`
    - `A/UseIt.cs`: `using Lib; namespace A { public class UseIt { public Thing
  Item { get; set; } } }`
    - `B/B.csproj`: an SDK project.
    - `B/Thing.cs`: `namespace Lib { public class Thing { } }`
  - **The reader gives:** `A.csproj → B/B.csproj` and `UseIt.cs → B/Thing.cs`.
  - **Right:** no reference. XML comments are not elements, so MSBuild never
  sees it. B is outside A's reach; at most the "declared only outside the
  project's reach" notice should fire.
  - Commented-out references are common in legacy `.csproj` files, so this
  probably happens on real repositories.

  **4. Wrong connection: PowerShell's own module-less functions and its aliases
  are missing from `SHIPPED_COMMANDS`.**
  - **Where:** `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/powershell.builtins.ts`,
  used in `powershell.ts` `calledFiles` (~1857). Shown by running.
  - **Input:**
    - `lib/Helpers.ps1`: `function Pause { Read-Host 'Press Enter' | Out-Null }`
  and `function Invoke-Thing { }`
    - `run.ps1`: `Write-Host 'Building'`, `Pause`, `Invoke-Thing`, loading
  nothing.
  - **The reader gives:** `run.ps1 → lib/Helpers.ps1` with `Helpers.Pause` and
  `Helpers.Invoke-Thing`.
  - **Right:** `Pause` is PowerShell's own built-in function, not the
  workspace's. In that script, pwsh 7.7 reports `(Get-Command
  Pause).CommandType` = Function with no module, and so it does for `help`,
  `prompt`, `oss`, `TabExpansion2` and `cd..`.
  - **Why the list misses them:** it was gathered with `Get-Command -Module`,
  which lists no module-less built-ins. It has no aliases either (`select`,
  `sort`, `%`, `?`, `gci`, …), and an alias outranks a function in command
  lookup. On Windows, `mkdir` and `more` are built-in functions as well.
  - **Oracle:** PowerShell's command lookup (alias > function > cmdlet), run in
  pwsh.
  - **The grader cannot see this:** `scripts/oracle/powershell-wires.ts:16`
  imports `SHIPPED_COMMANDS` from the adapter's own module. Its header ("share
  no code with the adapter") is therefore not true.

  **5. The PowerShell grader cannot fail on its rule, only on parse facts.**
  - **Where:** `scripts/oracle/powershell-wires.ts` `wiresOf` (~199–215). By
  reading.
  - It re-implements "loaded, else unique" line for line: the same
  test-exclusion and the same `SHIPPED_COMMANDS`, and `resolveArchetype`
  imported from the engine. Its agreement figures measure tree-sitter's facts
  against PowerShell's parser, not whether the rule draws true wires. Findings 4
  and 6 cannot show up in it.
  - The script's own header does say it "scores the adapter against [the rules],
  not against a run". Its claims of independence should be narrowed to that.

  **6. A PowerShell notice fires for a load the text does fix, and the module
  name is then dropped.**
  - **Where:** `powershell.ts` ~1402–1411 (the `import-module` case). Shown by
  running.
  - **Input:** `run.ps1` = `$m = 'Pester'`, then `Import-Module $m`.
  - **The reader gives:** the notice "dot-sources and imports whose path the
  text does not fix, left unlinked: run.ps1: line 2: Import-Module $m".
  - **Why:** `pathValue` returns "Pester", but that is not a module path, so the
  code falls to the branch that keeps only literal strings. No `Pester`
  dependency is kept; in my case it appeared only because a third line imported
  it literally.
  - **Right:** a kept name `Pester` and no notice. The variable is a constant
  assigned once at file scope.

  **7. A change to the meaning of tests in every language, carried in under the
  PowerShell reader.**
  - **Where:** `packages/engine/src/live-docs/archetype.ts` ~99–143. The
  test-folder rule now reads only `path.posix.dirname`, and `^tests?\.` was
  added for all languages.
  - **Shown by running** main's archetype.ts beside the branch's, with the
  default configuration:

    | File | main | branch |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | `src/test-utils.tsx` | test | implementation |
    | `src/test-helpers.ts` | test | implementation |
    | `lib/test-setup.js` | test | implementation |
    | `src/Test-Thing.cs` | test | implementation |

  - The code comment justifies the change by PowerShell's `Test-DbaPath.ps1`
  alone. A JS/TS `test-utils.tsx` (Testing Library's custom-render convention)
  is test-support code; it now reads as working code.
  - This is a design fork the root should decide. As written, it is not confined
  to `.ps1`/`.psm1`/`.psd1`.

  **8. Lesser: SQL names across databases and schemas.**
  - **Where:** `openings.ts` `sqlObjectName` (~303–307) and `sqlNameMatches`
  (~972–977). Shown by running.
  - **Input:**
    - `tables.sql` creates `dbo.Orders`; `archive.sql` creates `archive.Orders`.
    - `procs.sql` has `CREATE PROCEDURE dbo.ListOrders AS SELECT Id FROM
  Orders;` and a second procedure that reads `SisterDb..Orders` and
  `SisterDb.dbo.Orders`.
  - **(a)** The one-part `Orders` inside `dbo.ListOrders` links to both
  `dbo.Orders` and `archive.Orders`. T-SQL binds one: the module's or default
  schema, then `dbo`. The rule is old, and `sql-compare.ts` lines 45–48 adopt it
  "as the product matches them", so the grader cannot fail on it.
  - **(b)** `SisterDb..Orders` (a same-server, three-part name) is marked
  `linked` and drawn as contract edges to both local `Orders` tables.
    - Since this branch, `slice(-2).filter(nonempty)` reduces it to the one-part
  `orders`. Main kept `sisterdb.orders`, which matched nothing.
    - Meanwhile `SisterDb.dbo.Orders` is drawn as a source (same-database) edge
  to `dbo.Orders`.
    - The same cross-database reference is therefore classified two ways
  depending on spelling. The grader does not grade the basis.

  **9. Lesser, by reading: ASP.NET `~/`.**
  - **(a)** A page in web project SiteA linking
  `MasterPageFile="~/Site.Master"`, where only web project SiteB holds that
  file, is linked to `SiteB/Site.Master` (shown by running). That is right only
  if SiteA is deployed into SiteB; for two independent sites it is a cross-site
  edge. The header acknowledges the trade-off.
  - **(b)** `<script src>` in the same file (`aspnet.ts` ~96–118) resolves `~/`
  and `/` from the workspace root, not from the project folder that directives
  use. An unresolved src is dropped with no kept name and no notice. This
  behaviour was already on main and is now inconsistent with the branch's own
  `~/` rule.

  estate-directory on Sonnet medium and Haiku default, two each, plus repo-uses
  on Sonnet and Haiku, one each, under each value. They are named

  One thing the instrument hides: the window waits for the page to come to rest
  (up to 15 s) before the tester's first picture. So "Reading, Then Neighbours"
  testers never saw the reading-size moment; their first picture is the same
  neighbours frame as under "Neighbours". The second of reading size a person
  would see was not measured.

  ### What each arrival looks like
  Text sizes are rough line heights, by my eye on the 1,280 × 800 pictures.

  because the map keeps its names at a readable size and drops content instead:
    - every description is gone;

    - `index.ts`'s one row reads "Inter…".

  - **Reading Size** (`main`): this is the owner's "one deliberately framed
  card", with the file's own words. Its cost is that the answer to "what uses
  this file" lies past the edges.

  who wanted more found it, by Fit or by the reader. Today 2 of 4 estate-uses

  find what lies beyond a reading-size card. It does show that a stranger

  fits, and the cut-off picture comes from opening `Data/`, which Arrival does
  not frame.

  ### Why the second field says "Nothing connects to Scan root"
  It comes from a general rule, with the root treated as an ordinary directory
  pin.
  - **`search/search-bar.ts`:** line 121, `destinationGroup.hidden = !newest`,
  draws the second field whenever there is a newest pin. Lines 126–127 choose
  its words:
    ```ts
    const connects = !reach || reach.forward.size + reach.reverse.size > 0;
    destinationInput.placeholder = connects || !newest ? askWords : `Nothing
  connects to ${newest.name}`;
    ```
    `askWords` is the field's own placeholder, "Path to…" (line 78, from
  `shared/template.html`).
  - **`index.ts` lines 1170–1175:** `reach = reachOf(endFiles(newest, ground),
  …)`.
  - **`history/moment-paths.ts` lines 61–68:** `endFiles` stands a directory pin
  for every file under it. **`views/localView/directory-state.ts` lines
  28–30:** `under` says the scan root, `""`, holds everything. So the root pin
  stands for every file in the map.
  - **`pathfind.ts` `reachOf` (lines 124–134):** it uses the engine's
  `reachFrom` (`packages/engine/src/live-docs/pathfind.ts` lines 108–116), which
  drops the start files themselves. With every file a start, nothing is
  reachable, so the answer for the root is always "nothing connects".

  This is now in the README's "Scan root" section.

● The independent review of the readers found nine faults, and two settings were
  tried on strangers. Nothing is waiting on you.

  The readers' review

  The reviewer found the three merges sound and the engine's guarantees intact.
  The nine faults are in how names are resolved and in what two graders claim;
  four are older than this branch. The ones that draw a false line on the map:

  - C# inherited nested type: a type inherited from a base class gets linked to
    a different class of the same name.
  - C# alias: an alias to Helper also links Helper<T>.
  - Commented-out project reference: it is read as real, which then widens what
    C# names can reach.
  - PowerShell built-ins: a call to one of PowerShell's own commands (Pause, or
    an alias like select) wires to a workspace function of that name.
  - "Test" for every language: a change made on PowerShell's evidence altered
    what counts as a test everywhere, so test-utils.tsx stopped being one. I've
    confined it to PowerShell files.
  - SQL schemas: a bare table name links to that table in every schema, where
    T-SQL would pick one.

  The PowerShell and SQL graders each borrowed part of the reader's own rule, so
  they could not fail on it; both are being made independent. All nine went
  back to the builder, each as a failing test first, with the right answer taken
  from the language's own tool. The same reviewer then makes a second pass
  before the history rebuild and main. The full list is on the work item
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/readers-on-main.md).

  ## The check is built and committed

  All three levels are in one commit, `8f4a86d2e`, on `page-check` (branched
  from `501a993f2`, not pushed). Everything passes. There were no changes under
  `packages/` or Memory/Work.

  **Added**
  - `tests/page-scan/`: `build-page.mjs`, `page-entry.mjs`, `node-needs.mjs`,
  `node-needs.json`, `node-needs.test.ts`, `node-scan.ts`, `fixtures.ts`,
  `fixtures.test.ts`, `compare.ts`, `compare.test.ts`, `differences.json`.
  - `tests/page-scan/stand-ins/`: `calls`, `fs`, `fs-promises`, `path`, `glob`,
  `url`, `process`, `child_process`, `require-resolve`, `engine-root`,
  `web-tree-sitter` (all `.mjs`).
  - `tests/e2e/page-scan.spec.ts`.

  record's Turn 2.
  - The evidence README at `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-
  11-21/Probes/page-check/README.md`.

  **Changed**
  - `scripts/e2e-bundles.mjs`: a separate `SCANNING_PAGE` step after the suite's
  bundles, writing `dist/page-scan/`.
  - `vitest.config.ts`: the integration project now also takes
  `tests/page-scan/**/*.test.ts`.
  - `tests/tsconfig.json`: includes `page-scan/**/*.ts`.
  - `.mdmd/layer-2/internal-tooling.mdmd.md`: new section "The engine in a
  page".
  - Notes added to the docs of `e2e-bundles.mjs`, `vitest.config.ts` and
  `tests/tsconfig.json`.

  ## Where I departed from the brief, and why
  - **All 45 folders**, not a chosen few. The list is read from
  `tests/integration/programs/*/*` and `tests/integration/fixtures/*`, so new
  samples join by themselves. The owner's Turn 2 asked for breadth, and the
  whole set costs about 150 s.
  - **Same clock on both sides.** Page and Node get the same `now`, so
  `Generated At` is compared like any other line instead of being set aside.
  That is stricter than the brief.
  - **Level 1 sits in the integration project, not unit.** It bundles the engine
  with esbuild, which takes seconds.
  - **The needs list has more than the brief asked for.** It also records:
    - Node globals the engine names (`__dirname`, `require.resolve`,
  `process.env`);
    - what packages from `node_modules` import of Node;
    - `hostedByNode`: TypeScript's `ts.sys`, a reach into Node that is not an
  import. It turned out to be the one real cause of every difference.
  - **Stand-ins rewritten to behave as Node does:**
    - `path` follows Node's own POSIX code instead of `pathe`, which turns
  backslashes into slashes and is only here as a dependency of a dependency.
    - `fs` errors carry Node's `code`, `syscall` and `path`, and
  `statSync`/`lstatSync` honour `throwIfNoEntry`.
    - `glob` walks through the `fs` that `gitIgnored.ts` hands it, and returns
  folders when `nodir` is unset, as glob does.
    - `web-tree-sitter` loads each grammar from the path the engine computes, so
  the choice between the engine's own PowerShell grammar and the bundled ones
  is made as under Node.
  - **Stand-ins are wired by an esbuild plugin, not `alias`.** An engine module
  importing any Node module without a stand-in stops the build, naming both.
  esbuild already refuses a named import a stand-in lacks.
  - **Refusal of a stale page.** `dist/page-scan/inputs.json` holds a digest of
  every input. The spec refuses a page built from other sources and says to run
  `npm run e2e:bundles`.
  - **Calls made while the page loads are counted separately**
  (`callsOnLoading`).
  - **The page leaves `.git` and `node_modules` out of a folder it is handed**,
  as the probe did. A real repository would therefore be scanned in the page as
  if it were in no repository; no fixture has either folder.

  ## Fixtures
  - **Result:** 43 identical. `fixtures/entry-points` has 4 differing files and
  `typescript/tidepool` has 2, all in the ledger.
  - **Readers:** all 19 registered readers are reached; none is missing.
    - sql: only `csharp/estate`.
    - powershell: `powershell/rollout`, `fixtures/powershell-compendium`.
    - pyproject: `python/ledger`, `fixtures/manifests`.

  file. Other source files are read 3 to 5 times.

  ## What of Node the scan's path needs today
  - 36 engine/generator modules in the bundle reach Node; 13 of them only
  through `node:path`. No history module is in the bundle.
  - **`fs`**: lstatSync, readdir (callback), readdirSync, readFileSync,
  statSync, and promises access/mkdir/readFile/stat/writeFile.
  - **`fs/promises`**: mkdir, open, readFile, readdir, rm, rmdir, stat,
  writeFile.
  - **`path`**: 14 members, including `posix.*`.
  - **Others**: `url` fileURLToPath/pathToFileURL, `process` env/nextTick,
  `child_process` spawnSync/execFile, `glob`.
  - **Globals**: `__dirname` and `require.resolve` (treeSitter), `process.env`
  (gitUtils, only under `--changed`).
  - **TypeScript's host on Node**: `ts.sys.useCaseSensitiveFileNames` in
  `json.ts`.
  - **Calls summed over the 45 scans:**

  | Call | Count |
  | --- | ---: |
  | path.relative | 6,800 |
  | path.join | 5,974 |
  | path.resolve | 3,820 |
  | path.dirname | 3,496 |

  - Never called on these folders: `readFileSync`, `rm`, `rmdir`, `nextTick`,
  and `child_process` entirely.
  - **Against the November 19 hand count:**
    - Git is no longer asked anything for a folder with no `.git`; Nov 19 asked
  `git check-ignore` once.
    - `existsSync` is gone from the scan's path.
    - New: `node:process`, `lstatSync`, `throwIfNoEntry`, the callback
  `readdir`, `access`, `readFileSync`, `dom.ts`'s `readdir`, `open`.
    - The surprise is `ts.sys`, which no hand count of imports could see.

  ## The differences and their cause
  - **All six are the engine's**, from one cause. `compilerPaths` in
  `adapters/json.ts` builds a config host with
  `ts.sys.useCaseSensitiveFileNames`. TypeScript only builds `ts.sys` under
  Node, so in a page it is undefined and the read throws. The generator then
  reports `package.json` as a file it could not map. As a result there is no doc
  and no index node for it, and the files it names as entries lose their Entry
  lines.
  - **What the engine would need:** its own case-sensitivity setting, chosen
  where the scan knows the folder, instead of TypeScript's host. Then those six
  ledger entries stop occurring and must be removed.
  - **No stand-in fault remains.** On the first run all of the estate's C#
  failed because the tree-sitter stand-in looked for the runtime `.wasm` at the
  wrong path. I fixed it in the stand-in.

  ## Each new assertion shown red once
  - An `existsSync` added to the `fs` stand-in: "exports existsSync, which the
  scan's path does not name".
  - `lstatSync` removed from the committed list: "needs other things of Node
  than node-needs.json says".
  - `url` removed from `STAND_INS`: the build refused, naming `pathUtils.ts` and
  `node:url`.
  - A doc altered in the page: an `unlisted` difference.
  - A fake ledger entry: a `gone` entry.
  - A stand-in edited after the page was built: every case refused ("calls.mjs
  changed").
  - Each was restored and the full set rerun green.

  ## When `readers-gathering` lands with `createHash`
  - The build will refuse: "…adapters/powershell.ts imports node:crypto, which
  the scanning page has no stand-in for…".
  - The gate will stop at the integration stage. `e2e:bundles` will also fail at
  its last step, after the other bundles are built.
  - **Recommendation:** since the digest only keys a cache, compute it with a
  small pure-JS hash in the engine (for example FNV-1a), so no new Node
  dependence appears.
  - If it must be a SHA, write `stand-ins/crypto.mjs` with a working synchronous
  JS `createHash`, then run `node tests/page-scan/node-needs.mjs --write` and
  review the diff.
  - Not a throwing stand-in: every PowerShell file would fail in the page, and
  the ledger would record a stand-in's refusal instead of the engine's
  behaviour.

  ## The interface I'd give the engine, having seen every call
  - **Five shapes cover nearly all of it:**
    - walks (glob);
    - reads of bytes, including a head read;
    - is-file/is-folder probes, mostly synchronous (`statSync` alone 2,163
  calls, which a directory handle cannot answer);
    - listings of one folder;
    - writes, done only by the generator and `graphFiles`.
  - **So:** one `WorkspaceFiles` taken once per scan:
    - `paths()` from a single walk, serving every glob by matching in memory;
    - a synchronous `kind(path)` answered from that listing;
    - `read(path)` and `head(path, n)` returning bytes;
    - plus a `DocsSink` for `write` and `remove`.
  - **Alongside it:** git's word as an optional ignore provider, and the
  case-sensitivity setting passed in explicitly instead of `ts.sys`.
  - **The one remaining stand-in:** `path`, as a small POSIX module the engine
  owns.

● The first hard evidence on your architecture question is in: the engine
  already maps code identically in a browser page and under Node, for almost
  everything we have.

  The page check

  A standing test now bundles the engine for a browser and maps every sample
  folder twice, once in a real Chromium page and once under Node, comparing
  every output file. I re-ran it myself before merging it into the day branch.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                                    │                          │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Sample folders mapped both ways    │                       45 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Readers exercised                  │                   all 19 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Identical output                   │                       43 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Differing                          │ 2 (six files, one cause) │
  ├────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Scan time over all 45, page / Node │            6.3 s / 9.6 s │
  └────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  - The one cause: the engine asks TypeScript's Node host whether filenames are
    case-sensitive. A page has no such host, and Windows and Linux answer
    differently, so it was also a hidden difference between machines. The
    builder is replacing it with a fixed rule: names are read as written.
  - Drift now shows up. The bundle refuses to build if the engine imports a Node
    module that has no browser replacement, and a committed list of what the
    scan needs from Node changes visibly in review.
  - It will catch the readers' join. The PowerShell reader uses Node's crypto
    module for a cache key; I've sent that to the readers' builder to replace
    before the join lands.

  What the engine needs from Node today

  This is counted from every call made during those 45 scans:

  - Path arithmetic: the bulk of calls, and it needs no host at all.
  - "Is this a file?" probes: 2,163 of them, asked synchronously. A browser's
    folder handle can only answer asynchronously, so these must be answered from
    a listing taken up front.
  - File reads: 2,144. Each file is read 3 to 5 times, and one go.mod 30 times.
  - Folder walks: five per scan.
  - Writes: only the generator makes them.

  So the interface is small: list the folder once, answer "is this a file" from
  that listing, read a file's bytes, and write results somewhere. The
  listing-first step your ignore patterns need turns out to be the step a page
  needs anyway.

  The same builder is now writing that interface up as a design document for you
  to read, with the alternatives and what would overturn it. It builds nothing
  beyond the case-sensitivity fix.

  The numbers and the per-folder table are in the builder's account (AI-Agent-Wo
  rkspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/page-check/README.md).

  Still running: the readers' mends, the WebGL mend, the format research, and
  this design write-up.

  ## A cost to weigh
  The WebGL question runs on every page load, so it now costs something in
  browsers that do have WebGL, 2D-only pages included. Measured in fresh pages:
  on the graphics card the first page in a browser takes 731 ms, the next ones
  about 50 ms; in software about 30 ms. A 2D-only visit pays this without asking
  for 3D. Asking only at the first request for 3D, and setting the control's
  state when the page is idle, would avoid it. I did not build that; it is your
  call.

  ## A. The mend in the engine

  **The stop condition did not trigger.** Before choosing anything I parsed
  every `tsconfig*.json` in this repository and its fixtures (13 files,
  including `tsconfig.base.json`, the packages' and `tests/tsconfig.json`) with
  `useCaseSensitiveFileNames` true and then false. All 13 gave the same
  `outDir`, `rootDir` and `rootDirs`. So did a made-up config with capitals in
  `outDir`, `rootDir`, `rootDirs`, `declarationDir` and its `extends`.

  **Test first.** In `packages/engine/src/live-docs/adapters/json.test.ts` I
  added: "reads a manifest beside a tsconfig the same whatever TypeScript's host
  on Node says of names, and with no host at all".
  - It runs the JSON reader three times: with `ts.sys` as it is, with
  `useCaseSensitiveFileNames: false`, and with `ts.sys` undefined. It expects
  the same result each time.
  - `ts.sys` is a getter that can't be reassigned or reconfigured, so the case
  stands in for the whole `typescript` module with `vi.doMock`, then unmocks it.
  - Before the mend it failed on the third run: "TypeError: Cannot read
  properties of undefined (reading 'useCaseSensitiveFileNames')".

  **The mend.** In `compilerPaths` (`adapters/json.ts`) the setting is now
  `useCaseSensitiveFileNames: true`. The comment above it gives your reasons in
  plain words. That was the only change under `packages/`; the test file and the
  two Live Docs are the rest.

  - `fs.readFileSync` is now called in the page (2 calls in each of those two
  fixtures): the tsconfig really is being read there now.

  `json.ts` no longer lists `typescript: sys.useCaseSensitiveFileNames`, and
  `hostedByNode` became `{}`.
  - **This departs from what you asked.** You said `hostedByNode` gone. I left

  ## B. The design document

  `.mdmd/layer-3/engine-and-hosts.mdmd.md` opens with your italic line exactly
  as given. It is linked only from internal-tooling's section on the engine in a
  page. The regeneration added one Cited By entry, on `node-needs.json`'s doc.

  **What it says, section by section:**
  1. **What was asked:** Turn 2 and Turn 3 quoted exactly, Turn 4's
  "Interesting. Wasn't that piece going to change?", and the store's trial with
  "I think it's time to try, eh?" and "Export on demand, I'd expect." Both hosts
  keep a scan's facts as data.
  2. **The idea:** a redrawn picture, and five sentences on ports and adapters,
  each tied to today's code (`packages/engine` + `generator`,
  `scripts/live-docs/generate.ts`, `tests/page-scan/page-entry.mjs`, the
  stand-ins). It notes that `LanguageAdapter` is already the same idea one level
  down.
  3. **The interface:**
     - The five shapes of call (walks, reads, probes, folder contents, writes)
  and what each becomes.
     - The host gives `list()` and `read()`, plus `head()` to save work, and
  takes the facts.
     - The engine builds `WorkspaceFiles` once per run over those: `paths`, a
  synchronous `kind`, `children`, a memoized `read`, and `head`. A table lists,
  for each method, the modules it replaces (from `node-needs.json`) and the call
  counts over the 45 folders.
     - Paths (34 modules, about 25,700 calls), file URLs, filename case and the
  grammars move out of the host entirely.
     - The listing is taken first, and what is left out is decided on it by one
  function both hosts share: `.gitignore`, `.tfignore` by TFS's rules, a
  starting list of build folders, and the person's own patterns.
     - Each file is read once, with the five causes of today's 3 to 5 reads
  named.
  4. **What cannot cross into a page:** git's word on what is ignored,
  `--changed`, and the history, each with what the page does instead.
  5. **The readers' cross-file tables:** C#'s type table, a project's reach,
  PowerShell's tables, the symbol index, and the neighbour probes. Described as
  shapes, from day-branch code with the joined readers noted. One general
  sentence: "a table is a function of the listing and of the bytes of the files
  it names."
  6. **The road:** nine steps, each deleting named stand-in members. The first
  move is file URLs out of `pathUtils.ts`: it is small, changes no fact, and
  deletes a whole stand-in. Four places where the road could fork are named.
  7. **The alternatives:**
     - stand-ins for good: what it costs to keep;
     - a scan as a pure function over a snapshot: in effect the port with eager
  reads;
     - the engine in a Web Worker: beside the port, not instead of it;
     - Node running inside the page: not recommended.
  8. **What would overturn it, or is not known:** time and memory at your scale,
  browser policies, a file opened from disk being marked as downloaded, listing
  100k files in a page, a reader that needs a second copy of its logic,
  synchronous library hosts, and the store's form.

  **Places where the code did not match the brief:**
  - **"A project's reach" for C# types is not on `day/2026-11-21`.** It exists
  only on `readers-gathering`. On the day branch, `project.ts` just links
  `<ProjectReference>`s, so the document describes both.
  - **"PowerShell's table of definitions" is also only on `readers-gathering`.**
  The day branch has only a case-folded lookup of paths.
    - The joined reader reads each PowerShell file a second time for its own
  analysis, and compares the two texts by an FNV-1a fingerprint. Reading once
  would make that comparison unnecessary.
    - That fingerprint is what already replaced `createHash`, as recommended
  last time.
  - **One genuine design constraint I found:** TypeScript's config host must
  answer synchronously, so a `tsconfig` and everything it extends has to be read
  ahead before TypeScript is called. Section 5 says so.
  - **Not checked:** the Chromium policy names in section 8 are from memory, and
  the document says so.

  **B's checks, all passing:** live-docs:generate, live-docs:lint (exit 0),
  docs:links:enforce, and slopcop markdown, symbols and assets (0 issues each).

    every call it counted, and I read and corrected it.

  What the design says

  - What a host gives the engine: a listing of the folder's file names, a file's
    bytes on request, and optionally just a file's first bytes.
  - What a host takes back: the scan's facts as data, and word of how far the
    scan has come.
  - Listing first: the folder is listed before anything is read. What gets left
    out is decided on that listing by one function both hosts share, drawing on
    .gitignore, .tfignore, a starting list of build folders and your own
    patterns. That choice can be shown to you and changed before the scan runs.
  - Each file read once. Today it is three to five times.
  - What cannot cross into a page: anything that runs git. The history is
    computed by the command line and reaches the page as facts.
  - The road: nine steps, each removing one of the browser stand-ins, with the
    page check proving after each that nothing changed.
  - Alternatives and unknowns: section 7 sets it beside three alternatives.
    Section 8 lists what would overturn it, chiefly scan time and memory at your
    scale on your VM, and whether your company's browser policy allows the
    folder picker.

  My two corrections

  - The listing carries names only. The draft had it carry each file's size and
    modified time, but a page can only learn those by opening each file, which
    on 100k files is the cost the listing exists to avoid.
  - The scan reports progress and hands control back between files. You asked
    for progress indicators, and today a scan in a tab freezes the page until it
    ends.

  Building the interface waits for the readers' join to land, since it touches
  every reader's file.

  research.

  ### Before the findings: the C# graders have two weaknesses of their own
  I built the reviewer's cases for findings 1–3 as one fixture: `App`

  - **Finding 1, confirmed by the compiler.** scip-dotnet draws `Derived.cs →
  Base.cs` with the symbol `App/Base#Options#`, and no edge to `Other.cs`. A
  build adding `Base.Options GetSettings(Derived d) => d.Settings;` gives 0
  errors.

  arity: `Lib/Helper#` is listed as *ambiguous* between `Helper.cs` and
  `HelperOfT.cs`, and both edges are recorded. The compiler itself is clear:
  adding `Lib.Helper GetItem(Use u) => u.Item;` builds with 0 errors, so `H` is
  the non-generic type.
    - So `oracle:index` cannot be finding 2's oracle. A compile check like this
  one, kept as the fixture's hand-verified edge with how it was verified, or the
  11-19 probe's Roslyn `compiler-edges` program (which binds real symbols),
  would have to be.
    - Say which you want committed.
  - **Finding 3: the committed oracle shares the blindness.**
  `scripts/oracle/scip-edges.ts:143` reads project references with the same kind
  of regex, so the expected file says `A` references `B`.
    - `dotnet msbuild A/A.csproj -getItem:ProjectReference` says it references
  nothing.
    - So the C# oracle's project references must come from MSBuild's evaluation
  before they can grade the mend; otherwise the grader agrees with the bug.
    - That changes our oracle, not a reader, so I am raising it rather than just
  doing it.

  ## Milestone 1 of the stored-form research (handing back at about fifty
  minutes; resume me for the browser leg)

  **Commit:** `72832287e` on branch `stored-form-research`, made from
  `70a3b9481`. It adds one folder, `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/
  11/2026-11-21/Research/stored-form/`, holding:
  - the README, marked in progress;
  - `scripts/` (`facts.cjs`, `shape.mjs`, `bench-node.mjs`, `run-node.mjs`,
  `history.mjs`, and `designs/*.mjs`);
  - `results/` (`node.json`, `history.json`, `shape-dnn.json`,
  `shape-self.json`).

  No product code was changed. `slopcop:markdown` and `docs:links:enforce` both
  pass. No command was refused.

  ### The data's shape (DNN at 9bc6b06f4f15d11f6e52446505ec3117a7c84200, 10,776
  files; this repository, 1,134)
  - **Counts:** 41,618 symbols, 44,183 docstring sections, 31,071 dependency
  lines, 44,598 edges, 24,948 file-to-file wires. As compact JSON it is 39.0 MB,
  which matches the November 19 survey.
  - **Where the JSON's bytes go:** edges 25 %, symbol names/kinds/lines 17.5 %,
  docstrings 11 %, adjacency 9 %. The doc path is derivable and is 2.9 %. In
  this repository the written words are the largest part (28 %).
  - **Strings repeat heavily:** of 475,296 strings, only 109,035 are distinct
  (5.8 of 15.3 MB). Sorted paths share 77 % of their bytes with the path before.
  The kinds are tiny vocabularies (28, 7, 3, 11).
  - **The graph is sparse and lopsided:** the median file has no inbound and no
  outbound wire. The hub `Globals.cs` has 632 dependents.
  - **What the questions read:** "what depends on" reads 44 KB if inbound lists
  are stored (8.4 % of the whole if not). "Reachable" reads 1.3 %. A typical
  file's Local Map reads 5 records; the hub's reads 661 records, 32 % of the
  whole.

  ### The designs so far (DNN, Node 26, cold, median of 3)
  Every design reads back identical, field by field, and gives the same bytes
  when written twice.

  | Design | On disk | Gzipped | Write | First answer | Whole graph | Read peak
  |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | Baseline: JSON gzipped by the platform | 2.74 MB | 2.73 MB | 1.9 s | 1,913
  ms | (in that) | 300 MB |
  | **Press**: our own binary (offset header, adjacency as raw u32 arrays,
  interned strings in blocks, records in chunks of 256, each section deflated
  separately; nothing but browser built-ins) | 2.21 MB | 2.21 MB | 2.1 s | 117
  ms | 432 ms | 206 MB |
  | **Database**: SQLite through `node:sqlite` (tables for files, symbols,
  dependencies and edges, indexed on the edge's target), loaded into memory |
  29.6 MB | 3.86 MB | 1.1 s | 95 ms | 647 ms | 293 MB |
  | The same database, read in place from disk | | | | **20 ms** | 575 ms | 234
  MB |
  | **Ledger**: one fact per line, sorted, each line a JSON array starting with
  the path, gzipped (grep and diff work on it) | 2.80 MB | 2.80 MB | 1.3 s | 820
  ms | 568 ms | 372 MB |
  | **Parts**: the ledger split by directory, each part named by its SHA-256,
  many moments in one pool | 4.87 MB | 3.78 MB | 3.0 s | 83 ms | 1,571 ms | 502
  MB |

  ### History, five commits of this repository (Nov 18 to 21)
  | How the five are held | Bytes |
  | --- | ---: |
  | Five separate JSON files | 5.16 MB |
  | Five press files | 4.65 MB |
  | One ledger plus each step's added and removed lines | **1.92 MB** |
  | One pool of parts | 3.89 MB |

  All four answer "what changed between the first and the last" alike: 99 files
  born, 3 dead, 197 changed. The parts design first disagreed (78/70/127). The
  cause was a bug of mine, now fixed: a directory's files are not contiguous in
  path order (`a/b.ts` < `a/b/c.ts` < `a/c.ts`). The agreement across designs is
  what caught it.

  ### What surprised me
  - **Readable text costs almost nothing in bytes.** The ledger is 2.80 MB
  against JSON's 2.74 MB. What it costs is time to the first answer.
  - **SQLite is large on disk but fastest in place.** It is 10.8 times the press
  on disk, yet read in place it gives the fastest first answer (20 ms), because
  nothing is inflated.
  - **Shared parts are not shared bytes.** Directory parts share 76 % of their
  parts across moments but save only 25 % of the bytes: a changed directory is
  stored again whole, and each part is compressed without context from the
  others. Line deltas save 63 %.
  - **The owner's HDF5 fails two values outright.** `h5wasm` stamps object times
  and gives no switch to turn them off (the subagent measured 32 bytes
  differing between two writes). It cannot compress variable-length strings. It
  weighs 3.55 MB of WebAssembly.

  ### What was tried before: SQLite, and whether its reasons still hold
  It went on 2026-01-12 (`0d89e857`), its schema on 01-13 (`cb35a274`) and its
  last script on 01-15 (`59665f98`). The record of 01-12 gives three reasons.

  1. **It duplicated the docs.** "Two systems: generation pipeline + SQLite
  graph. Duplicated edge knowledge". It also kept a second, hardcoded idea of
  what counts as a code file. This expires if the database is the one store and
  Markdown is only an export. It would hold again if Markdown stayed a store
  beside it.
  2. **Its consumer went away.** The ripple analyzer and the persisted
  acknowledgements ended when the owner asked "could detection of link breakages
  ... be **stateless**?" This does not apply now: the facts are derived, not
  user state. Every design here is a cache, which the trial already gives up:
  the decision of 2026-09-28 that the index "is not a cache".
  3. **Native-module pain** (2025-10-19 and 10-25): `better-sqlite3` was built
  for the wrong runtime and crashed Windows tests under parallel workers.
  **Expired for Node.** `node:sqlite` is built in, needs nothing compiled, is a
  release candidate in 26, and has had `serialize`/`deserialize` since 26.1.0.
  In a page, `sql.js` ships 688 KB of WebAssembly inline.

  **Still open for SQLite:** the subagent found that `node:sqlite`'s serialize
  and `sql.js`'s export differ by 6 header bytes on the same SQLite version. So
  "same bytes" across the two hosts bends unless the file is normalized. I will
  measure this in the browser leg.

  ### Survey
  One general-purpose subagent of my tier did the survey, with sources, and has
  removed its scratch. The deciding facts:
  - **Policy:** Arrow's writer, Avro and protobuf reflection fail under the
  page's policy (they need `new Function`).
  - **DuckDB-wasm:** about 36 MB and needs workers, so it is ruled out for the
  page.
  - **Parquet:** `hyparquet` and `hyparquet-writer` are pure JS but have Snappy
  only.
  - **Browser compression:** Chromium's `CompressionStream` offers gzip and
  deflate only, with no brotli or zstd.
  - **zstd in Node:** still experimental in 26.

  ### Not yet established (the next leg, after you resume me)
  - Pages per design under the policy (`script-src` hash plus
  `'wasm-unsafe-eval'`), with the data inline as base64. In each, measure: open,
  the questions, the whole graph, and writing in the tab, at full speed and at
  4× throttle on two cores, through the browser lane.
  - Bytes carried in the HTML and the cost to get them out.
  - Whether Chromium's and Node's deflate give the same bytes for the press,
  ledger and JSON. Whether `sql.js` and `node:sqlite` agree.
  - The HDF5 design, measured rather than cited.
  - How each design composes an estate, and what becomes of today's
  `live-docs-history/3` (already interned tuples, deltas and keyframes, the same
  kind of thing as the ledger's line deltas).
  - The final table and the README rewrite.

  ### Provisional lean (not yet a recommendation; the browser numbers may
  overturn it)
  - **The press as the stored form**, inside the page and beside the scan. It is
  the smallest and needs no library. It is deterministic by construction. Its
  first answer is fast and it never parses the whole.
  - **The ledger as the one-way export.** It is grep-able and diff-able, and it
  is the history's delta form.
  - **SQLite instead, if person-queryability without our tool and in-place
  partial reads on disk rank above "nothing but the browser's own"** (which
  costs 688 KB of WebAssembly) and above size on disk (10×).
  - **What would overturn it:** Chromium's deflate writing different bytes from
  Node's, if same bytes across hosts outranks the size gain. Or the press's
  per-section inflate being slow when throttled in a tab.

  ### Scratch
  I kept `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/1501b25e-9b79-43c7-b25
  c-c8b29500c331/scratchpad/stored-form-builder/` because the next leg needs
  everything in it:
  - the DNN clone;
  - the facts and shape JSON;
  - the five moments;
  - the trial libraries (`sql.js`, `h5wasm`, `hyparquet`);
  - the encoded outputs.

  I will remove it when the work is done. The worktree has its own
  `node_modules` and build.

  The research on how a scan's facts should be stored has finished its first
  leg. It measured five designs on a real scan of DNN (10,776 files, 39 MB as
  plain JSON). Each design read back identical to the facts it was given, and
  wrote the same bytes twice.

  ┌───────────────────┬───────┬─────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │                   │ Size  │  Time to    │                              │
  │      Design       │  on   │   first     │         What it buys         │
  │                   │ disk  │   answer    │                              │
  ├───────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ JSON, gzipped     │ 2.74  │       1.9 s │ Nothing; every question      │
  │ (the baseline)    │    MB │             │ reads everything             │
  ├───────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ A compact binary  │ 2.21  │      0.12 s │ Smallest; needs nothing a    │
  │ layout of our own │    MB │             │ browser lacks                │
  ├───────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLite            │ 29.6  │ 0.02–0.10 s │ Fastest; you could query it  │
  │                   │    MB │             │ without our tool             │
  ├───────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sorted lines of   │       │             │ grep and diff work on it;    │
  │ text, one fact    │ 2.80  │      0.82 s │ five commits of history fit  │
  │ per line, gzipped │    MB │             │ in 1.92 MB against 5.16 MB   │
  │                   │       │             │ as JSON                      │
  ├───────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ The same lines,   │ 4.87  │             │ Less than hoped; sharing     │
  │ cut into shared   │    MB │      0.08 s │ parts saved 25% where line   │
  │ parts             │       │             │ differences saved 63%        │
  └───────────────────┴───────┴─────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  - HDF5 fails two of your values by measurement. It stamps the time into every
    file, so two writes of the same facts differ by 32 bytes, and it cannot
    compress text of varying length.
  - SQLite is a real candidate again. It was dropped in January mainly because
    its native module broke on Windows. Node now ships SQLite built in, so that
    reason has expired.
  - Nothing is chosen. These numbers are from Node only. The next leg measures
    each design inside a browser tab, slowed to resemble your VM, at five times
    DNN's size, and checks whether each can be written as a scan proceeds and
    survive a closed tab.

  The readers

  - I reversed one of my own rulings. I had told the builder to reset the
    history's memory estimate whenever the code changes. It showed that every
    upgrade would then start from a default guess, which on an 8 GB machine
    risks running out of memory. The estimate now carries across code changes.
  - Two of our C# graders shared the reader's blind spots. One reads project
    references by the same text pattern the reader uses, so it agreed with the
    reader's mistake on a commented-out reference. The other cannot tell Helper
    from Helper<T>. Both will now take their answers from Microsoft's build
    system and the C# compiler directly.
  - The nine mends are split across three builders (C# and ASP.NET, PowerShell,
    SQL), since they touch different files.
  - A gap I found in our own tests is being closed. The test that compares
    readers against their graders on sample code only checks that the counts add
    up, so a reader getting worse could never fail it. Three connections we
    verified by hand on the estate sample are missing today, and nothing reports
    it.

  Started: choosing what a scan leaves out

  This is your ask from earlier ("a good solid way to create ignore patterns on
  what they scan, before they scan it"). A builder is writing the single
  function, shared by the page and the command line, that decides what is left
  out from a folder's listing before any file is read.

  - For .gitignore, git is the judge. The function must match git's own answer,
    rule for rule, on generated cases and on real repositories.
  - For .tfignore, Microsoft's documentation leaves three rules unstated. The
    builder is checking whether Microsoft's open-source TFS client answers them
    in its code. Whatever cannot be established stays marked unknown.
  - It will also produce the listing you would see on a folder built to your
    shape (over 100k files, mostly packages), with counts by folder and by rule.
    I will draw the choice screen from that.

  directions:

  - Reading Size (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Prob

  ## 1. The question now costs a 2D visit nothing
  - **When it is asked.** At the start only when 3D is asked for then (by the
  address, or by a remembered 3D where the address does not say). Also at a
  press of 3D, and on Back or Forward to 3D. Otherwise once the page is idle
  after its first picture: `whenIdle` is `requestIdleCallback` with a 3 s limit,
  or a timer where the browser has none.
  - **One place holds the answer**: `webgl-support.ts` (`webglOffered`, plus
  `webglKnown`, which asks nothing).
  - **A late "no"** makes the 3D control `aria-disabled` and calls
  `withholdThreeDLayout` (now exported from `tuning.ts`; the config field is
  gone). It also resyncs the toolbar and schedules the remembered-navigation
  write.
  - **The remembered choice.** `perspectiveRemembered` leaves a remembered 3D
  alone until the browser has been asked. One consequence: if a page closes
  before its idle question, a remembered 3D is not yet replaced by the 2D that
  was shown.
  - **`perspectiveShown`** now takes the question as a function and asks it only
  for a 3D request. Unit cases cover this.
  - **New spec case**: on a 2D arrival in the no-WebGL browser, the 3D control
  and Tuning's 3D Layout become unavailable with no press, within 5 s.

  **Time to first picture**, estate sample in 2D, a fresh browser each run, the
  two builds served side by side and taken in turn, 9 runs each. "Before" is
  this branch plus one line asking at the start.

  | Drawing | Before, median (range) | After, median (range) |
  | --- | ---: | ---: |
  | Graphics card | 1,023 ms (860–1,278) | 935 ms (860–1,446) |
  | Software | 907 ms (789–1,143) | 922 ms (787–1,207) |

  On the card the first picture comes about 90 ms sooner. In software there is
  no difference above the noise. The 731 ms I measured on a blank page did not
  show in full, because the Explorer's own page has already started the graphics
  process by the time it asks. An earlier pair of runs taken one build after
  the other, under loads of 6.6 and then 16.5, gave 1,112 against 1,447 ms. That
  gap was the machine's load, so I discarded it. The numbers and the method are
  in the pictures' README under "What the question costs a 2D visit", and the
  design rule's new "why it is asked late" line links there.

  ordinary case again and passes. The gap is gone from the design rule's status,

  ## 3. `tuning-reset.spec.ts` line 65
  **The cause:** the case measured something nobody can see.
  - It reads the dot on Stub Min, which stands in the "2D Wires" group, folded
  by default.
  - The case's own failure picture shows that group folded, and the dots drawn
  on every changed heading and on Column Gap.
  - Inside a folded group nothing is drawn, so the dot's computed width is
  whatever the browser last laid out. On the same Chromium 149 and the same
  code, it read 5px in my standalone probe and 0px three times in three in the
  case.
  - The suite's recorded runs of 2026-11-20 show it passing then.
  - Neither the dot's CSS rule nor the test had changed since 2026-11-19.
  - Opened, the dot is drawn: I looked at a 4x enlargement of the lever, a dot
  right after "Stub Min".

  **The fix:** the case opens the 2D Wires group, as a person would, waits for
  the lever to be visible, then reads the dot. What it asks of the dot is
  unchanged. Red before: 0 of 3 runs passed. Green after: 3 cases × 3 runs, 9
  passed.

  ## Handback: pool record and twin redone, finding 3 done with MSBuild as its
