  ### 1. `db2b3b4e1` The twin is now listed
  - Every run now says "kept `<folder>`, the same code's cache under the other
  way of running workers: `<size>`", and never lists it as superseded.
  - The integration case for twins checks that line for each way.
  - The `--help` text, the CLI reference and `change-over-time` now say the twin
  is listed. The help text was already in `5c80a3c41`; this commit only changes
  its words.

  ### 2. `03327f666` The pool record is one per way of running workers, under
  the old rule (your partial reversal)
  - **Layout:** one `pool.json` beside the caches, `{"one change a worker": …,
  "workers kept": …}`. Each entry is kept by `nextPoolRecord`, restored with its
  unit case exactly as at `a0da03f59`.
  - **Removed:** `adoptSharedPoolRecord` is gone. A file in the old shape counts
  as no record and is written over at the next measurement. The function that
  reads the file is still `recordedPool`, so the machine-failure boundary test's
  allowance still matches it.
  - **Integration case, covering three things:**
    - The two ways alternate with distinct records (111 MiB and 3 GiB); each run
  is sized by its own way's peak and adds to its own count only.
    - Within one way, another lens's record of 4 GiB over 900 changes sizes the
  run, and a one-change run does not lower it.
    - A file in the old shape counts as no record.
  - **Shown red:** the case fails with the carry within a way taken out.
  - **Notes:** dated notes in `change-over-time` and in the decisions log
  (beside "the pool is sized by…") say the cross-lens rule was dropped for one
  commit and restored per way, and why: the 8 GB machine, and oversubscription
  at every upgrade.

  ### 3. `b1347588e` Grader point 1: the C# compiler oracle takes references
  from MSBuild
  - `scip-edges.ts` now asks `dotnet msbuild -getItem:ProjectReference` for each
  project.
  - A project MSBuild cannot evaluate stops the oracle with its name and
  MSBuild's reason; there is no fallback to a pattern.
  - **Unit case:** a commented-out reference counts as none, and an unevaluable
  project is named.
  - **Expected files:** none of the committed C# fixtures moved. For `basic`,
  `estate`, `rosetta` and `webforms`, MSBuild gives the same references the
  pattern did, so their edges cannot have moved either. I compared their
  references rather than regenerating them, because the installed scip-dotnet is
  0.2.16 and the files were made with 0.2.14.
  - `6528e216c` gives these three MSBuild cases a two-minute timeout. They start
  MSBuild once per project, and inside the gate they ran past 5 s and failed on
  time alone. (Risk: if CI has no `dotnet`, these unit cases fail there.
  GitHub's Ubuntu images normally carry the .NET SDK, but I have not checked
  CI.)

  **What this Linux's MSBuild can evaluate** (`dotnet msbuild -getItem` on every
  project file):

  | | Evaluates | Stops on |
  |---|---|---|
  | DNN | **50 of 84** | All 34 fail with MSB4019, a missing import: 17 Visual
  Studio's `Microsoft.WebApplication.targets`, which the .NET SDK on Linux does
  not ship; 14 DNN's own `Build/BuildScripts/Package.Targets`, which is
  `Package.targets` on disk; 1 `Microsoft.CSharp.Targets`, which is `.targets`
  in the SDK; the last 2 truncated past classifying. |
  | mojoPortal | **21 of 25** | All 4 MSB4019 on
  `Microsoft.WebApplication.targets`. |

  So on this Linux, the owner's kind of old-style web projects are exactly the
  ones MSBuild cannot evaluate, plus imports Windows finds only because it
  ignores case.

  ### 4. `6344a0692` Finding 3: no element an XML comment holds is read
  - **The shared step:** new `dotnetXml.ts`, `withoutXmlComments`. It writes
  each comment over in spaces and keeps line breaks, so positions and lines stay
  put. A CDATA section is passed over whole, and a comment left open runs to
  the end of the file.
  - **Where it applies, each with a case shown red first:**
    - `project.ts`: every pattern, plus `projectKind`, which `aspnet.ts` uses to
  find web projects.
    - `dotnetProjects.ts`: the C# reader's reach. The reviewer's whole case now
  draws nothing.
    - `solution.ts`: the `.slnx` form.
    - `dotnetConfig.ts`: `Web.config`, `App.config`, `packages.config`.
  - **Corpus count:**
    - Project files hold no reference inside a comment, in either corpus.
    - On the 71 projects MSBuild evaluates, our patterns read the same project
  references as MSBuild, except three of mojoPortal's (below).
    - Elements inside configuration comments: 6 in DNN's `App.config` files, 164
  in mojoPortal's `Web.config` files.
  - **Generated before and after** with the commit before the mend, in a
  separate checkout: **no edge gained or lost** on either corpus (DNN 31,798,
  mojoPortal 9,749).
    - Settings no longer read from comments: DNN 2 (`UsePortNumber` in
  `development.config` and `release.config`).
    - mojoPortal 18 in 7 files: in `Web/Web.config`, the commented sample
  `MSSQLConnectionString` (as a connection string and as a setting),
  `LoginLink`, `RegistrationLink`, `Log404HandlerExceptions` and
  `Site1-SSLIsAvailable`; plus `MyDB` in six transform files.
  - `3625bbfb6` corrects my own note in `dotnetXml.ts`'s doc, which had
  overstated the 164 as settings read.

  ### A fork the corpus raised (not built): conditional references
  Three mojoPortal projects reference their data projects under
  `Condition="'$(Configuration)'=='Debug - MySql'"` and similar, one condition
  per database. Two examples:
  - `mojoPortal.Business.csproj` lines 315–346 hold eight conditional
  ProjectReferences.
  - `SuperFlexiBusiness.csproj` and `mojoPortal.Features.Business.csproj` do the
  same.

  MSBuild, evaluating the default configuration (Debug), sees only MSSQL. Our
  reader reads every condition's reference as real: on these three projects that
  is MySql, SQLite and pgSQL, and on two of them both pgSQL spellings. That
  widens the C# reach to every provider's namesakes.

  Which is right depends on which configuration a person builds. The candidates
  are the default configuration (MSBuild's answer), the union (ours), or a
  configuration named in `.live-docs.config.json`. It changes what the product
  reads, so I stopped there.

  ## Stored-form research, milestone 2: the browser leg and your three additions

  The commits are on branch `stored-form-research`, made on top of milestone 1's
  `72832287e`:
  - `f6d95400b`
  - `9c8fd4d14`
  - `2569580b6`
  - `a168ec29f`
  - `890679d95`

  They change one folder only, `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2
  026-11-21/Research/stored-form/`. No product code, Memory or work items were
  touched. `slopcop:markdown` and `docs:links:enforce` are clean.

  The README is rewritten around a table you can read in two minutes. For each
  form it gives: how long it takes to open on a weak machine, the size of the
  saved page, whether you can `grep` it, and what survives a closed tab. The
  internals follow after that.

  ### The forms, with headline numbers
  All numbers are DNN (10,776 files) in a Chromium 149 tab slowed four times on
  two cores, unless marked. "5×" is the synthetic corpus of DNN repeated five
  times.

  | Form | First answer, then whole map | Saved page | Write as facts arrive |
  At 5× (first answer, read memory) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | **JSON gzipped** (the baseline) | 3.2 s | 3.65 MB | cannot (all at once: 625
  MB) | 25.8 s, 1.3 GB |
  | **Press** (our binary, written as facts arrive) | **0.15 s**, then 2.2 s |
  **2.83 MB**, reader 12 KB | 8.7 s, 243 MB | **1.0 s, 351 MB**, page 9.1 MB;
  writes 305 MB |
  | **Database** (sql.js in the page, node:sqlite in Node) | 1.6 s, then 5.0 s |
  6.19 MB (of which 0.99 MB is the engine) | 17.2 s, 317 MB | 7.2 s, 819 MB;
  writes **854 MB / 132 s** |
  | **Ledger** (sorted fact lines, one gzip) | 1.8 s, then 2.4 s | 3.74 MB |
  15.4 s, 227 MB | 12.2 s, **1.3 GB** |
  | **Indexed ledger** (built this leg) | **78 ms**, then 15.7 s as its reader
  is built | 3.98 MB | 26.8 s, 243 MB | 0.6 s, **1.7 GB**, hub's Local Map 125 s
  |
  | **Parts** (directory chunks named by hash) | 0.17 s, then 27 s | 6.50 MB |
  47 s, 196 MB | 0.9 s; whole map 166 s, 844 MB |
  | **HDF5** (h5wasm) | 0.8 s, then 2.6 s, but **6.7 s before the page is even
  ready** | 8.82 MB (5.6 MB of it library) | cannot as built (all at once: 20 s)
  | 1.5 s, 395 MB |

  The indexed ledger is the ledger written as gzip pieces of 256 files each.
  Each piece's header (gzip's extra field) records its length and the first path
  it holds, and the dependents of each file follow as lines. `gzip -t` passes
  it, and `zcat` or `grep` read it as one text.

  - **Node at 5×:** the database read in place on disk gives the first answer in
  162 ms; written as facts arrive on disk it needs only 92 MB.
  - **History, five commits:** keeping the ledger's line deltas takes 1.92 MB,
  against 5.16 MB for five separate JSON files.
  - **Correctness:** every form reads back identical to the facts, field by
  field, on every corpus.

  ### Your additions
  1. **Scale.** The five-times corpus is DNN repeated under five prefixes, each
  copy wired only inside itself; the README says so, and says it flatters the
  forms that intern strings. Two forms are out by your test: JSON and the plain
  ledger, each about 1.3 GB to read in a tab at 5×. Two more blow up in a tab at
  5×:
     - the database written in a page (854 MB);
     - the indexed ledger's reader as built (1.7 GB).

     Only the press, and HDF5, which holds the press's own sections, stay in
  proportion.
  2. **Facts arriving one file at a time.** Measured both ways in Node and in
  the tab:
     - **Press:** streams, holding only distinct strings, paths and wires as
  numbers. Its first version needed two passes (it numbered strings by
  frequency). The one-pass version is 4.5 % *smaller*.
     - **Database:** streams. Files get ids at first mention, so an edge can
  name a file not yet written.
     - **Ledger and parts:** stream, and give the same bytes as writing all at
  once (checked by hash).
     - **JSON:** cannot stream, because each file's dependents sit inside its
  record.
     - **HDF5:** cannot stream as built.

     This research does not contradict the proposal's sink, but adds two
  conditions the proposal does not state:
     - three forms need facts handed over in path order;
     - a file's dependents (and its Cited By) are known only at the end, so the
  sink must build them after the last file.
  3. **A stopped scan**, ranked in the README below the owner's values as you
  asked. Stopped at 60 % of DNN:
     - **Database on disk:** keeps 6,144 of 6,465 files, every committed batch
  of 512.
     - **Ledger:** Node can read the cut gzip back, giving 6,376 files. A page
  cannot, because `DecompressionStream` refuses a cut stream, and Chromium and
  Node both refuse two gzip pieces in a row.
     - **Indexed ledger:** keeps 6,400 files in 25 whole pieces, valid gzip,
  readable in a page.
     - **Press:** its prefix is unreadable without per-section labels (an
  argument; not built).
     - **JSON:** nothing.
     - **In a tab:** everything is lost unless the page writes it down as it
  goes. IndexedDB opens from a `file://` page (measured).

  ### The question: what "the same bytes" is for
  - **The record:** the value is the root's own, from 2026-11-20: "The same
  facts give the same bytes, so runs can be compared". The owner made it a value
  to trade, and the root's note that night added "bending 'the same bytes'
  admits containers that are compared by content".
  - **How it is used today:** comparing two derivations by `cmp`; caches keyed
  by the lens; texts named by their hash. None of these needs a file identical
  across hosts. So naming the content meets it, and the README says so.
  - **The evidence, Chromium against Node:**
    - JSON, both presses, the ledger, the indexed ledger and the parts are
  byte-identical between the two, at DNN, and at 5× for the press and the
  ledger. Both bundle Chromium's zlib fork (`1.3.2.1-motley`). That is a fact of
  today's builds, not a promise.
    - The hash of the canonical facts is the same for every form, `884045c3…`.
    - SQLite pages are identical, but 6 header bytes differ between node:sqlite
  and sql.js.
    - HDF5 writes different bytes on every write.

  ### What surprised me
  - **Many small pieces are slow in a page.** Starting the browser's own
  decompressor costs a slowed tab dearly, so the parts take 27 s to load the
  whole map at DNN and 166 s at 5×.
  - **HDF5's page needs 6.7 s slowed just to become ready**, because of its 5.6
  MB script.
  - **The streaming press is smaller than the one that needed the whole graph
  first.**
  - **The indexed ledger's first answer beats every other form (78 ms).** But
  anything touching many files is slow with its simplest reader. A reader that
  indexes each piece's paths once might change that; it was not built.

  ### Recommendation (provisional, apart from the findings in the README)
  - **Stored form:** the press written as facts arrive, in both hosts. Add two
  small things, neither built: section labels so a stopped scan can resume, and
  a content hash in the footer.
  - **Export:** the indexed ledger, one way. It can be grepped and diffed, and
  line deltas are the history's natural form.
  - **One store only.** Keeping two would be the duplication SQLite was removed
  for.

  What would overturn it:
  - the owner ranking "usable without our tool" above whole-map load time and
  memory, which makes the indexed ledger the store;
  - the Explorer moving to reading one neighbourhood at a time;
  - only the command line mattering, which makes SQLite the store;
  - the engine being unable to hand facts over in path order.

  ### Not established
  - Parquet, measured (surveyed only).
  - HDF5 written as facts arrive.
  - Progressive persistence into IndexedDB or a picked folder, and reading it
  back.
  - In a tab at 5×: writing all at once, the parts streaming, and the indexed
  ledger streaming. These were cut by the lane's 5-minute hold, or given up to
  make room. Node's figures stand in.
  - The owner's machine, and Windows.
  - A real 50k-file corpus.

  Several 5× tab runs were taken at loads of 37 to 61, with other agents on the
  machine; the README marks each one.

  ### Commands refused
  One. A `python3 -I` heredoc that edited `scripts/page/build-pages.mjs` was
  refused as "too complex to verify that it stays inside the worktree". It named
  no git. I then made the same three edits with the Edit tool, as the brief
  directs for edits. I am reporting it in case you read that as reaching a
  refused command another way.

  ### Scratch

  `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/1501b25e-9b79-43c7-b25c-c8b29
  500c331/scratchpad/stored-form-builder/`. It holds the DNN clone, the facts
  and one-line-per-file inputs, the stored outputs, the pages, the trial

  facts JSON is kept) and the disposable outputs. Nothing of mine is still
  running.

● The format research's browser measurements are in, and they set up one choice
  that is yours: how much it is worth for the stored facts to be readable
  without our tool. Nothing is built on it until the readers land, so there is
  no hurry.

  What was measured

  Seven formats held the same scan and each read back identical. The numbers
  below are from a Chromium tab slowed four times on two cores, as a stand-in
  for your VM (it ranks the formats; it does not predict your machine). "10k" is
  DNN's 10,776 files. "50k" is DNN repeated five times, which is not a real
  codebase and flatters formats that store each string once.

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬───────────────┬─────────┬─────────────┐
  │              │ Opens at 10k:   │ Opens at 50k: │ Saved   │  Readable   │
  │    Format    │ first answer,   │     first     │ page at │ without our │
  │              │    whole map    │   answer,     │   10k   │     tool    │
  │              │                 │    memory     │         │             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┼─────────────┤
  │ JSON,        │                 │ 25.8 s, 1.3   │         │             │
  │ gzipped      │ 3.2 s (both)    │ GB            │ 3.65 MB │ Yes         │
  │ (today)      │                 │               │         │             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Compact      │                 │               │         │             │
  │ binary of    │ 0.15 s, 2.2 s   │ 1.0 s, 351 MB │ 2.83 MB │ No          │
  │ our own      │                 │               │         │             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Indexed      │                 │ 0.6 s, then   │         │             │
  │ text, one    │ 0.08 s, 15.7 s  │ 1.7 GB for    │ 3.98 MB │ Yes: zcat,  │
  │ fact per     │                 │ the whole map │         │ grep, diff  │
  │ line         │                 │               │         │             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┼─────────────┤
  │ SQLite       │ 1.6 s, 5.0 s    │ 7.2 s, 819 MB │ 6.19 MB │ Yes: any    │
  │              │                 │               │         │ SQLite tool │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────────┼─────────┼─────────────┤
  │              │ 0.8 s, 2.6 s,   │               │         │             │
  │ HDF5         │ after 6.7 s to  │ 1.5 s, 395 MB │ 8.82 MB │ Yes: HDF5   │
  │              │ load its        │               │         │ tools       │
  │              │ library         │               │         │             │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────┴───────────────┴─────────┴─────────────┘

  - JSON is out. At 50k files it needs 26 seconds and 1.3 GB before it can
    answer anything.
  - HDF5 is out. It writes different bytes every time for the same facts, and
    its library adds 5.6 MB to every saved page.
  - SQLite is the best on the command line and the worst in a page. Read from
    disk by Node it gives the fastest first answer of any format, in the least
    memory. Written inside a page at 50k it takes 854 MB and over two minutes.
    Since you want both surfaces from one design, I count it out.

  The two that remain

  - Compact binary. A table of contents, the connections as plain arrays of
    numbers, each string stored once, each section compressed separately. It is
    the only format whose numbers stay in proportion at 50k, and it has the
    smallest saved page. The cost is that nobody can read it without our reader.
  - Indexed text. One fact per line, sorted by path, in compressed chunks with a
    small index. For example, one line lists the 632 files that depend on
    Globals.cs, so grep answers "what depends on this". It answers a first
    question as fast as the binary. Loading the whole map is slow and
    memory-hungry, but that was measured with the simplest possible reader, so
    it is not yet a fair comparison.

  Your decision

  1. Binary as the stored form, text exported on demand (my lean). Fastest and
     lightest on a weak machine. You run one command or press one button when
     you want something to grep.
  2. Indexed text as the stored form. Always readable with ordinary tools, about
     40% larger, slower to load a whole map. How much slower is what the next
     leg measures.

  I lean to the first because your stated priority is running "on absolute
  garbage", and you already expected Markdown to be an export. Which matters
  more to you: the speed and memory, or never needing our tool to read the
  facts?

  Still being measured

  - A closed tab. Today a scan running in a tab loses everything if the tab
    closes, in every format. The next leg builds a page that saves as it goes
    and resumes.
  - A fair reader for the indexed text, so the two finalists are compared at
    their best.
  - The page saving a copy of itself with the facts inside, at 50k files.

  The full account is the research README (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistor
  y/2026/11/2026-11-21/Research/stored-form/README.md); its first table is the
  two-minute version.

  ## Part one

  **What the documentation says.** Each sentence is quoted with its page in the
  head of `scripts/oracle/sql-compare.ts`:
  - **A one-part name in a batch.** "Ownership and user-schema separation":
  "When database objects are referenced by using a one-part name, SQL Server
  first looks in the user's default schema. If the object is not found there,
  SQL Server looks next in the dbo schema. If the object is not in the dbo
  schema, an error is returned."
  - **A one-part name in a module.** "Execute a stored procedure": "If the
  nonqualified procedure name appears inside the body of another procedure
  definition, the schema that contains this other procedure is searched next"
  (after sys, before dbo).
  - **Every object a module names.** Microsoft's SQL programmability team blog,
  "Name resolution, default schema, implicit schema Part I" (2006, archived on
  Learn): "Active default schema is schema of the SQL module if entity
  referenced by a statement (except DDL, dynamic SQL, a.k.a. EXECUTE statements,
  or intrinsic such as object_id()) inside a procedure, function or view.
  Otherwise, active default schema is current user's default schema."
  - **`database..name`.** Two sources:
    - The syntax conventions page says "Server and schema name are omitted".
    - Part III of the same blog: "For object references outside SQL modules, or
  DDL, dynamic SQL inside SQL modules, current user's default schema in the
  specified database is used. Otherwise, the module schema owner's default
  schema in the specified database is used."
    - SQL Server 2008 Books Online's DBZ..TableY example agrees.
  - **The default schema itself.** "By default, users created with the CREATE
  USER Transact-SQL command have dbo as their default schema."
  - **A trigger's schema.** CREATE TRIGGER: "DML triggers are scoped to the
  schema of the table or view on which they're created."
  - **Which statements are DDL.** The "Transact-SQL statements" page lists
  TRUNCATE TABLE under DDL.

  **Which source gives what.**
  - ScriptDom gives the parse. It gives a name's parts, and which are written:
  `SisterDb..Orders` is database SisterDb, no schema, object Orders. The emitter
  now also records which procedure, function, view or trigger holds each
  reference, and a trigger's table.
  - The documentation gives the binding order.
  - Neither gives the caller's default schema. The grader and the reader both
  take dbo, the documented default.
  - So a one-part name in a loose script binds to dbo alone. Any other schema
  would be a guess at a fact of the server.

  **Where the documentation corrects your ruling.**
  - **Inside a module, the caller's default schema is never searched.** A
  one-part name that neither the module's schema nor dbo holds binds to nothing,
  even when exactly one other schema holds it. `notes.sql` in the fixture holds
  that case.
  - **`database..name` inside a module uses a user's default schema in that
  database, never the module's schema.**
  - The three-part form keeps its old class: a source edge to the folder's same
  schema and name, with the database ignored. `Db..Name` now gets the same
  class.

  **The fixture.** `tests/integration/fixtures/sql-schemas`:
  - `dbo.Orders` and `archive.Orders`, plus `archive.Notes`.
  - A one-part `Orders` named from a dbo module, an archive module, an archive
  module's TRUNCATE (DDL), and an archive module's EXEC string.
  - A schema-less trigger on `archive.Orders`, and a loose script.
  - `SisterDb..Orders` and `SisterDb.dbo.Orders`.
  - Its ScriptDom record is kept, and its register in `sql-oracle.test.ts` holds
  one entry: a trigger, which the reader gives no symbol.

  **Red, then green.**

  | Commit | Fixture register | Adapter tests in `sql.test.ts` |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 1 | 9 lines invented, 1 kept name missing | both red |
  | 2 | only the two `SisterDb..Orders` contract lines left | the second green |
  | 3 | green | both green |

  I also added new `openings.test.ts` cases for `moduleSchema`, `bindSqlName`
  and `database..name`. I did not run them against the older reader; with the
  old code `moduleSchema` is always absent and `bindSqlName` doesn't exist.

  **Lines changed on the corpora.** I dumped the adapter's lines file by file
  and compared the dumps.

  | Corpus | Lines before | Changed by (a) | Changed by (b) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | tSQLt | 969 | 0 | 0 |
  | DNN `.sql` | 29 | 0 | 0 |
  | DNN provider scripts | 6,964 | 0 | 0 |

  - The new grader gives 969/969, 29/29 and 6,961/6,962 at every commit. No
  committed score moved, so no expectation was touched.
  - Their only `db..name` names point at tempdb, master and msdb, and stay kept
  as written.
  - Also unchanged after both mends:
    - **dbatools `.sql`:** 26/26 objects, 199/199 lines.

  512/365/147 files, and 658 expected, 337 right, 321 missing, 370 invented —

  **Forks I decided.**
  - **Where the binding lives.** It is `bindSqlName`, in `openings.ts` next to
  `matchSqlObject`, and only the SQL adapter calls it. I edited only the SQL
  part of `openings.ts`; it wasn't on either of your file lists, but the fault
  sits in its `sqlObjectName`.

  It is a sibling's file; adopting the fix there is one call.

  changed no C# or estate doc.
  - **DDL inside a module** binds by the caller's schema: TRUNCATE TABLE, ALTER
  TABLE, REFERENCES and ALTER.
  - **A schema-less trigger** takes its table's schema.
  - **An object created without a schema** is presumed to be dbo's for a
  one-part use. A written `dbo.X` still doesn't bind to it, as before on both
  sides. I didn't count how many such lines exist; the provider scripts have 265
  schema-less creates.

  `PaymentsContext.cs`, whose edge is drawn.

  - **`oracle.test.ts`** now fails on a missed edge the ledger doesn't list, on

    The rule now:
    - A `Module\Command` name whose module is shipped is PowerShell's own
  command, and no load.
    - Otherwise it is a script path only where the workspace holds
  `Module/Command` or `Module/Command.ps1`.
    - Otherwise it is neither a load nor a notice.

    Red: 3 notices. Green: none.

    **Open for you:** a call qualified by a workspace module's name is left
  unwired. pwsh says it reaches only that module's function, and the corpora
  have no such call to measure a rule on. Also, pwsh on Linux reads `lib\X` as a
  module name because `\` separates no folders there. The Windows reading (a
  path) is by analogy with `/`; no Windows is here to show which wins where both
  readings exist.
  - **A (module-less functions and aliases).**
    - pwsh: in `run.ps1`, which loads nothing, `Get-Command Pause` is
  PowerShell's own (no source file). After dot-sourcing the helper, it is the
  helper's.
    - **pwsh corrected ruling (b).** With `lib.ps1` defining `select` and
  `Get-ChildItem` and dot-sourced: `'x' | select` printed `x`, so the alias beat
  the loaded function, as ruled. But `gci` ran the loaded workspace
  `Get-ChildItem`. So the rule follows pwsh: an alias call is a call to the
  command the alias names. It never reaches a function of the alias's own name,
  and reaches a function of the named command's name only when the caller loads
  it.
    - Lists in `powershell.builtins.ts`, union of both editions:

  - **The function:** `packages/engine/src/tooling/leaveOut.ts`, beside
  `gitIgnored.ts`. Its call is `leaveOut({ ignoreFiles, startingList, person
  })`, which returns two questions: `file(path)` and `folder(path)`.
    - The answer is `{ kept, rule }`, where `rule` is a file and line, an entry
  of the starting list, or one of the person's patterns.
    - `folder(path)` also answers `{ prunable, why }`: whether a walk may leave
  that folder unlisted.
    - It imports nothing. Nothing calls it yet, so the page bundle does not
  include it and `node-needs` shows nothing new.
  - **Which rule wins:**
    1. the person's patterns;
    2. the ignore files (a path is left out if either kind leaves it out);
    3. the starting list, which only applies where no ignore file stands at or
  above a path's folder. A folder the starting list leaves out decides for
  everything beneath it, so pruning such a folder is safe.
  - **Tests:**
    - `leaveOut.oracle.test.ts` runs git itself.
    - `leaveOut.tfvc.test.ts` checks against
  `__fixtures__/tfignore-oracle.json`, which holds TFVC's own verdicts.
  - **The record:**
  `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/leave-out/`.
  It holds the README, `git-agreement.ts`, `shown-before-a-scan.ts`,
  `record-tfvc-oracle.mjs`, the TFVC oracle's driver and build scripts
  (`tfvc-oracle/`), `starting-lists/` and `listings/`.
  - **`internal-tooling.mdmd.md`:** one paragraph on the two oracle tests, under
  the Oracle section.
  - **Subagent used:** `a28570ec15ae6db1a`, general-purpose on Opus, for reading
  Team Explorer Everywhere and the NuGet page, and for building the TFVC
  oracle.

  ### Agreement with git, for `.gitignore`
  - **Committed test:** 16 named cases plus 60 random trees from a fixed seed.
  Each tree is made in a fresh repository, `git check-ignore -v -n -z
  --no-index` is asked of every path, and the function must give the same
  verdict and the same deciding file and line. All agree.
    - Shown red: letting `?` match `/` in an anchored pattern made `s/t/u`
  ignored where git keeps it.
    - The random trees found nothing the named cases had not.
  - **Real folders:**

  | Folder | Paths | `.gitignore` files | Agree | Disagree | Tracked by git but
  left out by the rules |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | This repository (my worktree, with `node_modules`, `dist`, `test-results`) |
  22,229 | 1 | 22,229 | 0 | 11 |
  | DNN (read only) | 26,223 | 26 | 26,223 | 0 | 26 |
  | MediatR (shallow anonymous clone, 224-line `.gitignore`, 81 build outputs I
  placed where a .NET build puts them) | 479 | 1 | 479 | 0 | 0 |

  - **What a listing cannot know:**
    - `.git/info/exclude` and the global excludes file. On these three folders
  neither decided anything.
    - A file git tracks beneath a rule that would leave it out: 11 here (e.g.
  `.vscode/settings.json`) and 26 in DNN (e.g. `Build/Tools/NuGet/*.nuspec`).
  The Node host can still ask git about these; a page cannot.
  - **Not used:** the main checkout itself. My worktree stood in for it, to stay
  inside my own checkout.

  ### What `.tfignore`'s rules rest on
  **The source.** Team Explorer Everywhere is Microsoft's cross-platform TFVC
  client, MIT licensed, commit `21e0e598`. It is a Java port of Visual Studio's
  client, whose source is not public, so everything below is TEE's behaviour.
  The matching code is `IgnoreFile.java`, `IgnoreEntry.java`,
  `LocalItemExclusionEvaluator.java` and `ItemPath.matchesWildcardFile`. The
  subagent compiled Microsoft's classes unchanged, with four compile-only
  stand-ins for Eclipse types and a small driver, into an executable oracle.

  **The three questions Microsoft's page leaves open:**
  - **A subfolder's file adds to its parent's rules, it does not replace them.**
  The deepest file with an opinion decides, and a file with none passes the
  question up (`LocalItemExclusionEvaluator.java:279-303`). A folder's own
  `.tfignore` governs its contents, not the folder itself.
  - **Case:**
    - the file-name part is compared without case on every platform
  (`IgnoreFile.java:274-293`, `ItemPath:283`);
    - a pattern's folder part (`Docs\` in `Docs\*.md`) goes through
  `java.io.File.equals`, which ignores case on Windows and respects it on Linux;
    - I compare the folder part without case, as on Windows. That is the
  function's one stated difference from the oracle, which ran on Linux.
  - **Order within one file: the last matching line wins**
  (`IgnoreFile.java:221-316`).

  **Pruning, the direct answer to your question:**
  - TFVC's change scanner, which fills Pending Changes, enters every folder and
  judges every file (`LocalWorkspaceScanner.java:166-168`). So `out` followed by
  `!out\keep.dll` keeps `out/keep.dll`.
  - `tf add /recursive` skips an excluded folder without looking inside
  (`FileSystemWalker.java:276-279`).
  - So a folder a `.tfignore` excludes is reported as not prunable, with that
  reason. A folder git ignores is reported prunable.
  - A surprise: `!bin` re-includes the folder `bin` but not the files inside it.

  **Your memory of the NuGet page did not hold.** The page is https://learn.micr
  osoft.com/en-us/nuget/consume-packages/packages-and-source-control, section
  "Omitting packages with Team Foundation Version Control". Its example is
  `*.nupkg`, `packages`, `project.lock.json`, `project.assets.json`,
  `*.nuget.props`. There is no `!packages/*.targets` line. Its comment says "in
  the root of the repository", but `packages` without a leading `\` is
  recursive: it leaves out a `packages` folder at any depth.

  **What the function does not take from TFVC:**
  - the global exclusion list a collection's server sends. TEE falls back to 56
  entries when it has none (`ExclusionSet.java:47`: `bin`, `obj`, `Debug`,
  `Release`, `TestResults`, `*.dll`, `*.pdb`, `*.user` and so on, but not
  `packages`). That makes it a candidate starting list;
  - TFVC's skipping of its own `$tf` / `.tf` folders.

  **The committed test:** 52 trees (12 probing one question each, 40 random),
  468 verdicts recorded from TEE's matcher with no global list. All agree, apart
  from the one stated case difference.
  - Shown red: letting an inclusion match any part of a path, rather than only
  its last part, made 3 trees differ.

  **Still inferred, not proven:**
  - Visual Studio's own handling of case and of encoding (a file without a
  byte-order mark is read in the ANSI code page);
  - two edge lines that name nothing, which are skipped.

  ### What a person would be shown: DNN
  The full listings, by folder with counts and the deciding rule, are in
  `listings/dnn-*.txt`. DNN has 21,717 files; Node lists it in about 0.2 s.

  | How DNN is read | Kept | Left out | Time to decide (not tuned) | Source
  files the starting list wrongly leaves out |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | With its own 26 `.gitignore` files | 21,686 | 31 | 3.9 s | — |
  | As a TFS folder with no ignore file, fixed list (`bin/`, `obj/`,
  `packages/`, `node_modules/`, `.vs/`, `TestResults/`) | 21,717 | 0 | 2.0 s | 0
  |
  | The same, with GitHub's `VisualStudio.gitignore` (426 lines, licensed CC0) |
  21,574 | 143 | 9.5 s | **60** |
  | The same, nothing left out | 21,717 | 0 | 1.4 s | — |

  - **The standard Visual Studio list leaves out real code.** Its `[Ll]og/` and
  `**/[Pp]ackages/*` lines catch `Services/Installer/Log/*.cs` and
  `Services/Installer/Packages/*.cs`.
  - **The fixed list only looks harmless here.** It leaves nothing out of DNN
  because a clean clone has no build outputs, and its lowercase `packages/` does
  not match `Packages` under git's rules. On Windows and TFS, where case is
  folded, it would catch those folders too.
  - **The made folder of 100,000+ files is not made yet.** That is where the
  three lists would really differ.

  ### The three forks
  1. **The starting list:** measured on DNN, above. The made folder is still
  owed.
  2. **Where a person's patterns are kept:** not yet read against the work item.
  One constraint found: the function needs them before the walk, together with
  the ignore files.
  3. **One syntax or two:** today the person's patterns are read by git's rules.
  Git's syntax costs a TFS user four things: `/` instead of `\`, case, the
  leading `\` for "this folder only", and `!` under an excluded folder, which
  git refuses and TFVC allows. TFVC's syntax has no `**`, no character classes,
  and no anchoring by a slash in the middle of a pattern. Nothing further
  measured.

  ### What the design document should change
  - **A `.tfignore` exclusion does not let a walk skip the folder.** TFVC's own
  scanner looks inside an excluded folder, and NuGet's recommended `.tfignore`
  names `packages` recursively. So "the packages are never listed" holds for a
  folder git ignores and for the starting list, but for a folder a `.tfignore`
  excludes only if we choose `tf add`'s reading. That is a fork for the owner.
  - **Filename case is not one rule for ignore files.** `.gitignore` names match
  as written; `.tfignore` names match without case, as TFVC's own rule.
  - **The starting list applies only beneath folders that have no ignore file at
  or above them.**
  - **The figures for tracked files that a listing cannot see are now
  measured:** 11 here and 26 in DNN.
  - **The decide step must remember each folder's verdict.** Today it judges
  every path from the top: 2 to 10 s for about 25,000 paths, too slow for
  100,000.

  out, `discovery.oracle.test.ts`, "seed 9", 5,226 ms against its 5,000 ms
  limit, with the machine's load average at about 31.
    - Rerunning that file alone at that load failed 23 of its cases; 2 minutes
  later, at a load of about 25, all 60 passed.
  - Then, stage by stage, all passed:
    - unit: 220 files, 2,983 tests;

  - No browser was used for this piece.

  ## Stored-form research, milestone 3: the four items you asked for, all four
  done

  **Commits** on `stored-form-research`, after merging `day/2026-11-21` (a
  fast-forward to `323828e41`): `35ae6b292`, `71ef9ea5c`. Only the research
  folder changed. No product code, Memory or work items. `slopcop:markdown` and
  `docs:links:enforce` are clean. Every table cell in the README is now measured
  or named as not measured. Load is marked on each row; it ran from 5 to 61
  over the leg.

  ### 1. A page that keeps a scan as it goes, and carries on after the browser
  is closed
  Built for the resumable press (`scripts/page/page-keep.mjs`). Each frame goes
  into IndexedDB as it is set down. Tested with a persistent browser profile:
  the browser is closed at 60 %, then a new browser is opened on the same
  profile.

  **Where a page opened from disk can keep a scan:**
  - **IndexedDB:** works. Quota offered was 10 GB on this machine's disk;
  Chromium takes a share of free disk, so the owner's VM will give less (not
  measured there). A 64 MB block was put, the browser closed, and the block read
  back whole in the next browser.
  - **The origin's private file system:** refused (`SecurityError`).
  - **`showDirectoryPicker` / `showSaveFilePicker`:** both present, but neither
  can be automated, because a person must answer the dialog and Playwright's
  interception aborts it. I used IndexedDB and a blob download instead. Writing
  into a picked folder is not measured.

  **Cost of keeping each frame, slowed:**

  | | Memory only | Kept in IndexedDB |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | DNN | 22.9 s, 234 MB | 26.3 s, 229 MB (15 % more time, no more memory) |
  | 5× | 40.2 s, 313 MB | 38.4 s, 316 MB (no difference beyond load variation) |

  **Closed at 60 %, then resumed in a new browser:**

  | | Files found | Found in | Finished in | Final file |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | DNN | 6,400 | 3.4 s | 10.9 s more | byte-identical to Node's unstopped file
  |
  | 5× | 32,512 | 4.1 s | 20.4 s more, at 351 MB | byte-identical to Node's
  unstopped file |

  Not tried: killing the browser outright, or the machine losing power.

  **The page saving a copy of itself.** It uses its own head and script
  unchanged (so the policy's hash still admits it), puts the facts as base64 in
  a non-running block, and offers the result as a blob download:
  - DNN: built in 166 ms, 3.00 MB.
  - 5×: built in 419 ms, 9.98 MB, tab peak 330 MB.
  - Both copies opened from disk and answered (5×: first answer 735 ms, whole
  map 12.8 s).

  ### 2. The press, resumable and self-naming (`d1k-press-kept.mjs`, press/3)
  - **What changed:**
    - Each frame carries a label: kind, length, and for strings and records the
  first id and the count.
    - A chunk's new strings are written just before that chunk.
    - Each chunk carries its own paths and outbound wires.
    - The footer names the content as the SHA-256 of the per-section SHA-256s.
  - **Cost:** 5.8 % more bytes at DNN, 9.7 % at 5×, because string blocks are
  smaller and compress less well.
  - **Stopped at 60 % in Node:** 6,400 of 6,465 files back at DNN, 32,256 of
  32,328 at 5×.
  - **The finished file is byte-identical to an unstopped scan** in Node and in
  the page, at both sizes. Up to 255 files handed over just before a stop are
  lost and scanned again (one chunk).

  ### 3. The indexed ledger with a careful reader
  (`d6r-ledger-indexed-careful.mjs`, same bytes as before)
  - **What the reader does:**
    - indexes each piece's paths once;
    - parses only the lines a question asks for;
    - drops each piece's text after reading it, keeping only its outbound wires;
    - interns strings as the whole map loads.
  - **What of the earlier cost was the reader's.** At 5× in a slowed tab:

  | | Simple reader | Careful reader |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Memory | 1.7 GB | 537 MB |
  | Hub's Local Map | 125 s | 2.5 s |
  | Whole map | 69 s | 27.6 s |

  - **What is the form's own**, measured against the press in the same lane
  hold:
    - whole map takes 2.5× the time (27.6 s against 11.0) and 1.5× the memory
  (537 against 350 MB);
    - walking the wires takes 1.8 s against 13 ms, because it stores no array of
  wires;
    - its first answer is faster (0.26 s against 0.83);
    - the file is 1.3 to 2.2× larger.

  ### 4. Facts out of path order (Node, 5×)
  Arrival times were modelled as i + d, with d spread evenly up to 64 or 1,024
  (`scripts/reorder.mjs`).
  - **A bounded wait** (hold early arrivals, release in path order): no time
  cost worth naming; memory in proportion to what it holds, at most 549 files
  (+20 to 55 MB).
  - **Sorting at the end:** 260 to 380 MB more than waiting, growing with the
  scan.
  - Every way wrote the same bytes.
  - **What the engine owes the sink:** a bound on its disorder. Without one,
  every form must hold the whole scan.

  ### Recommendation (revised, still provisional)
  - **Stored form:** the press, now in its resumable form; the 6 to 10 % bytes
  are worth it on the owner's machine.
  - **One-way export:** the indexed ledger.

  **What changed my mind:** the careful reader moved the indexed ledger from
  "out at scale" to "a fair rival at a price": about 2.5× the whole-map load
  time, from a file 1.3 to 2.2× larger, in exchange for `grep` and `diff`. I
  still choose the press, because the Explorer loads whole maps today and the
  owner's machine is where that factor is felt. But it is now a choice between
  two forms each at its best, and the owner's taste for a readable file should
  weigh.

  **What would overturn it:**
  - the owner preferring grep-ability to that load cost;
  - the Explorer moving to reading one neighbourhood at a time;
  - only the command line mattering, in which case SQLite;
  - an engine unable to bound its disorder.

  ### Also
  - **Untried idea:** an array of wires added as a piece kind to the indexed
  ledger would likely close its walk gap. Not built, because it changes the
  form.
  - **Commands refused:** none this leg.
  - **Scratch:** kept at `/tmp/claude-1000/-workspaces-Live-Documentation/1501b2
  5e-9b79-43c7-b25c-c8b29500c331/scratchpad/stored-form-builder/` (1.6 GB:
  corpora, facts, inputs, stored outputs, pages, libraries, raw runs), trimmed
  of disposable outputs and profiles. Nothing of mine is running. Remove it when
  no further leg is wanted.

  A page that keeps its scan, and saves itself

  This is your "page that writes a page", shown possible as an experiment; none

  - A closed browser no longer loses the scan. A page opened from disk saved
    each finished piece as it went. The browser was closed at 60%; a new browser

    no extra memory and at most 15% more time.

  Choosing what a scan leaves out

    real folders.
  - .tfignore: Microsoft's open-source TFS client answered the three rules its
    documentation leaves out. A subfolder's file adds to its parent's, names
    match without case, and the last matching line wins.
  - A decision I made, for you to overrule: a left-out folder is skipped whole,
    never looked inside. TFS itself has two behaviours here; I took the one that
    guarantees your packages folders are never even listed. If a ! line might
    have rescued something inside, the listing will say so.

  ### 2. `dbo.X` and an object created as plain `X`: measured, then built
  I tried your hypothesis on both sides, reader and grader, and diffed the
  adapter's lines before and after.

  | Corpus | Lines added | Removed | Graded afterwards |

  | tSQLt | 0 | 0 | 969 / 969 |

  scripts, `01.00.00` to `01.00.07`. Those create procedures with no schema
  (`create procedure AddTab`), and later scripts name them with `dbo.` through

  - **Ten drawn at random (seed 1121), each read in both files: all ten are
  true.**

  | Using script | Its use | Script that creates it |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `02.02.00` | `alter procedure dbo.GetUsers` | `01.00.02` |
  | `02.02.02` | `ALTER procedure dbo.GetPortalByAlias` | `01.00.05` |

  `01.00.05` |

  `01.00.06` |

  | `09.00.00.01` | `EXEC dbo.[UpdateHostSetting]` | `01.00.05` |

    - In every case the later script names the same procedure an earlier version
  created.

  applies.
  - **Where the change lives.** `sqlNameMatches` in `openings.ts` now takes a
  schema-less declaration for `dbo`'s, so `dbo.X` names a declared `X`. An
  `ALTER dbo.X` now finds a `CREATE X` in the same script. The grader's
  `bindName` does the same.
  - **C# is affected too.** C# shares `sqlNameMatches`, so its SQL strings get
  this presumption as well. Regeneration changed no C# or estate doc in this
  repository.
  - **Red, then green.** The fixture's new `legacy.sql`, plus `EXEC
  dbo.ListLegacy` in `report.sql`, failed the old reader three ways: a wrong
  line, a missing link, and two invented kept names. Three tests were red; the
  reader's commit made them green.

  ### 3. The comparison's fake project names: mended inside `compare.ts`, no
  field needed
  - **How a project is named now.** A project reference is graded between the
  manifests standing in the projects' folders. That is the project file named
  for the project, or failing that the folder's one manifest (Cargo, npm, Maven,
  Gradle, Go or Python).
  - **Projects with no manifest of their own.** Their references are listed
  separately by project name, counted as neither found nor missing.
    - `rust/stockroom`'s two Cargo-target references are of this kind: `src/`
  and `tests/` hold no manifest.
    - The estate's three references are found as before.
  - **The test's bookkeeping** is now found + missing + unplaced.
  - **To grade the Cargo references as file edges too,** `scip-edges.ts` would
  need one field: each project's root file (for a Cargo target, its crate root).
  They would then be found as `src/main.rs → src/lib.rs` and `tests/report.rs →
  src/lib.rs`. The readers already draw both; the ledger now lists them as
  extra.

  ### 1. The ledger by cause: built
  - **Where it is.** `tests/integration/programs/compiler-differences.json`,
  kept once at the programs' root because one cause explains edges in several
  programs. I placed it outside the per-program `expected/` folders for that
  reason.
  - **What a cause holds.** A sentence saying what the reader does and what the
  compiler does; a kind (`gap`, `grain` or `beyond the compiler`); and the edges
  it explains, each with program and side.
  - **What reads it.**
    - `compare.ts` checks each program's missing and extra edges against it. An
  edge is identified by its side as well as its ends.
    - The comparison prints each edge's cause and kind.
  - **What fails.** `oracle.test.ts` fails on an edge no cause lists, and on a
  listed edge no longer found. Two more cases check that the side matters and
  that the ledger names only measured programs, each difference once.
  - **It can fail.** I saw it fail both ways on `go/depot` (one listed edge
  dropped, one false extra added), then restored the ledger.

  **The seven causes**, each confirmed by reading its reader (`c.ts`, `go.ts`,
  `python.ts`, `rust.ts`) and the files its edges join:

  | Cause | Kind proposed | Edges |
  | --- | --- | ---: |
  | C/C++: a call and a header's prototype, to the `.c` that defines the
  function | grain, keep | 33 missing |
  | C/C++: a name declared in a header reached only through another header | gap
  | 6 missing |
  | Go: a file to the one file the indexer makes its package's defining file |
  grain, keep | 7 missing |
  | A method called on a value whose type the code doesn't write (Go, Python,
  Rust) | gap | 3 missing |
  | Python: `from ..util import money` to `util/__init__.py` | grain, keep | 1
  extra |
  | Rust: the crate root `src/lib.rs` | grain, keep | 2 extra, 1 missing |
  | The estate's HTTP call from `GatewayClient.cs` to the gateway's
  `PaymentsController.cs` | beyond the compiler | 1 extra |

  What each one is, and why I proposed that kind:
  - **C/C++ definition (grain).** The reader draws the `#include` a file writes,
  and no file includes a `.c`. scip-clang records every use, prototype
  included, as a reference to the definition the build compiled. Which file
  defines a declared function is a build fact (what is compiled and linked into
  which program), not text. Drawing header to implementation by name is possible
  and is your call.
  - **C/C++ nested header (gap).** The reader gives an include only the names
  its own header declares, so `types.h` reached through `models.h` gets no edge.
  The compiler records the use against the declaring header however it was
  reached. The include chain is text the reader already resolves.
  - **Go package symbol (grain).** The reader resolves names used across a
  package's files and imports, and draws nothing for a package clause or an
  import by itself. scip-go gives each package one defining file (the first of
  its files by name) and records every package clause and every import of that
  package as a reference to it. The edge names nothing that file declares.
  - **Method on an inferred type (gap).** For example Go's `inventory.Listen`,
  promoted from an embedded `store.Base`; Python's `account.balance()`; Rust's
  `item.describe(…)`. The readers follow no inferred type. Closing it takes type
  inference.
  - **Python package `__init__` (grain).** The import does run that file, so the
  reader's edge is true; scip-python records only the module the name comes
  from.
  - **Rust crate root (grain).** The two extras are the compiler's own crate
  references, drawn as files. The missing one is `pub(crate)` in `quantity.rs`,
  a visibility qualifier that names nothing in `lib.rs`.
  - **The estate's HTTP extra.** It is not the reader's fault, and the ledger
  says so. A person asserted it in the estate's `hand-verified-edges.json`
  (marked remote), where it is found — that list is its right home. It shows as
  extra only because the comparison counts every drawn edge between two indexed
  files that the compiler didn't link. The plainer fix would be to leave out of
  the extras any edge the hand-verified list asserts; I've left the lists as
  they are for you to rule.

  **Two of my first-round guesses were wrong, and reading the readers corrected
  them.**
  - **Go's seven.** They are not same-package names without an import, which the
  Go reader does resolve. They are the indexer's package symbol.
  - **Python's extra.** It is the package's `__init__.py`, not a submodule
  resolution.

  ## The five rulings, as built
  1. **A folder left out is left out whole, under either syntax.**
     - `folder()` now reports every left-out folder as prunable. Where a `!`
  line at or above it could have named something inside, the answer carries a
  note naming the line, for example: ".tfignore line 2,
  `!packages\keep\x.targets`, may name files inside folders left out whole; they
  are not looked for".
     - The TFVC oracle recording now keeps `tf add`'s verdict beside the
  scanner's. The two readings differ for 3 of 468 files, all in one tree
  (`d-reinclude-under-excluded-folder`); the test holds the function to `tf add`
  there and marks those cases.
     - Where the scanner kept such a file by a `!` line at or above the folder,
  the note must name that line. One of the three (`gen/keep.cs`) was kept by a
  line in `gen/.tfignore`, inside the folder left out. `tf add` never reads that
  file, so the note must not name it, and the test says so.
     - The module's head now states the ruling.
  2. **Case in `.gitignore` is the repository's, handed in as `gitIgnoreCase`.**
     - The git oracle test asks git both ways, `core.ignorecase` false and true.
  All 33 agree: 16 named cases each way, plus the random trees.
     - A new case holds git's own folding quirks, which the port keeps: an
  escaped letter and a class's single members are compared as written, while
  ranges and `[:upper:]` fold.
     - **Asking both ways caught a fault of mine**, in the literal prefilter I
  added for speed: it treated the slash of `**/` as required. Mended the same
  hour.
     - The person's patterns and a starting list of patterns are always read
  without regard to case.
     - **Can a page read `core.ignorecase`?** For an ordinary clone, yes:
  `.git/config` is a file in the picked folder, and git writes `ignorecase =
  true` there on Windows. It does not hold when the setting comes from a global
  or system file, when `.git` is a file pointing elsewhere (a worktree or a
  submodule), or under `GIT_DIR`. Today's scanning page leaves `.git` out, so it
  would have to read that one file first.
  3. **The tool's own output is never scanned,** by a new rule kind, `own
  output`, whatever the ignore files or the person's patterns say.
  4. **Each folder's state is derived once from its parent's.** On the made
  folder, 167,833 paths (every folder and every file asked once):
     - Node: 0.50 to 0.72 s;
     - a page at full speed on two cores: 0.16 to 0.27 s;
     - a page slowed four times on two cores: 0.65 to 1.17 s.
     - The first leg took about 10 s for 25,000 paths.
  5. **The evidence rules join the starting lists:**
     - `bin` and `obj` only beside a project file;
     - `packages` only when it holds NuGet `<Id>.<Version>` folders or a
  `repositories.config`;
     - `node_modules` only when one of its first three folders holds a
  `package.json`.
     - Cost: one extra folder listing for each `packages`, and up to three more
  for each `node_modules`; no file's bytes. On the made folder that was 99 extra
  listings, against the 13,763 entries the walk listed.

  ## The made folder
  `make-folder.mjs` (committed) builds it from DNN, reading only:
  - DNN's 10,941 files, without `.git`, its `.gitignore` files, or our own docs;
  - 1,722 built files under `bin/Debug`, `bin/Release` and `obj`, beside each of
  the 82 project files;
  - NuGet-shaped `packages` folders: one at the top with 600 packages, and one
  in each of the first 45 project folders with 80. Each holds a
  `repositories.config`, plus `.nupkg`, `lib/<framework>` assemblies, resources,
  `content`, `tools` and `build` files;
  - NuGet's own recommended `.tfignore` in 15 of those 45 project folders, and
  no ignore file anywhere else.

  That is 103,329 files, 92,399 of them made, all empty. A manifest of what was
  made lets the listing count made output that it wrongly keeps.

  ## The five starting lists, with case folded
  **The made folder** (the full listings are `listings/made-*.txt`):

  | Starting list | Kept | Left out | Made output wrongly kept | Real source
  wrongly left out | Entries the lazy walk listed | Walk and decide |
  | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
  | nothing | 76,899 | 26,430 | 65,969 | 0 | 123,418 | 5.6 s |
  | fixed (`bin/`, `obj/`, `packages/`, `node_modules/`, `.vs/`, `TestResults/`)
  | 11,228 | 92,101 | 313 | 12 | 13,749 | 0.44 s |
  | `VisualStudio.gitignore` | 11,170 | 92,159 | 313 | 63 | 16,789 | 1.4 s |
  | TFVC's 56 defaults | 40,374 | 62,955 | 29,543 | 29 | 121,805 | 6.6 s |
  | by evidence | 11,242 | 92,087 | 313 | 1 | 13,763, and 99 extra listings |
  0.36 s |

  **Clean DNN** (a copy in my scratch, without our docs), as a TFS folder with
  no ignore file:

  | Starting list | Left out | Real source wrongly left out |
  | --- | ---: | ---: |
  | nothing | 0 | 0 |
  | fixed | 15 | 12 (`Services/Installer/Packages/*.cs`, once case folds) |
  | VisualStudio | 73 | 63 (also `Services/Installer/Log/*.cs`) |
  | TFVC defaults | 99 | 29 (`Website/js/Debug/*`) |
  | evidence | 1 | 1, `Packages/repositories.config`, which is NuGet's own file
  and arguably right |

  With its own `.gitignore` files, clean DNN leaves out 26 files under every
  list, by DNN's own rules: a starting list never applies beneath a `.gitignore`
  at the top.

  **What the numbers say:**
  - Every list that names folders by name leaves out real source sitting in
  folders called `Packages`, `Log` or `Debug`. Only the evidence rules don't.
  - TFVC's defaults keep 29,543 package files (everything that is not a `.dll`,
  `.pdb`, `.exe` and the like), and they list almost everything.
  - **New fork for the design:** every list keeps the same 313 made files, the
  `bin` and `obj` output in the 15 project folders that hold NuGet's
  `.tfignore`. That file names `packages` but not `bin` or `obj`, and the
  starting list switches off wherever an ignore file stands. Under TFVC itself
  the server's global list would leave them out. One answer is to let the
  starting list's build folders apply beneath an ignore file that doesn't
  mention them.

  ## Listing in a page
  - **`webkitdirectory`:** handing over the made folder's 103,329 files took
  73.4 s on two cores at full speed, load about 9. Building the list of names
  from them took 0.5 s more. The 73.4 s includes Playwright's own handing over,
  which cannot be separated out. This input always lists everything, packages
  included, before the page sees anything.
  - **A lazy walk** through a directory handle cannot be automated: the picker's
  prompt cannot be pressed. It is untimed. Node's lazy walk is the stand-in:
  with the evidence rules it listed 13,763 entries where the input listed
  103,329 files.
  - I ran the browser once, through the lane (label `leave-out`), 117.6 s. The
  bundle was built before the hold.

  ## Forks 2 and 3 (in the README, against `scan-leaves-no-trace.md`)
  **Where a person's patterns are kept.** The function needs them before the
  walk, together with the ignore files, so they belong with the scan's settings.
  The options:
  - on the command line, inside the scan's own output folder: never scanned, so
  no trace in version control;
  - on the command line, outside the scanned folder: no trace at all, but lost
  when the folder moves;
  - in a page, inside the HTML file it saves: a blank copy carries none, a
  filled copy carries its folder's;
  - as a file in the scanned folder: leaves a trace, so only when the person
  asks.

  **One syntax or two.** Today's reading is git's rules with case folded.
  - The cost to a TFS user is writing `\` as `/`, and anchoring by a slash in
  the middle where TFVC anchors only by a leading `\`.
  - Ruling 1 removes the largest difference, `!` beneath a folder that is left
  out.
  - Accepting `\` as `/` on top of that would read every line of NuGet's
  `.tfignore` and TFVC's default list the same way. That last point is reasoned,
  not measured.

  repository and every fixture.

  ## For the design document, beyond your own list of corrections
  - **The 313-file fork above:** whether the starting list's build folders apply
  beneath a `.tfignore` that doesn't mention them.
  - **Evidence costs listings:** the evidence rules need one level more listing
  for `packages` and two for `node_modules`, but no bytes.
  - **`webkitdirectory` lists everything first.** Only a directory handle can
  walk lazily, and that path cannot be tested automatically.
  - **Pages and `core.ignorecase`:** a page can read it from the repository's
  `.git/config`, with the exceptions above.

  ## The half-empty arrival: cause and mend
  **Cause:** a folder chosen in the search ends in `focusElement`, which handed
  `focusTransform` the directory's label alone as its target. `focusTransform`
  centres a target that fits, so the folder's name stood at the frame's middle,
  the half of the window above it empty and the folder running on below.

  **Mend:** for a directory label, the target is the directory's whole box. It
  is centred where it fits and entered at its label where it does not. The new
  case: the estate's `PaymentService` chosen in the search stands whole in the
  frame, with as much space above as below. It passed on the full build. I did
  not run the case against the code from before the mend; the "before" evidence
  is the playtest's shot 005 and my reading of the code.

  **Two more faults went into the same commit,** both found while looking at the
  trial:
  - **Stale newcomer.** The folder the search opened stayed pending as a step's
  newcomer, because the focus that framed it never let it go. The next folder
  opened inside it was then framed as that first folder again; on the estate,
  opening `Data` inside `PaymentService` left `Payment.cs` cut at the bottom
  partly for this reason. A focus now lets go of the newcomer its own change
  made.
  - **First row read above the label.** An opened directory's "first row" was
  read from every box it holds. A membrane need not be a rectangle, so some of
  those boxes stand above the label; the pan entered there and pushed the label
  below the view (this happened on `live-docs/history`). The first row is now
  read under the label.

  **These change Least Pan, the default:** the estate's `Data` opening now pans
  to `Data`'s own label and first row, and `Payment.cs` comes in whole.
  `PaymentService.cs` is still cut at the right edge.

  ## The lever
  - **Where:** Tuning → 2D Layout → **Step**, after Arrival and Framing. Values:
  Least Pan (default), Whole, With Its Lines. A link carries a non-default
  choice, so the playtest instrument can set `localMap.step=whole|lines`. The
  Explorer design describes it as a trial, in a paragraph after Framing's.
  - **Arithmetic** (`camera-intent.ts`):
    - `stepWholeFrame` keeps the scale where the whole newcomer fits. Otherwise
  it eases out just far enough, never past `ARRIVAL_FLOOR_RATIO` and never in.
  Where even the floor cannot hold it, the label and first row are brought in.
    - `stepLinesFrame` is the frame Fit would give the newcomer and the cards
  its lines reach (read from the connection paths' source and target ids), never
  closer than reading scale, under the same floor.
  - **Motion:** the ease is the following camera's, so any input ends it, and
  reduced motion makes it a cut.
  - **Fork I decided:** under Whole and With Its Lines, opening a directory
  whose label was already drawn (by its handle) also counts as a step. Under
  Least Pan it does not, as before. Without this, opening `live-docs/history` by
  its handle would give the lever nothing to frame.
  - **The newcomer** is the whole of what the step produced: the opened
  directory's box with its label, or the new card.

  ### 2. A path written without its extension
  pwsh 7.7.0 was run with `pwsh -NoProfile -NonInteractive -File run.ps1` beside
  folders built for each case. The full table is in the account. What it said:
  - **`.ps1` is added.** With only `a/build.ps1` present, all of these ran it:
  `a/build`, `& a/build`, `& "$PSScriptRoot/a/build"`, `./a/build`, and the
  dot-source `. a/build`.
  - **The exact name wins when both stand.** With `b/build` and `b/build.ps1`,
  `Get-Command b/build` is `b/build`. A non-executable one fails when run; an
  executable one runs.
  - **No other extension is tried.** `d/build`, beside only `d/build.psm1`, gave
  `CommandNotFoundException`.
  - **`.ps1` is added whatever the name ends in.** `f/build.local` and
  `f/Get-Thing.Tests` each ran their `.ps1`.
  - **A name without a folder is never resolved to a script.** `. Stop-It` and
  `& Stop-It`, beside `g/Stop-It.ps1`, gave `CommandNotFoundException`.

  **The rule:** for a script dot-sourced or run by a path, take the file so
  named, else add `.ps1`. This applies to paths only.
  - My first version also added `.ps1` to bare names. On dbatools it resolved `.
  Stop-Function` in
  `private/functions/flowcontrol/Test-ElevationRequirement.ps1` to the
  `Stop-Function.ps1` beside it. pwsh's answer for `. Stop-It` corrected that
  before anything was committed.
  - The grader's record script (`powershell-ast.ps1`, `Resolve-FixtureFile
  -Script`) now applies the same answers.

  **Test:** red (`a/build` and the dot-source unresolved, said as unfound
  loads), then green.

  ### The dropped folder: where it stops
  Every run went through the lane (label `leave-out`), at a load of 3 to 10.
  - **Made folder (103,329 files):** `dragEnter`, `dragOver` and `drop` were

  - **Clean DNN (10,941 files):** the protocol call hung until my 170 s timeout.

  So the lazy walk stays untimed until a person drops or picks a folder. This is
  Playwright 1.61.1's headless Chromium; a newer or headed browser is untried.
  For a person, a drop is a second way to give a folder with no dialog. Chromium
  documents that the dropped item gives the same directory handle the picker
  does, so the page could walk it lazily and never list `packages`, unlike
  `webkitdirectory`'s 73 s for everything. Writing beside the code would still
  need their leave. None of that last part is measured.

  ## Both layouts stand, the trial page is built, and it caught a fault in the
  function

  One commit, `6bf47891a`, on `page-check`, after `git merge day/2026-11-21`.
  Nothing pushed.

  **The gate passed in one run.** Load was 3.3 at its start and 16.3 at its end.
  - Unit: 222 files, 3,029 tests. Integration: 34 files, 468 tests.
  - Live Docs regenerated and linted, lint, build, test type-check, link
  enforcement and the three audits all clean.

  **Your count of 91 was right for the third leg; my 92 was wrong.** The `.git`
  pointer-file check went into an existing case rather than a new one, so it
  added no test. The three leave-out files now hold 92, because this leg added
  one case (below).

  ## The trial page
  It lives in `Probes/leave-out/choice/`, and nothing in the product changed.
  The owner opens `choice/choice.html`, a 30 KB file run from disk.
  - **Sources:** `page.html`, `page.mjs`, and `model.mjs`, which the page and
  its terminal twin share. `build.mjs` builds it.
  - **Policy:** one script admitted by its hash, `default-src 'none'`, listeners
  only and no inline handlers. It holds `leaveOut`. It reads only names, the
  text of ignore files, and `.git/config` for `core.ignorecase`.

  **Giving it a folder, three ways,** each an icon, one word and an accessible
  name:
  - **Pick:** a directory handle, walked lazily; a folder left out is never
  listed.
  - **Drop here:** the same handle, if the browser gives one.
  - **Choose:** `<input webkitdirectory>`; the browser lists everything first.

  While it walks it hands the thread back every 20 folders and says how many it
  has listed. The timings line at the bottom gives the listing and deciding
  times, so whoever picks or drops a folder there times the first lazy walk.

  **By reason:**
  - A totals line.
  - What is left out, grouped by a plain reason, largest first, with folder and
  file counts. "Not looked inside" appears where nothing was listed. The
  reasons:
    - "Build output beside a project", "NuGet packages", "Node packages";
    - "`.tfignore`: packages", "`.gitignore`: .vscode/";
    - "The tool's own output", "Version control's own folder", "Your pattern:
  …".
  - Each reason row has a Keep, and opens to its folders, each with its own
  Keep.
  - Then what will be read, by kind of file and by top folder.

  **Folders:**
  - A tri-state checkbox tree. Each row has a name, kept and left-out counts,
  and a reason beside a row that is left out.
  - Only opened rows are drawn.
  - One tab stop: arrows, Home and End move; Space ticks; Enter opens.
  - Ticking a folder that is left out adds `!/path/` and walks it then.

  **In both:**
  - a pattern field (git's rules, case folded), with the person's patterns
  listed and each removable;
  - the evidence switch, on to begin with;
  - the notes about `!` lines;
  - **Scan**, which in this trial only says how many files it would read.

  The look follows the design system's dark grounds and text tones, with the
  offered blue for controls. No word is below 11 px, focus is visible, and there
  is no emoji.

  ## The function change, with its test
  **What the pictures showed.** On the first pictures DNN's nested `.gitignore`
  files did nothing on the first walk, and took effect only after a later change
  of choice.

  **The cause.** `leaveOut` asked for a folder's own ignore files when the
  folder itself was decided. A lazy walk only lists a folder, and reads its
  ignore files, after deciding it.

  **The mend.** A folder's ignore files are now asked for when something
  *inside* it is decided.

  **The test.** The new case in `leaveOut.test.ts`: deciding folder `a` asks
  only for the top folder's files; deciding `a/x.log` then asks for `a`'s. Under
  the old code `a` was asked as soon as the folder was decided, so the first
  expectation would fail. I reasoned that, but did not run the old code against
  the new case.

  **Earlier numbers.** The Node probes of earlier legs listed folders on demand
  inside the same call, so their numbers are unaffected. The never-run
  `drop-walk.mjs` had the same ordering fault.

  ## The pictures
  They are in `choice/screenshots/`, at 1,280 by 800, given through the folder
  input because automation cannot pick or drop. For each folder there is: empty,
  by reason, a group opened, a folder kept, a pattern added, and the tree with
  keyboard focus.

  **Clean DNN** (10,941 files):
  - 10,915 files read, 26 left out;
  - handed over and decided in 6.2 s, of which deciding was 344 ms;
  - keeping `Build/Tools/` brings it to 13 left out; the pattern `Build/` brings
  it to 108.

  **The made folder** (103,329 files):
  - 10,929 files read, 92,400 left out, in three rows: "Build output beside a
  project" (162 folders, 1,714 files), "NuGet packages" (31 folders, 64,271
  files), "`.tfignore`: packages" (15 folders, 26,415 files);
  - about 65 to 71 s is the browser listing everything before the page sees
  anything;
  - the walk over what it was handed takes 2.7 s, of which listing is 266 ms and
  deciding 38 ms; the rest is the thread being handed back.
  - **A fault of the page's own was fixed:** it first took 12.7 s, because it
  counted the files beneath each left-out folder by scanning the whole list.

  ## What my eye says
  - **"Why is this left out" and "how do I keep it": by reason is faster for
  both.** The reason and its Keep sit on the same row.
  - **An opened group is heavy.** 162 rows each with a full Keep button. One
  Keep for the group and a quieter control per folder would read better.
  - **The tree is better at "what about this folder?" and worse at "why?"** The
  reason shows only at the right edge of rows that are left out. On the made
  folder nearly every project reads "mixed", because each holds a `bin` and an
  `obj`, so the tick tells little until a row is opened.
  - **"NuGet packages" and "`.tfignore`: packages" read as the same thing** to a
  person, though different rules made them. Whether to merge them in the
  display is your design question.
  - **Wording:** "`.gitignore`: .vscode/" assumes the person knows what a
  `.gitignore` is. "0 folders not looked inside" is true after the folder input
  but meant for a lazy walk.
  - **What the page hides:** loose files at the top level show only in the
  counts, since the tree lists folders.
  - **Mended after the first pictures:**
    - the layout switch did not hide the other layout (a CSS rule beat
  `hidden`);
    - the patterns list ran under the footer;
    - the Scan icon was a plus;
    - the drop target looked disabled (it now uses the offered blue and says
  "Drop here");
    - one `.tfignore` pattern split into 15 rows, one per file (now grouped by
  pattern).

  **Which layout strangers understand faster is theirs to say.**
