  ## Second-pass review of `readers-gathering` at a14ed4cd9

  Seven of the nine faults I reported are gone, and finding 5 is mostly gone:
  the PowerShell grader now takes its shipped names from pwsh itself, but only
  Linux 7's. Finding 9 is not: the ASP.NET reader's new script rule has two new
  faults that real projects will hit, and no test catches either. I also found a
  wrong kept name from the PowerShell loop mend and a hole in the C# grader.

  How I worked:
  - The worktree was reset to a14ed4cd9, then `npm ci` and `npm run build` (exit
  0).
  - I committed nothing. Temporary edits to code, made to break a reader on
  purpose, were put back with the file tools; `git status` is clean. My scratch
  folder `readers-review-2/` is removed.
  - One subagent (general-purpose, Opus) ran the corpus graders; its figures are
  at the end.
  - "Shown" below means I ran it: the generator CLI on my own small folders,
  `npm run oracle:csharp` on a folder I wrote (it compiles with Roslyn and
  evaluates with MSBuild), `npm run oracle:sql` (ScriptDom), or `pwsh`.

  ### 1. My nine findings, run again with neighbouring cases

  | # | Finding | Now | How shown |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 1 | C# nested type inherited from a base class | Gone | `oracle:csharp` on
  my folder: `Derived.cs` agrees with the compiler |
  | 2 | C# alias to the non-generic type | Gone | `using H = Lib.Helper;`,
  `using HI = Lib.Helper<int>;`, `using L = Lib;` with `L.Helper` and
  `L.Helper<string>`: all agree with the compiler |
  | 3 | Commented-out `ProjectReference` | Gone | Commented references in a
  `.csproj` and in a `.slnx` give no line |
  | 4 | `Pause` and other module-less built-ins | Gone | `Pause` no longer wires
  to a workspace definition |
  | 5 | PowerShell grader sharing the reader's shipped list | Mostly gone |
  `powershell-wires.ts` now runs `powershell-shipped.ps1` on the machine's pwsh;
  caveat in section 3 |
  | 6 | `$m = 'Pester'` then `Import-Module $m` | Gone | It also correctly
  refuses a `$m` reassigned inside an `if`, and a `param()` default |
  | 7 | Archetype change spilling into every language | Gone (by reading) | The
  non-PowerShell path is back to the whole-path rule |
  | 8 | SQL schemas | Gone, with one open part | One-part names bind by the
  module's schema then `dbo`, and agree with ScriptDom's grader on my cases
  (procedure, view, trigger, inline function, an `EXEC` inside a module,
  `TRUNCATE` and dynamic SQL taking the caller's default) |
  | 9 | ASP.NET `~/` | Not gone | Findings A and B below |

  What stays open under finding 8: `SisterDb..Orders` and `SisterDb.dbo.Orders`
  are now drawn as same-database source links to this folder's `dbo.Orders`. The
  database part is dropped, and the SQL grader does the same thing on purpose
  (`sql-compare.ts` around lines 77–89), so neither side can catch it. That is a
  design decision to make, not a fault the grader will ever report.

  ### 2. What the mends broke or got newly wrong

  **A. ASP.NET Core: `~/` in `<script src>` points at the wrong folder.** New on
  this branch. Shown; I am highly sure.
  - Input:
    - `Web/Web.csproj` with `Sdk="Microsoft.NET.Sdk.Web"`
    - `Web/Views/Shared/_Layout.cshtml` containing `<script src="~/js/site.js">`
  and `<script src="~/lib/jquery/dist/jquery.min.js">`
    - `Web/wwwroot/js/site.js` and `Web/wwwroot/lib/jquery/dist/jquery.min.js`
  - We draw: no link, plus the notice "paths no file in the workspace holds,
  left unlinked: …~/js/site.js; …~/lib/jquery/dist/jquery.min.js".
  - Right: in ASP.NET Core, `~/` is the web root (`wwwroot` by default;
  Microsoft's "Static files in ASP.NET Core"). Both files exist.
  - Every ASP.NET Core `_Layout.cshtml` is shaped like this. The directives'
  rule (`~/` = the project folder) is right for Web Forms and MVC 5, but not for
  Core's static files.

  **B. Commented-out script tags are still read.** Already wrong before this
  branch, but the mend says it reads scripts "by the directives' rule", and the
  directives skip comments. Shown; I am highly sure.
  - Input, in `Classic/Default.aspx`:
    - `<%-- <script src="Scripts/server-commented.js"></script> --%>`
    - `<!-- <script src="Scripts/legacy.js"></script> -->`
  - In the `_Layout.cshtml` above: `<!-- <script src="~/js/old.js"></script>
  -->`.
  - We draw: links to all three files.
  - Right:
    - ASP.NET removes `<%-- --%>` from its output.
    - A browser loads no script that sits inside an HTML comment.
    - The directive reader already strips server comments (`readDirectives` uses
  `SERVER_COMMENT`); `resolveScriptDependencies` strips neither kind.

  **C. PowerShell loop over written-out names keeps names that were
  reassigned.** New, from the loop mend. Shown; I am highly sure.
  - Input: `foreach ($m in 'ModE', 'ModF') { $m = "$m.Extra"; Import-Module $m
  }`
  - We keep the names `ModE` and `ModF`.
  - pwsh, for the same loop body: `would import: ModE.Extra`.
  - Plain cases are right: `'ModA','ModB' | ForEach-Object { Import-Module $_ }`
  and `foreach ($m in 'ModC','ModD') { Import-Module $m }` keep ModA–ModD.

  **D. A script run by a path without its extension is read as Linux finds it,
  not Windows.** New, from the extension mend. Shown on Linux; the Windows half
  is inferred, so I am only moderately sure.
  - Input: `& ./tools/build`, where `tools/build` is an extensionless shell
  script beside `tools/build.ps1`.
  - We draw `ext.ps1 → tools/build` (the shell script).
  - pwsh 7.7 on Linux agrees: `Get-Command ./tools/build` gives `tools/build`,
  which then fails to run.
  - On Windows, PowerShell finds a path command by trying `.ps1` and the PATHEXT
  extensions. I believe it never runs an extensionless file, so it would run
  `build.ps1`. I could not run Windows here.
  - The engine promises the Windows answer, and a `build` beside a `build.ps1`
  is common in cross-platform repositories.

  **E. A member a C# type inherits still does not hide a type.** Older than this
  branch, and outside what the mend covered. Shown; I am highly sure.
  - Input: `Base` has `public int Gadget { get; set; }`; another file has `using
  App.Other;` and `class MemberOverUsing : Base { int Twice() => Gadget * 2;
  }`; `App.Other.Gadget` is a class.
  - We draw `MemberOverUsing.cs → Other.cs`. The compiler binds the property and
  draws no type edge; `oracle:csharp` counts ours as "a namesake 1".
  - The base lookup only searches nested types, not members. On real code the
  grader counts 2 such on DNN and 3 on mojoPortal.

  **F. Windows PowerShell 5.1 aliases always win.** New. By reading; I am only
  moderately sure this matters.
  - `SHIPPED_ALIASES` is the union of both editions, so 5.1-only aliases
  (`curl`, `wget`, `ls`, `cat`, `sort`, `cp`, `rm`, …) always beat a function
  the script loads.
  - In PowerShell 7 on Linux those names are not aliases, so a loaded `function
  curl` shim is what answers there. The call edge is dropped; nothing is drawn
  wrongly.
  - On Windows `sort` and `ls` stay aliases, so this is consistent with the
  Windows promise. I am not counting it as a fault.

  Checked and sound:
  - An alias whose name clashes with an inherited or namespace type: at
  file-pair level our lines equal the compiler's, because the alias directive
  itself binds the target.
  - SQL dynamic strings inside modules take the caller's default schema.
  - The trigger takes its table's schema.

  ### 3. The graders: can each fail, and what each misses

  I broke the readers on purpose, one rule at a time, then put every edit back:

  | Rule switched off | What went red |
  | --- | --- |
  | C# base-type lookup | 2 unit tests, `csharp-oracle.test.ts` (names),
  `oracle.test.ts` (csharp/names) |
  | C# alias arity | unit test, `csharp-oracle.test.ts`, `oracle.test.ts` |
  | `withoutXmlComments` | 5 unit tests: project, solution, config,
  `dotnetProjects`, `dotnetXml` |
  | SQL `bindSqlName` | 2 tests in `openings.test.ts` and `sql.test.ts`,
  `sql-oracle.test.ts` on `sql-schemas`, and the C# SQL-binding unit test |
  | PowerShell alias mapping | only the unit test in `powershell.test.ts`;
  `powershell-oracle.test.ts` stayed green, because its fixture has no alias
  call |

  During one of these runs `treeSitter.test.ts` "marks the process for a trap in
  the parse, in the index pass" failed at its 5-second timeout. It passes when
  run alone, so it is flaky under parallel load.

  What the C# grader (`csharp-compare.ts` with the kept `csharp-compiler.json`)
  can pass while the reader is wrong:
  - **New fixture files are never graded.** Line 125: the reader only analyses
  the C# files listed in the kept record's `declared`, and only edges between
  such files count. A C# file added to a fixture without re-running `--write` is
  silently left out, and nothing ties the kept record to the fixture's current
  sources (no hash). This is a test that cannot fail for new cases.
  - **It compares files, not names.** A wrong link to a file the compiler also
  links for another reason (the alias case above) is invisible.

  Each grader's independence:
  - **C#:** project references come from MSBuild's evaluation, falling back to
  System.Xml for projects MSBuild cannot evaluate. Independent of our pattern
  reading.
  - **PowerShell:** shipped names come from pwsh on this machine, so the grader
  is independent of the reader's list. It can only grade PowerShell 7 on Linux;
  the Windows 5.1 half (aliases, `mkdir`, `more`) cannot be graded here. The
  loaded-else-unique rule is still written twice, so the rule itself is graded
  only by `powershell-verdicts.ts` against the hand reading. That mode was not
  run: its samples file lies in a record folder I was told not to read.
  - **SQL:** binding is implemented independently from ScriptDom's parse and
  quoted from Microsoft's documentation. It shares the "database part ignored"
  rule with the reader, and grades per file rather than per module: two modules
  in one file that bind differently look the same. On my case it caught one
  disagreement that is older than this branch: `SELECT Id FROM Srv...Orders`
  (linked server, schema left out) draws `-> tables.sql :: dbo.orders (linked)`,
  where the grader expects the kept name `srv...orders`.

  I did not read the two ledgers, `hand-verified-misses.json` and
  `compiler-differences.json`, in full. `csharp-oracle.test.ts` requires its
  disagreement list to equal the register exactly (a fixed miss or a new one
  both fail), and every register is empty.

  ### 4. Merges and the engine's promises
  - PowerShell's `node:crypto` digest was replaced by a hash written in the

  - `tests/page-scan/node-needs.test.ts` passes.

  - I did not re-read the two merges of the day's branch (`leaveOut*`, the
  Explorer client); they are not mine to review. Nothing I ran showed them

    Probes/leave-out/choice/screenshots/made-5-tree-focus.png)): the folder tree
    with three-state checkboxes, the familiar installer pattern.

  My eye:
  - By reason answers "why is this left out?" and "how do I keep it?" on one
    row.
  - The tree answers "what about this folder?", but on your shape nearly every
    project shows the half-ticked box, because each holds a bin and an obj. The
    boxes say little until a row is opened.
  - By reason is heavy once opened: 162 folder rows, each with its own button
    (picture (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/l
    eave-out/choice/screenshots/made-2-group-opened.png)).
  - Some wording is still in the tool's voice, not a person's (picture
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/leave-out/
    choice/screenshots/dnn-4-pattern-added.png), the line under the buttons).

  I lean towards By reason as the main view, but your eye decides, and no
  strangers have tried either yet.

  To try it: download choice.html (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/1
  1/2026-11-21/Probes/leave-out/choice/choice.html) (right-click it in VS Code's
  file list, then Download), open it in Edge or Chrome, and press Pick on any
  folder of your own. Its Scan button only says how many files it would read.

  One number only a person can get: the last line of the page times the walk
  that skips left-out folders. Automation cannot press the folder picker, so
  yours would be the first measurement. Through the browser's older folder
  input, which lists everything first, the 103,329-file folder took 65 to 71
  seconds before the page saw anything.

  The page also caught a real fault in the deciding function: nested .gitignore
  files were missed on a first walk. That is fixed, with a test I ran against
  the old code and saw fail.

  The reviewer's second pass over the readers

  Seven of its nine earlier findings are gone. It found these, all now with two
  builders:

  - ASP.NET Core scripts unlinked. In Core, ~/js/site.js means
    wwwroot/js/site.js; we read it as the project folder, so every Core layout
    page shows its scripts unconnected. The rule is being measured on two public
    Core codebases before it is built.
  - Commented-out script tags drawn as real connections.
  - PowerShell: a loop over module names keeps the wrong names when the loop
    body changes its variable.
  - C#: a property inherited from a base class does not hide a class of the same
    name (5 cases across two large codebases).
  - SQL: a table on a linked server, written without its schema, is tied to a
    local table.
  - Two tests that could not fail: a C# file added to a sample went ungraded,
    and the PowerShell alias rule was checked by one unit test only.

  The readers reach the day branch after these fixes, my review of them, and one
  rebuild of the history (about 71 minutes).

  One thing you could settle in thirty seconds

  When a script runs & ./tools/build and both build (no extension) and build.ps1
  exist, we draw the line to build, which is what PowerShell 7 on Linux does.
  The reviewer believes Windows runs build.ps1; I recall the opposite; neither
  of us has a Windows machine. A builder is reading PowerShell's source for the
  answer.

  On your own Windows machine (not the work one), this shows it without running
  either file:

  mkdir t; cd t; 'x' > build; 'x' > build.ps1
  Get-Command ./build | Format-List Name, CommandType, Source

  `pathfind.test.ts` with none of `history`'s cards whole. `cutPan` now brings
  the nearest card cut at that edge (the one that reaches least far past it)
  wholly into view on both axes, at the same scale; the next press goes to the
  next card.

  the owner: "a multitude of self-loops creates the illusion of a directional
  arrow. Specifically, the blue pin to the right of `DocLocation` gives this
  impression. There also is this semblance of a half-arrow". The owner asked for
  "a slightly more distinctive loop-around shape … highly atypical for a
  node-to-node connector".
    - Record: `2026/10/2026-10-05/2026-10-05.1.record.md` L776, L868.
    - Decision log: `.mdmd/layer-3/architectural-decisions.mdmd.md` L351-356.
  Still open there: "the shape that says 'behind' is still to be found" (owner:
  "a little rough").
  - **Membrane Map Q7, 2026-03-23.** The agent offered two options:
    - "Option A: Direction lives on the LINE, not the pin … ▶ arrowhead +
  green→blue gradient encodes direction. Animated pulse optional."
    - "Option B: Preserve L/R grammar". The owner chose it: "Believe it or not,
  Option B has my attention the very most … an inter-card corset is the most
  visually understandable and truthful".
    - "Hover-promotion" of a stub to its full route was deferred then. It is
  built now: the route shows only on hover or focus.
    - Sources: `2026/03/2026-03-23/2026-03-23.1.md` L2172-2197, L2287;
  `.mdmd/layer-3/membrane-map.mdmd.md` L57, L59, L170.
  - **Bundles by offering pin** (2026-10-05, the owner accepted the agent's
  answer). The owner's complaint was "guitar strings"; they asked to keep wires
  "bunched until actual navigable separation is warranted". Built as a shared
  run as wide as its member count. Decision log L486-489.
  - **World Map flow dashes** (2026-09-28). Owner: "I absolutely adore the
  motion from outputs to inputs across the dangling connectors in your world map
  demo. I would love that gentle data flow motion on the local map too."
  `2026/09/2026-09-27/2026-09-27.1.md` L1534; `Memory/owner.md` L358;
  `Memory/ideas.md` L23. The Local Map motion stays an idea, not built.
  - **3D button cue counts returning connections** (owner 2026-11-01/02: "That's
  truly what the switch from 2D to 3D exists for"). Extended 2026-11-19: either
  lace, or the card's foot, lights the whole connection. Explorer L74. The
  owner has not said whether it reads (direction L207).
  - **3D hover words "X uses Y: n references"** are kept after the wire colour
  was withdrawn. direction L300.

  ## 4. Open or unchosen ideas

  - **How direction is carried in 3D.** "**Open:** how direction is carried and
  how the web becomes legible enough to find a thing in." (direction L300)
  - **The two words for the roles.** "Which two words name the roles is a
  question open with the owner" (explorer L224). The owner on the word:
  "'Exposes' and 'Consumes'/'Utilizes'/'Uses' could be fine … I don't feel fully
  settled on this." (owner.md L390; direction L348)
  - **A whole-file pin against a flow pin.** Owner on 2026-11-19: "we need to do
  some disambiguation between the node-level pins ("I eat the whole file") and
  the flow pins ("I am involved in some way")". Not yet put to the owner.
  (direction L207)
  - **Laces that read as "behind".** Still open (decision log L356). A returning
  connection routed round its own card was noted and not built (ideas L162;
  direction L165).
  - **A returning connection is explained only at reading size.** It "looks like
  a mistake when zoomed out" (direction L166). The quoted words are root's; see
  contradiction 5.
  - **Motion on the Local Map** (ideas L23). Motion "to convey information" was
  left open (ideas L28; owner 2026-10-06: "perhaps motion, sparingly used, could
  be leveraged in other ways to convey information? I'm not yet sure.").
  - **A pause for the World Map's flow.** WCAG 2.2.2 fails today. This is a fork
  for the owner. (`2026/11/2026-11-18/Probes/design-system/README.md` L55;
  interaction L419)
  - **Unchosen ideas:**
    - A hover popover floating over the map, set aside (ideas L147).
    - "Semantic colours as 'a special glow' on a connector" (owner 2025-12-08;
  colour-pass history L230).
    - RTL and flipping the flow direction (owner 2026-03-23; colour-pass history
  L228).
    - Copilot's "boundary ports or edge badges on the hovered membrane"
  (membrane-map L139).
  - **Asked on 2026-11-18/19 and still open:** "should what a wire carries, and
  which way it goes, be said where the wire is (on hover, or on the card)? And
  should the map make the reader's Dependents and Dependencies easier to find?"
  (`2026/11/2026-11-18/Probes/blind-playtests/README.md` L150)
  - **The issue that tracks it now.** "Which way a line runs, and what it
  carries", state open, not begun.
  (`AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/stranger-follows-a-request.md` L20-25)

  ## 5. What testers misread (their words)

  **2026-11-18/19** (Opus, Sonnet, Haiku; tasks estate-directory and
  estate-uses). This record is in
  `2026/11/2026-11-18/Probes/blind-playtests/README.md`, not under
  `2026-11-19/Probes`. Nothing in `2026-11-19/Probes/*` covers wire direction.

  - Two testers said they "couldn't get labels or tooltips" for the lines and
  took direction "from the legend's 'Offers → Uses'" (L41).
  - "I read the direction of each line from the map's colours, so a few arrows
  could be the other way around" (Opus low, estate-directory 2, L145).
  - "the connections are only as exact as the lines I could trace" (Sonnet,
  estate-uses 2). Lines into the App.config files "overlap" and were "hardest to
  follow" (L144-147).
  - The cycle was read as a link "back to itself" in three of four Opus-medium
  runs and in 5 of 10 runs overall (L40, L139-141).

  **2026-11-21** (`2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-playtests/README.md`):

  - **estate-directory** (L269):
    - Sonnet 1 had App.config → `PaymentsContext.cs` and `PaymentService.cs` →
  App.config "both the wrong way".
    - Sonnet 2: "I read the directions from the line colours, so a few details
  may be off".
    - Haiku 2: "I read the links from the on-screen lines and badges rather than
  the underlying data, so the exact wiring is my best reading".
    - Haiku 1: "I couldn't read every edge label, so I can't say exactly which
  file each line runs to".
  - **Step trial:** "I read the links off the diagram, so the direction of each
  one is my best reading" (L506).
  - **Cycle:** "App.config and PaymentService.cs seem to refer to each other"
  and "a link with App.config that loops back on itself (the map flags a cycle)"
  (L268). Also "one of its connections returns to itself" and "so the two
  reference each other" (L438). No tester named the cycle through
  `PaymentsContext.cs` (L518).
  - **estate-trace, SQL Server → Oracle** (L176). The sqlserver card says "calls
  oracle"; oracle's card says it offers "CENTRAL.ACCOUNT · table" and is
  "called by sqlserver". Testers said:

    - "It may update the account, but that's my guess" (Opus 2)
    - "the payment ends up there" (Sonnet 2)
    - "the map doesn't say" (Sonnet 1, Haiku 1)
    - "downstream of where the payment is saved" (Haiku 2)

    The source only reads the balance there. Sonnet 2 also "read portal →
  gateway 'from the arrows on the map'" (L166), though the World Map draws no
  arrowheads.

  ## 6. Contradictions and doubtful attributions

  1. **The World Map flow the owner "adored" ran the other way.** On 2026-09-28
  the flow went "the way the request goes", from the caller's blue door to the
  server's green door (`2026/09/2026-09-28/Probes/black-boxes.md` L29;
  `2026-09-27.1.md` L1237). On 2026-09-29 the agent reversed the curves with the
  colour swap: "the flow animation runs the same way" (`2026-09-29.2.md`
  ~L1308, L1356). The record shows the swap reported to the owner, not asked
  separately. visual-foundations L735 cites the 09-28 quote as the reason for
  today's flow "from offers to uses", which is the reverse of what the owner
  saw.
  2. **World Map words against the drawing.** The card head says "sqlserver
  calls oracle" (`views/worldMap/controller.ts` L1425, L1433). The wire's
  gradient and dashes run from oracle's offering door to sqlserver's using door
  (L690-704). The help says "A wire in the air is one call, flowing from what is
  offered to where it is used" (L2141). This is a fact from the code, not a
  proposal.
  3. **Six vocabularies for one relation:**
     - legend "Offers/Uses" (`template.html` L71-73)
     - 2D lace and gathered-wire names "X offers Y"
  (`views/localView/wire-gathering.ts` L96, L109)
     - 3D hover "X uses Y: n references" (`views/forceGraphView.ts` L2061)
     - card counts "Uses N files / Used by N files"
  (`views/localView/card-reading.ts` L223)
     - reader "Dependencies / Dependents" (`detailPanel.ts` L488, L497)
     - World Map "calls / called by / stands on" (`controller.ts` L1425, L1467)
  4. **"Wires revealed on hover", rejected by whom.** direction L185 and root
  (2026-11-12) say "rejected 2026-03-30 and 2026-04-01". The 03-30 rejection is
  Copilot's analysis, about Membrane Map browse mode. The owner's own words on
  2025-11-24 asked for hover-only lines and praised them. On 04-01 the owner
  objected to how the arcs looked, not to the principle. Hover-reveal is also
  kept in one place: a returning connection's route shows only on hover or focus
  (explorer L74).
  5. **"Looks like a mistake when zoomed out" is root's phrase.** It was quoted
  back to the owner as theirs: "You noted on November 13…" (2026-11-18 record,
  Turn 22 quoted message) and "Is this what you meant on November 13 by…" (Turn
  23, L3432). In `2026-11-13.1.record.md` L869 the phrase is root's, in root's
  reply.
  6. **The linked server's line.** direction L337 says which piece carries it
  "was asked and is not answered". direction L79 gives the owner's lean ("I
  would generally suspect the SQL Server piece?"). `Work/readers-on-main.md`
  L139 says "by their word of 2026-11-20".
  7. **Two senses of "left".** explorer L25: "one file with what it uses on the
  left and what uses it on the right" (where neighbours are placed). AGENTS.md
  L47: "uses enter on the left" (which edge of a card). They agree but use the
  word differently.
  8. **Naming in the data model.** The data model's `source` is the user and
  `target` is the provider (`wire-gathering.ts` L107-109). The owner on
  2025-12-07: "Very odd that we would call blue the target and green the
  source." (`2025-12-07.2.md` L1118). It is still so.
  9. **Stale line reference.** interaction L399 says 2D wires are `role="img"`
  at `connections.ts:614`. Today only gathered wires and returning laces carry a
  role; ordinary wires carry none.

  ## 7. What the code does today

  **2D map** (`packages/explorer/src/client/views/localView/connections.ts`):
  - All wires are drawn in one SVG overlay with `pointerEvents = "none"`
  (L134-140).
  - Every wire uses a `userSpaceOnUse` linear gradient from the provider's
  outbound pin to the consumer's inbound pin, with stops 0%/10% `--role-offers`
  and 90%/100% `--role-uses` (L285-309). Opacity is 0.7 (`styles/theme.css`
  L16).
  - There are no arrowheads, markers, animation or labels.
  - **Ordinary wires** have no role, no name, no pointer and no focus
  (L249-281).
  - **Gathered wires** get `role="img"` and an `aria-label` "X offers Y n
  references", with width by count, but no pointer or focus (L667-675).
  - **Returning laces** are filled polygons in amber (`--signal-cue`). They get
  `role="img"`, `tabindex="0"`, a `<title>` and an aria-label "X offers Y"
  (L365-401), and a hit band of 8 px (`styles/local.css` L594). Pointing at or
  focusing a lace lights both laces, both cards and the route. The route is
  hidden unless lit and is stroked amber (local.css L585, L590).
  - **Ports** (`.symbol-anchor` dots, blue on the right, green on the left) are
  `pointer-events: none` with no ARIA (`styles/view-shared.css` L142-151,
  L189-201; `card-factory.ts` L816, L846).
  - **Hovering a row or card** fades other wires to 0.1 at once, with no dwell
  (local.css L194-196, L578).
  - **Card foot:** a button "N connection(s) return in a cycle", titled "Show
  the files joined by connections that return against the layout's reading
  direction", opens a menu (`branch-renderer.ts` L1700-1735).
  - **Row chips:** "→" means a return type and "←" a parameter
  (`card-factory.ts` L979, L984).

  **World Map** (`views/worldMap/controller.ts`, `styles/world.css`):
  - Each wire has a gradient (0/0.1 offers, 0.9/1 uses) and a dashed `w-flow`
  path animated over 1.4 s from the provider's door to the consumer's door
  (controller L662-704; world.css L141-162). It stops only under reduced motion.
  - An invisible 16 px hit path shows the pointer cursor (world.css L132-139).
  - A rested hover shows a panel headed "A calls B" with file lines "from → to".
  A click pins it.
  - Door labels stay hidden until hover (L741).
  - Only pieces are keyboard stops (L503-512). Wires and doors are not.

  **3D map:**
  - Reference wires are white and carry no direction. Words appear only on a
  rested hover (`forceGraphView.ts` L2061).
  - Citation lines have an arrow at the cited end (explorer L252).

● I searched our whole record for what was already decided about showing which
  way a connection runs, and it turned up three things that change the picture.
  One is a question for you.

  1. The World Map's moving dashes run against the request

  On the estate's board, the panel for a wire says "sqlserver calls oracle", but
  the dashes on that wire travel from oracle to sqlserver. For your own demo, a
  payment's dashes run from the databases back towards the portal.

  - September 28: the dashes ran "the way the request goes", and you wrote "I
    absolutely adore the motion from outputs to inputs".
  - September 29: when the colours were swapped to "blue offers, green uses",
    the agent also turned every wire round and reported it in one line; you were
    not asked.

  I checked both in the record and in today's code.

  Question: on the World Map, should the dashes run with the request (portal →
  API → router → service → databases) or with the offering, as now? I recommend
  with the request, since that board is where a person follows one. The colours
  would not change. I'll have it built as a switch so you can see both.

  2. "Wires revealed on hover" was never your rejection

  Our notes said this was "off the table by the record". The source shows it was
  an agent's analysis in March, about one old layout where revealing wires made
  the whole picture shift. Your own words in November 2025 praised hover-only
  lines.

  It says nothing against a wire that is already drawn answering when you rest
  on it or press it. I corrected the note in direction.md
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Memory/direction.md).

  3. The fix for "which way does this line run" is partly a rule we already have

  On November 19 you ruled that words should wait for a pointer that rests. The
  World Map's wires already do this. The 2D map's wires do nothing: they cannot
  be pointed at, pressed or reached by keyboard.

  What I plan to build as one trial setting

  1. As now: colour and side only.
  2. A wire answers: rest on it, press it or tab to it, and it says one line
     where it is, such as "PaymentService.cs uses PaymentsContext.cs". This
     would also replace the six different wordings the screen uses today for the
     same relation.
  3. Chevrons along the wire, pointing from what is offered to what uses it (our
     direction).
  4. Chevrons the other way, as a dependency arrow points. This is against your
     left-to-right rule and is in the trial only to learn which habit strangers
     bring. Most developers read an arrow from A to B as "A uses B", the
     opposite of how our wires run, so a stronger arrow in our direction might
     make people more confidently wrong.
  5. Motion on what you point at: dashes flowing along the wires of the card
     under a rested pointer. This is your own wish from September 28, never
     built. Testers only see stills, so this one is for your eye.

  Arrowheads have never been shown to you on the 2D map; they were only ruled
  out for 3D (by you) and for the World Map (in an agent's brief).

  The full findings, with every quote and its location, are in the research note
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/line-direct
  ion/README.md). The alternatives are on the work item
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Work/stranger-follows-a-request.md).

  and get reviewed when that builder reports. It is noted in the record.

  Five builders are still out: the two on reader fixes, the pre-scan page, the
  test tool, and the edge counters.

  ## The rule
  `cutByEdge` now counts each card that is not wholly in view at one edge: the
  one whose far side reaches farthest past it on screen. On a tie it goes to the
  left or right edge, the edges a person reads a row of the map across; the
  comment says so. A press at an edge goes to the nearest of that edge's own
  cards. The control's name follows ("28 cards of what you opened past the
  view's right edge: show the nearest").

  ## Test before the change
  - **The `history` unit case**, written from the page's own 32 boxes, held 31 /
  27 / 4 / 0. I worked the partition out from those boxes before touching the
  function: left 0, right 28, top 1, bottom 2.
    - `shape.ts` and `bundled.ts` go to the bottom: 619 and 854 px past it,
  against 534 past the right.
    - `lens.ts` goes to the top: 1,669 above, against 1,537 past the right.
    - Every other cut card is farthest past the right.
    - The case also checks that the counts sum to the cards not wholly in view
  (31).
  - **Two small cases** for a card cut at a corner (it goes to the edge it is
  farther past) and for an exact tie (it goes to the right).
  - **Red, then green:** the three new cases failed on the old function and
  passed after the change.
  - **The browser case** now reads each control's count against the directory's
  cards by the same rule, and checks the sum.

  ## Pictures and what my eye sees
  In `Screenshots/step-framing/`, with the README brought level:
  - **`cut-counted--history--rest.png`: ↑1, →28, ↓2.** The numbers add up and
  still tell a person where to go: almost all of it is to the right.
    - What they no longer show is that most of those 28 also lie far above, in
  the L of `history`'s membrane. For 26 of them the right edge wins by only a
  few hundred pixels over the top.
  - **`cut-counted--history--after-press.png`, after pressing →28:** the view
  lands on `format.ts`, the nearest card past the right edge, whole at the lower
  right, with `summary.ts` and `history`'s label still in view. The place is
  kept.
    - The numbers become ↑6, →21, ↓3; they move between edges as the view moves.
    - A person who watched 28 become 21 may expect seven more cards in view;
  only one came.
  - **`cut-counted--history--after-press-above.png`, after pressing ↑1
  instead:** the one card counted above is `lens.ts`, so the press goes there
  whole, about 1,600 px right and 1,300 px up at the same scale.
    - It lands somewhere sensible: the head of the folder's main block, with
  `lens.ts`, `bundled.test.ts` and `rows.ts` whole. The counts are then →23, ↓7.
    - But it is a long jump with nothing kept: `history`'s label and

  | | dbatools | AL-Go |
  |---|---|---|
  | Wires | 7,479 / 7,478 / 7,478 | 493 / 492 / 492 |

  ### 1. A loop whose body sets its item
  pwsh 7.7.0 was run on each case with the modules missing, so its errors name
  what it looked for:

  | Case | pwsh looked for |
  |---|---|
  | Reviewer's case, `$m = "$m.Extra"` | ModE.Extra, ModF.Extra |
  | `$m += '.Plus'` | ModH.Plus |
  | A nested `foreach ($m in 'Inner')` | Inner |
  | `$_ = …` inside `ForEach-Object` | what it was set to |
  | `$PSItem = …` inside `ForEach-Object` | what it was set to (`$PSItem` and
  `$_` are one variable) |
  | `$m = 'Other'` after the load | ModG, ModG2 (the items) |
  | Assignment in an `if ($false)` | ModL (the item) |
  | The two plain loops | ModA, ModB; ModC, ModD |

  **The mend.** A loop body that sets its item is not read as a loop. That
  covers any assignment, `++` or `--`, a nested loop over the same name, and
  `$_`/`$PSItem` in `ForEach-Object`. It is applied in two places:
  - the reader (`setsItem`), for loops over written-out names and over
  `Get-ChildItem` listings;
  - the grader's parser script (`Test-SetsItem` in `powershell-ast.ps1`), so
  both sides stay alike.

  What the body loads is then read the way any variable is. `$_` assigned once
  before its single use keeps `ModJ.Changed`, which is pwsh's answer; the rest
  become notices.

  Where the body sets the item after the load, or in a branch that never runs,
  the reader gives a notice where pwsh imports the item. That is a missing name,
  never a wrong one, matching how a variable assigned more than once is
  treated.

  The test was red (ModE, ModF, ModH, ModI, ModK and ModG kept), then green.

  ### 2. The alias rule can now fail the oracle test
  - I added `Aliases/Helpers.ps1` to the `powershell-wires` fixture, defining
  `select` and `Get-ChildItem`. `Aliases/Use-Aliases.ps1` dot-sources it and
  runs `'x' | select` and `gci`.
  - pwsh printed `x`: Select-Object answered `select` ahead of the loaded
  function. It then printed "the fixture's Get-ChildItem": the loaded function
  answered `gci`.
  - I rewrote the parser record with `npm run oracle:index`. It gains only the
  two files and one wire to `Helpers.ps1`: the dot-source plus "calls
  Get-ChildItem".
  - **With the reader's mapping switched off, `powershell-oracle.test.ts` fails
  with:**
    - "PowerShell depends on Aliases/Helpers.ps1 (resolved), calling
  Get-ChildItem, the adapter does not"
    - "the adapter depends on Aliases/Helpers.ps1 (resolved), calling select,
  PowerShell does not"
  - With the mapping on, it passes.

  The account now has a line saying that Windows PowerShell 5.1's aliases
  (`curl`, `ls`, `sort`, …) winning over a loaded function of the same name is
  the Windows answer, which the engine promises.

  ### 3. `build` beside `build.ps1`: the platforms differ (fork for you)
  A general-purpose subagent of my tier read PowerShell/PowerShell at
  `ba2221aed027041b6620c5fe1c0675567f14452a` (MIT), fetched anonymously and read
  as text. It also read dotnet/runtime at
  `8815a4ee8bd143180de3a717af9ca05be3ea9e56` for how .NET matches file names.
  Paths below are under `src/System.Management.Automation/`.

  **Relative path (`./tools/build` or `tools/build`).** It isn't treated as
  qualified (`LocationGlobber.cs` 1393–1401, 1491–1499 exclude `./` and `.\`),
  so the literal path isn't tried first (`CommandSearcher.cs` 285–289, 303–308).
  Discovery searches the folder instead, using the names from
  `ConstructSearchPatternsFromName` (1322–1363): `build.ps1`, then on Windows
  only (`#if !UNIX`, 1346–1354) `build` with each `PATHEXT` extension, then
  `build`. What is listed on disk differs by platform (`CommandPathSearch.cs`
  57–67):
  - **Windows** lists one pattern, `build.*`. .NET's matching lets it match the
  bare `build` too (`FileSystemName.cs` 35–37, 139, 314–318), and results come
  back in the order of the names above (499–509). So Windows runs
  **`build.ps1`**.
  - **Unix** lists `build` first, then `build.ps1`. So Unix runs **`build`**.
  pwsh here agrees: `Get-Command b/build` gave `b/build`.

  **Rooted path (`& "$PSScriptRoot/tools/build"`, `C:\`, `/`, `~`).** The
  literal is tried first on both platforms (`CommandSearcher.cs` 458–465, under
  `ResolveLiteralThenPathPatterns`, which `CommandDiscovery.cs` 653 always
  sets). Both platforms run **`build`**.

  `&`, `.` and a bare path all take the same route (`MiscOps.cs` 38–39, 153;
  `ExecutionContext.cs` 640–642); the dot only changes scope.

  This is a reading of the code, not a run on Windows. The two points that rest
  on .NET were read in dotnet/runtime `main`, not in the runtime PowerShell
  ships with. Your memory of `CommandSearcher` holds for rooted paths only.

  **What the reader draws:**
  - **Today:** `tools/build` if that file stands, else `tools/build.ps1`. That
  is Unix's answer for a relative path, and both platforms' answer for a rooted
  one.
  - **Under Windows' answer:** `tools/build.ps1` for a relative path when both
  files stand.

  The reader keeps `$PSScriptRoot` as written until it resolves, so it can tell
  the two shapes apart once you decide.

  **In the corpora:** none of dbatools, AL-Go, AzureStack-Tools or tSQLt has an
  extension-less file beside a `.ps1` of the same name, and no load names a
  `.ps1` path without its extension. The two hits a search found were help text
  naming UNC paths.

  ### Part 1: what changed in the instrument, and how it was checked
  **`playtest.mjs`:**
  - `--page <file>[?query]`, or a task's `page` (path from the repository root)
  plus `query`, serves that one file read-only at `http://127.0.0.1:8793/`. The
  address the tester sees names no folder of this repository, and the window's
  block on other origins holds as before. The Explorer is not served for such a
  run.
  - A task's `handFolder` (`{input, ready}`) with `--folder <dir>`, or `--folder
  --input --ready` on the command line, hands that folder to the page's file
  input before the first look.
  - `--press <selector>` (repeatable, or a task's `press`) presses a control
  during setup.
  - `run.json` records the page, query, folder and presses, and its time stamps
  become the page file's.

  **`browser-window.mjs`:**
  - `--hand-folder`, `--hand-input`, `--ready`: it calls `setInputFiles`, waits
  until the ready element is shown with text, then waits for the page to rest.
  - `--press`: presses each control in setup.
  - `setup.json` records the folder's name, the ready text, how long the
  hand-over took, and each press. Any failure ends the run, like any failed
  setup.

  **Two faults found in `leaveOut.ts`, each mended after a failing test (92 → 94
  tests pass):**
  1. **Keep on a merged folder did not keep its contents.**
     - A `.tfignore` entry matches any folder of a path, as TFVC does. So
  `!/…/GoogleAnalytics/packages/` kept the folder, but its 80 package folders
  and its `repositories.config` came back as "Ignored by .tfignore: packages".
     - Now a person's `!` on a folder takes away an entry's match on that
  folder's name, or a name above it, for what it holds.
     - Seen after a Keep on that folder: files read go from 10,929 to 12,610,
  1,681 of its 1,761. The other 80 are `.nupkg` files, which the same
  `.tfignore`'s `*.nupkg` line names directly; they now show as their own row.
  NuGet packages drops from 46 to 45 folders, and the also-line from 15 of 46 to
  14 of 45.
     - **A fork you can reverse:** this goes past what TFVC itself does, on the
  grounds that a person's Keep of a folder means its contents.
  2. **False notes about `!` lines.** On DNN's nested `.gitignore` files, seven
  notes said lines like `!DNN [Pp]latform/[Cc]omponents` "cannot keep anything
  in …/bin/ or 176 more folders". The function read a pattern only as far as its
  first `[`. It now compares the pattern with the folder part by part, as git
  would match it.

  ### How the testers coped with the twelve-result cap
  - **The Sonnet testers** worked round the cap one letter at a time: "The
  search shows only 12 results at a time, so I searched by first letter to get
  past that cap"; "The explorer has no folder listing". That took 46–60 actions,
  at $0.20–$0.27 a run, and two of the three then miscounted the files they had
  listed.
  - **The Haiku testers who stayed with the search** answered with the first
  twelve.
  - **The one right answer came by accident.** Haiku 2 pressed Enter in the
  search and arrived on `bundled.test.ts`, which opened `history` around it. It
  then said "The dropdown is capped at 12 entries, so I'll read the folder from
  the graph instead". It zoomed out and panned until the folder stood whole, and
  read the 32 names off the cards

  was an arrival, not a step, so no counter was drawn. Along the way it also
  pinned two things by accident and cleared them.

  effect cannot be separated from the task's.

  search first. The search answers with a capped list and offers no way into the
  folder. The only tester who saw the folder whole got there by an arrival it
  had not meant.

  | Repository | Attribute, spelling | Where its file is | Count |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | eShopOnWeb (one app, `Sdk.Web`, `wwwroot`) | `src ~/` | the project's
  `wwwroot` | 2 |
  | | `src ~/` | nowhere: `wwwroot/lib`, restored by LibMan, git-ignored | 6 |
  | | `asp-fallback-src ~/` | same | 6 |
  | | `src`, relative | nowhere: `_content/…`, `_framework/…`, served from
  packages | 3 |
  | | remote | not a path | 6 |
  | nopCommerce (`Nop.Web` `Sdk.Web` + `wwwroot`; plugins `Microsoft.NET.Sdk`,
  built into `Nop.Web/Plugins/{X}` by their `OutputPath`) | `src ~/` | the app's
  `wwwroot` | 81 |
  | | `src ~/Plugins/{X}/…` | the plugin project whose `OutputPath` names it | 4
  |
  | | `src ~/` | nowhere (`lib_npm/fine-uploader`, not shipped) | 1 |
  | | Razor expression | not a path | 14 |
  | | remote | not a path | 1 |
  | Orchard Core (modules `Sdk.Razor` with `wwwroot`; 365 scripts named only by
  `asp-name`) | `asp-src ~/{module}/…` | that module's `wwwroot` | 249 |
  | | `src ~/{module}/…` | same | 3 |
  | | `asp-src ~/` | nowhere | 2 |
  | | `asp-src`, relative | nowhere (the demo module's sample names) | 8 |
  | | remote | not a path | 4 |

  No source in the three is spelled from the site's root.

  **The rule I chose:** a web project whose project file names the SDK
  `Microsoft.NET.Sdk.Web` and has a `wwwroot` beside it serves that `wwwroot`.
  The evidence is the files: the new `projectSdk` in `project.ts`, and a
  `wwwroot` in the index.
  - Its views read `~/` and `/` from that folder alone.
  - A module's or plugin's view reads `~/` from the web roots of the
  applications it may be deployed into.
  - Classic projects are unchanged.
  - I also decided one small fork myself: Razor code anywhere in a value
  (`summernote-@(language).min.js`) counts as computed, so it is not read.

  **Root-relative `/js/site.js` in a Core view:** today it gets the "paths from
  the site's root" notice; now it is read from the web root. No corpus holds
  one.

  **Before and after, file by file:**

  | Corpus | Lines gained | Notices |
  |---|---|---|
  | eShopOnWeb | +2 | 11 → 9 |
  | nopCommerce | +81 | 87 → 5 (one fewer is the Razor expression) |
  | Orchard Core | none | unchanged |
  | DNN, mojoPortal | none | unchanged |

  **What it gets wrong, and needs your call (not built):**
  - Orchard Core's 252 module scripts and nopCommerce's 4 plugin scripts stay
  unlinked with a notice, though their files are in the repository. Each product
  maps those paths by its own convention: Orchard's `~/{module}/` to the
  module's `wwwroot`, nopCommerce's `~/Plugins/{X}/` through the build's
  `OutputPath`. Neither is ASP.NET's rule.
  - The script pattern reads Orchard's `asp-src` as it reads `src`, because it
  matches either attribute.
  - Two Core shapes go unrecognised, and none of the three repositories holds
  either:
    - a project that moves its web root in code;
    - a Blazor WebAssembly project (`Sdk.BlazorWebAssembly`).
  - The ASP.NET Core sample folders `razor-appsettings` and `blazor-telemetry`
  (under `tests/integration/fixtures/`) have no project file, so they still read
  as before.

  ### The C# grader's corpus figures beside the present ones

  0, other 35), compiler-only 1,276. Unchanged.
  - **DNN:** 20,971 / 24,837 / 20,392, ours-only 579 (namesake 2, conditional 0,
  other 577), compiler-only 4,445. Unchanged.

● Strangers have tried the pre-scan page, and the result is clear enough that
  I've drawn one design from it; a builder is making it now for your eye.

  Forty blind runs on the two layouts

  Each tester got one of four tasks on a 17,244-file folder shaped like yours.
  The answers were written down before any run.

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │             Task             │         By reason          │  Folder    │
  │                              │                            │    tree    │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Say what is left out and why │ 4 of 4 right, in 1 to 4    │ 0 of 4     │
  │                              │ actions                    │ right      │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Keep one named packages      │ 4 of 4                     │ 4 of 4     │
  │ folder                       │                            │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Leave one named source       │ 4 of 4                     │ 4 of 4     │
  │ folder out                   │                            │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Say why one named bin is     │ 4 of 4                     │ 4 of 4     │
  │ left out                     │                            │            │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  - The tree cannot answer "what is left out and why". Its rows count folders,
    never files, and lump build output and packages into one phrase (picture
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-play
    tests/leave-out-read-folders-sonnet-medium/shots/002-click.png)).
  - The two kinds of checkbox made no difference where a box was acted on.
  - The small plus for "keep this folder" was found every time (picture
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/blind-play
    tests/leave-out-keep-reason-sonnet-medium/shots/009-scroll.png)).
  - One control was misread by every tester: the switch "Leave out build output
    and packages". They took it for the reason things are left out. One unticked
    it to keep a single folder and got the only wrong answer.
  - To leave a folder out in By reason, every tester typed its path as a
    pattern. They all got it right, but these testers are models and type paths
    readily; a person may not.

  The design I drew from it

  One screen, not two layouts:

  1. By reason is the screen. This is overview first, details on demand: three
     or four rows say everything, and each opens only when asked.
  2. Leaving out by pointing moves into its right-hand column. Each folder and
     each file kind under "Will be read" gets a quiet minus, the opposite of the
     plus on the left. So "don't read the 1,325 .png files" or "skip the Tests
     folder" is one press, with no pattern to type.
  3. The misread switch goes. The Keep button on the "Build output" and "NuGet
     packages" rows already does its job.

  The tree stays reachable by a link parameter so you can still compare. Your
  eye decides; I'll show pictures when the builder reports.

  Also running

  - The history rebuild on the readers' branch; the machine is heavily loaded
    while it runs.
  - The line-direction trial.
  - The search fix.

  ## Search-folder mend: handback

  **Commit:** `4ccdddd3b` on branch `worktree-agent-af343511ff46a497d`, a single
  commit on top of `824fab0ad`. Nothing was pushed or amended, and no command
  was refused. Worktree:
  `/workspaces/Live-Documentation/.claude/worktrees/agent-af343511ff46a497d`.

  The browser verification is incomplete. Read the third section before
  accepting this round.

  ### Your reading, corrected in one place
  - You were right that a directory was scored by its last segment only.
  - You were right that `FILE_LIMIT = 12` cut the list with no sign.
  - `DIRECTORY_LIMIT = 6` also cut directories silently.
  - You were not right about symbols: they were never cut silently. Their cap of
  5 already showed a fold row reading "n more symbols".

  ### Matching rule (`directoryScore` in `graph-search.ts`)
  - **Word without a slash:** scored against the directory's name, as before.
  - **Word with a slash:** it names a directory when its segments equal the
  directory's last segments.
    - Every segment must match whole, except the last.
    - The last segment matches by its start, since it may still be being typed.
  A closing slash ends it, so it must then match whole.
  - **Scores:**
    - Whole last segment: 100. Begun last segment: 50. These are the same
  weights a name gets.
    - Typing the whole path from the top adds 10, so the directory named most
  exactly stands first.
  - **Examples:**
    - `live-docs/history`, `live-docs/hist`, `history/` and the full path all
  offer the folder.
    - `docs/history` and `engine/history` offer nothing.
  - **Why whole segments:** a substring rule would let `docs/history` name
  `live-docs/history`. A bare word has never matched part of a segment's middle
  by prefix either.

  ### The cut, said
  - **Pure module:**
    - A group now carries `moreFiles` (files of its directory left out).
    - The result carries `elsewhere` (left-out files whose directory has no
  group) and `moreDirectories`.
    - New options: `expandedDirectories`, `elsewhere` (how many more files to
  list from directories that had no group, best first) and `directoryLimit`.
    - `searchRows` now takes the whole result.
  - **On screen, every fold is the same control:**
    - A small pill: a down arrow (the map's cut-sign arrow) and the number.
    - At the list's end only, a file or folder glyph, because there the place
  does not say what is counted.
    - No visible words. The words are the row's `aria-label` and tooltip, from
  `foldName`, e.g. "21 more files in packages/engine/src/live-docs/history: show
  them".
  - **Where each fold stands and what a press does:**
    - Under a group: the press lists all of that directory's matches in place.
    - At the end, files elsewhere: the press lists the next 12.
    - At the end, directories: the press offers the next 6.
    - Under a file, symbols: the same form as before, but no longer italic
  words.
  - **Keyboard and list:**
    - Each fold is an `option` in the listbox. Arrows, Enter and Escape are
  unchanged.
    - After a press, the keyboard's row moves to the first row the press
  brought.
    - Press state belongs to the current words (reset on input and on settle)
  and is kept separately for the offered and the apart sections.
  - `FILE_ICON` now lives in `panels/icon-button.ts`, and `pin-tray.ts` uses it
  instead of its inline copy.

  ### What the list shows now
  - **`live-docs/history`:**
    - First row: the folder (`PACKAGES/ENGINE/SRC/LIVE-DOCS/HISTORY`, DIRECTORY
  badge).
    - Under it, 11 of its files and "⌄ 21".
    - Then the JSON fixture's group.
    - At the end, "⌄ 9" with a file glyph.
    - Before the mend: no folder row, and the JSON fixture's group stood first.
  - **Whole path:** the folder row, 12 files and "⌄ 20". Nothing else.
  - **`history`:**
    - The top rows are in the same order as before.
    - Each group whose directory holds more matches carries its count: ⌄1, ⌄23,
  ⌄9, ⌄1, ⌄7.
    - The list ends with "⌄ 7" plus a file glyph.

  ### Fork decided
  - I gave the symbols' fold the same arrow-and-number form, so the list has one
  sign for hidden rows and less text of ours.
  - `search-bar.spec.ts` was updated to match: it now checks the count and the
  accessible name.
  - It is easy to revert if you or the owner prefer the old words.

  ## Line-direction trial: handback

  **Commit:** `fa4cafe00` on `worktree-agent-a2e1671885ba40563`, on top of
  `day/2026-11-21` at `bd72b6ecb`. It is one commit holding both levers, because
  both run through `attendWire` in `connections.ts`. It is not pushed or
  merged. My scratch folder is removed.

  Both levers sit in Tuning → 2D Wires, are off by default and are carried by a
  link (`layout-presets.ts`). The Tuning control count goes from **74 to 76**,
  in `tuning.spec.ts` and `tuning-levers.test.ts`.

  ### Wire Answers
  - **Files:** `views/localView/wire-answers.ts` (page side), `wire-words.ts`
  (pure words), `connections.ts` (`attendWire` draws the bands).
  - **Bands:** each wire gets an undrawn stroke as its band. It is 12 screen px
  wide at every zoom, or the wire's own width plus 6 px if wider. It takes the
  pointer and focus (`tabindex=-1`, never a Tab stop) and carries the spoken
  name.
  - **Rest:** uses the existing `pointerRest.ts`, not a second rest rule.
  - **Fade:** reuses `lightConnections`, the same one a returning lace uses. The
  wire's own rows and its two cards stay; everything else fades; nothing is
  raised.
  - **Press and Escape:** a press pins the words, using the World Map's pinned
  look (blue left border, close icon). Escape or a press elsewhere lets go. A
  press on a band gives it no focus.
  - **Drag:** a drag that starts on a wire still pans; a browser case proves it.
  - **Placement:** the panel side uses the World Map's `panelBeside`, with the
  wire's two cards weighted four times. My first pictures placed it at the
  pointer, where it hid the card it named.
  - **Gathered wires:** the visible wire loses its "X offers Y n references"
  name, and the band carries the new sentence instead.
  - **Pinned fade after another hover:** when a row hover ended it used to put
  out the pinned wire's fade. It now relights from the controller's
  `clearSymbolHighlight`.

  **What a wire says** (panel text; the spoken name joins it into one sentence):

  | Wire | Head line | Detail line | Spoken name |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Estate pair line | `PaymentsContext.cs uses App.config` | `PaymentsDb · 1
  reference` | "PaymentsContext.cs uses App.config: PaymentsDb, 1 reference" |
  | Repo reference | `pathfind.ts uses graph.ts` | `LiveDocGraph · 1 reference`
  | same pattern |
  | Bundle run ("gathered run") | `graph.ts is used by pathfind.test.ts,
  sources-facts.ts, emit.ts +3` | `LiveDocGraph · 20 references` | same pattern
  |

  - The only words of ours are "uses", "is used by" and "references". The rest
  of a list is counted as "+N".
  - References are weighed as the layout weighs them: `gathered ??
  referenceCount ?? 1`.
  - I read "a gathered run" as a bundle's shared run, since `branch-order`
  bundles the wires of one offering pin. A pair line or the overview's gathered
  wire says "X uses Y" with its symbols.

  **Keyboard model (built):**
  - On a focused card, → goes to the first wire leaving it on the right (to what
  uses it) and ← to the first wire entering on the left (what it uses).
  - On a wire, ↑/↓ move to the card's next or previous wire on that side, and
  →/← go on to the card at that end.
  - Enter or Space pins the words. Escape first lets go of a pin, then returns
  to the card the walk began from.
  - Reachable by keyboard: every wire attached to a card that has focus.
  - Not reachable: a bundle run as a whole (its member wires are), and a wire
  whose cards are out of view. The camera does not follow a focused wire, though
  its panel is clamped into view.

  ### Direction Marks
  - **Files:** `wire-marks.ts`. Values are None, Along and Against, with open
  chevrons written into the wire's own path, so they take its gradient colour at
  their point, its opacity and its fading.
  - **Along** points from what is offered to what uses it. **Against** goes the
  other way. Its help says "a trial, not our rule".
  - **Density chosen:** one chevron at the wire's middle; on a wire longer than
  640 px, one per roughly 320 px stretch. None within 28 px of an end, none on
  laces or bundle runs.
  - **Long-task bug (fixed):** the first build measured each wire through the
  page (`getTotalLength`/`getPointAtLength`). Opening `views/localView` (498
  wires) then held the page in a single **137-second** task during the move. The
  marks now walk the path's own commands in pure code, and the same opening
  settles.

  ### Pictures
  In `AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Screenshots/line
  -direction/`, with `README.md` and `shots.mjs`:
  - the estate `Data` step at each mark value, and with answers on;
  - a wire rested and a wire pinned (estate and repo);
  - the bundle run's answer;
  - the dense repo view at each value, plus 2× crops of the "strings" and of the
  bundle "trunk".

  What my eye sees:
  - **Rest:** only the rested wire keeps its look, its two cards stay unfaded,
  and the panel clears both names.
  - **Marks at a wire's middle:** they stand on the steep part of the S-curve,
  so most point up or down. They tell the way along the line, but not the
  left-to-right reading at a glance.
  - **Noise, bundles:** every member wire draws its own chevrons on the shared
  run, giving "»»»»" stacks and a pile on the riser. The mend I'd make next is
  to mark the run once.
  - **Noise, parallel wires:** on the "guitar strings" the chevrons become a
  busy field of arrows. Their direction is clear, but it's noisy.
  - **Run panel placement:** it covers part of `graph.ts`'s card, which is cut
  at the left edge.
  - **Unsettled:** in the repo rest pictures, one curve from `graph.ts`'s
  `LiveDocGraph` pin up toward the top edge stays bright while the rest fade. It
  is either a second wire with the same symbols or stacked bundle members.

  ### Frame cost of panning
  Measured on `views/localView` opened from `controller.ts`: 86 cards, 498 wire
  paths, 409 bands. Three drags of 120 moves per setting. Ranges are across the
  three drags.

  | Setting | Median frame | 90th percentile frame | Script + style + layout per
  frame |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Both levers off | 16.7 ms | 16.8 ms (all three) | 1.76–2.78 ms |
  | Wire Answers on | 16.7 ms | 33.3 ms in 2 of 3 drags | 2.05–2.97 ms |
  | Direction Marks on | 16.7 ms | 33.3 ms in 2 of 3 drags | 3.17–3.81 ms |
  | Both on | 16.7 ms | 16.8 ms (all three) | 1.56–2.73 ms |

  These are within the noise of a shared machine: both on measured lower than
  either alone. I can't show a panning cost for either lever. I did not take a
  marks-off probe of the move to separate the page's own load from it.

  ### Verification
  - **Build and checks:** build, the client type-check and eslint on everything
  touched are clean.
  - **Unit tests:** explorer suite 1287 passed. One failure, in
  `forceGraphOctree.test.ts`, passed when re-run alone.
  - **Docs and audits:** Live Docs are regenerated and linted, with authored
  Purpose and Notes for the six new docs. `docs:links:enforce` and the three
  slopcop audits are clean.
  - **Browser, `wire-answers.spec.ts`: 11 of 11 pass.** It covers:
    - lever off: no bands;
    - a pointer passing over draws and fades nothing, then rest fades the others
  and shows the words, and leaving clears them;
    - a press pins, Escape lets go, and a press elsewhere lets go;

❯ I'm working my way from old messages to new ones trying my best to reply to
  what I see.

  >Commented-out project reference: it is read as real, which then widens what
  C# names can reach.

  That's a total tee-hee! We've avoided capturing comments for a long long
  time, no? How did that lapse? XD

  >"Test" for every language: a change made on PowerShell's evidence altered
  what counts as a test everywhere, so test-utils.tsx stopped being one. I've
  confined it to PowerShell files.

  Where else do you see builders making regressions like this? Should any
  architecture be in place to scope test file matching patterns in some way?

  >Arrival, tried on strangers (20 runs)

  Yep, these results undoubtedly indicate that arriving on the whole
  neighborhood allows fuller intuition in fewer actions on these "strangers"
  who reason over screenshots. (Hopefully you attempted to account for the
  incredibly severe bias that all LLMs have which favors _more_ text on the
  screen in nearly all scenarios). But what I had described was an automatic
  zoom-out from the single focused pinned item to its neighborhood, ensuring
  that the user knew the full detail available _and_ that the user started with
  less outer visual context and _grew into it_ automatically. Still few
  actions -- the actions are just taken automatically. However, we attempted to
  build this at least once and found that, due to jarring performance hits
  caused by breakpoint transitions, the
  start-framing-only-one-card-then-zoom-to-neighborhood approach failed on
  complex scenes (for very very simple scenes it was honestly not bad at all;
  the perf hit and crossing of breakpoints is what was jarring and broke that
  for us).

  >Nobody read the drawing. Every tester handed the whole neighbourhood went
  straight to the reader's list of dependents.

  As in all went to the detail panel? All went to the modal text representation
  of the card? This is ambiguous to me.

  >The first hard evidence on your architecture question is in: the engine
  already maps code identically in a browser page and under Node, for almost
  everything we have.

  Awesome!

  >Drift now shows up. The bundle refuses to build if the engine imports a Node
  module that has no browser replacement, and a committed list of what the
  scan needs from Node changes visibly in review.

  How do we detect this? In addition, if a browser offers something, do we
  _need_ a node module for that something? Put another way: wherever you see
  yourself reaching for two implementations to maintain for the same problem,
  ask whether the right architectural choices have been made. Maintaining two
  parallel implementations of the nitty-gritty guts sounds like an
  architecturally poor choice and a good way to cause drift no matter what.

  >What cannot cross into a page: anything that runs git. The history is
  computed by the command line and reaches the page as facts.

  Fair. I can understand why that might be unavailable for the pure browser
  version, but do ponder whether there is some way we can have our cake and eat
  it too. Git is just a hidden directory of files. Complex as can be, but we
  can read files from the browser...

  >Alternatives and unknowns: section 7 sets it beside three alternatives.
  Section 8 lists what would overturn it, chiefly scan time and memory at your
  scale on your VM, and whether your company's browser policy allows the folder
  picker.

  I work in web dev. My two git repos are actually WEBFORMS!!!! Can you believe
  it?! I know, old tech for sure! We don't use any of the stupid webforms
  postback crap or OnClick crap, we just use it as a simple way to have C# do a
  bit of preformatting on simple pages, and then we let on-page fetches handle
  the rest of being dynamic. They're super vanilla frontends -- no funny JS
  frameworks or fancy sass stylings -- just aspx, js, and css (with bootstrap
  for simple components). But anyway, I have free reign on what the browser can
  do _inside_ my dev machine. I necessarily need to have that ability for my
  specific work.

  >The format research: first numbers
  >SQLite is a real candidate again. It was dropped in January mainly because
  its native module broke on Windows. Node now ships SQLite built in, so that
  reason has expired.

  Awesome! But does can it work in the browser? Otherwise the "compact binary
  layout of our own" seems like the better of the candidates in that table.

  >Two of our C# graders shared the reader's blind spots. One reads project
  references by the same text pattern the reader uses, so it agreed with the
  reader's mistake on a commented-out reference. The other cannot tell Helper
  from Helper<T>. Both will now take their answers from Microsoft's build
  system and the C# compiler directly.

  Not while the browser-based software is running though, right? We don't have
  any ability to do anything like that right? (And I think we generally
  wouldn't want to -- a build can _do things_ depending on the framework or
  language you're working in -- the wasm tree-sitter was brought in to help
  avoid this)

  >Started: choosing what a scan leaves out
  >For .gitignore, git is the judge. The function must match git's own answer,
  rule for rule, on generated cases and on real repositories.
  >For .tfignore, Microsoft's documentation leaves three rules unstated. The
  builder is checking whether Microsoft's open-source TFS client answers them
  in its code. Whatever cannot be established stays marked unknown.
  >It will also produce the listing you would see on a folder built to your
  shape (over 100k files, mostly packages), with counts by folder and by rule.
  I will draw the choice screen from that.

  I think that if `.gitignore` and `.tfignore` have very similar conventions
  (they do), then what we'd really be doing is -- after a user has pointed to a
  directory for scanning -- indicate that an ignore file was found, and then
  ask the user if they want to use (replace current ignore pattern in browser
  window), integrate (add to current ignore pattern in browser window), or
  ignore (make no change to current ignore pattern in browser window) the
  detected ignore file for that scan. The ignore pattern in the browser window
  must be easy to reuse, come with good available defaults (i.e. the standard
  Visual Studio gitignore) to optionally prefill with, and work functionally
  the same as a gitignore would. The list the user sees for ignore patterns is
  the source of truth for our software, and we can take that in from other
  ignore patterns as needed.

  >Your idea from the 19th, arriving at reading size and then pulling back,
  could not be tested this way, because the testers only see the picture after
  it settles. I have left Reading Size as the default.

  Which unfortunately makes the test not an excellent test. You've learned
  important information, but only important information that can be gleaned
  from _one_ thorough inspection of an image. The human operating the software
  will exercise a different attention pattern: they will inspect the images
  with almost _no_ thoroughness at all, but they will _navigate_ and _move_ and
  _change_ and _integrate_ over many many pictures. I think your "action
  counts" metric is perhaps more useful as a proxy? Even then there is massive
  bias to account for in the LLM playtesting protocol.

  >Commented-out XML is no longer read as real.

  Phew!

  >SQLite is the best on the command line and the worst in a page. Read from
  disk by Node it gives the fastest first answer of any format, in the least
  memory. Written inside a page at 50k it takes 854 MB and over two minutes.
  Since you want both surfaces from one design, I count it out.

  Dang! Good to know. Wow the compact binary of our own is looking better and
  better.

  >Binary as the stored form, text exported on demand (my lean). Fastest and
  lightest on a weak machine. You run one command or press one button when you
  want something to grep.

  Agreed. Yeah looks like this will have to be our lean.

  >A page that keeps its scan, and saves itself

  I personally lean towards the "copy of itself" for portability purposes

  >A question for you, not urgent: in a git repository this can leave out files

● Answers to each point, in your order. Four pieces of work follow from them;

  Readers

  - How the commented-out reference lapsed. The readers that parse a syntax tree
    have always skipped comments. The project-file reader never had a tree:
    since 2026-09-28 it has run one text pattern over the raw .csproj. Its
    grader used the same kind of pattern, so both agreed. Today's review found
    it by trying it, and its second pass found the same fault in ASP.NET markup.
    Both are fixed, but nothing yet guards every pattern-based reader; I'm
    adding that test.
  - "Test" for every language. archetype.ts
    (packages/engine/src/live-docs/archetype.ts#L104-L116) holds one list of
    every language's naming rules and applies it to every file. The C# rule
    FooTests.cs therefore also catches a TypeScript runTests.ts. Today's fix is
    an if (powershell) inside that list, which is a patch. The architecture I'm
    building: each language's reader declares what its own test runner treats as
    a test, applied to that language's files only and measured against the
    runner's own discovery. One table test across all languages makes a change
    for one that moves another fail.
  - Where else today. The same shape, shared code changed on one case's
    evidence, happened four more times:
    - The readers' join removed the premise of four browser tests; only the full
      suite caught it.
    - A larger graph crossed the 3D budget and veiled the main group; the suite
      caught it.
    - The scanning-page probe broke three times in two days; nothing caught it
      until today's check.
    - The World Map dashes were turned round in September; nothing caught it for
      two months.
  - Graders and builds. Correct. MSBuild and the compiler run only on our bench,
    by hand, to produce answer keys we keep as records. The product never
    builds, restores or runs the code it maps; it only reads text, on either
    surface.
  - Your Windows run settles it. ./build ran build.ps1, as PowerShell's source
    said and against my memory. The reader will follow Windows.

  Explorer

  - Arrival. The bias is only partly accounted for. We count what testers do,
    not what they wish for, but a model reads a dense still completely at no
    cost, and a person does not. What you described is the "Reading, Then
    Neighbours" value, which is in Tuning now. The testers cannot see its
    motion, and I have not measured that motion on a complex scene. I'm having
    it measured on this repository, with the usual cure as an alternative for
    your eye: hold the cards' detail level while the camera moves and change it
    once at rest. Designers call the jolt you saw "level-of-detail popping".
  - "The reader's list". That was my loose word for the Detail Panel, the side
    panel that opens when you click a file's name. Its Dependents section lists
    in words what uses the file. All twelve testers answered from there, so
    those runs say nothing about whether the drawing is understood.
  - The test's limits. Agreed. From now these runs report actions and routes
    first and are used to find stumbles. They will not choose between two looks
    or judge motion; those go to your eye.
  - 12 of 32. No, it was a fixed cap of twelve rows with no sign; as far as I
    can read the code, search ignores the kind filters. It is fixed on main: the
    folder has its own row, and a cut list shows a count that opens the rest. I
    have not checked what happens when you choose a result the filters hide.

  One engine, two surfaces

  - How drift is detected. A test bundles the engine for a browser and fails on
    any Node import that has no stand-in. A script also derives which Node
    functions each engine file uses and compares that with a committed list
    (tests/page-scan/node-needs.json), so a new dependence shows as a changed
    line in review.
  - Two implementations. You are right, and today's check does contain them:
    eleven stand-ins, including a rewrite of Node's path functions. They are
    scaffolding that counts what must go. In the design
    (.mdmd/layer-3/engine-and-hosts.mdmd.md) the engine imports nothing from
    Node. Paths become one small module of its own, and anything a browser
    offers is used through the web-standard API that Node also has. Only three
    functions are written twice: list a folder, read a file, read a file's first
    bytes.
  - Git in a page. I think it can be done. A repository's history is compressed
    files under .git, and a browser has both primitives built in (inflate and
    SHA-1). One reader of .git inside the engine would serve both surfaces, so
    the command line would stop needing the git program. Not known: whether the
    folder picker hands over .git, and the time and memory in a tab. I'm
    starting a probe with git as its oracle. TFS keeps no history on disk, so it
    has none on either surface.
  - WebForms and browser freedom. That closes the folder-picker unknown. DNN and
    mojoPortal, the two .NET codebases we grade on, are WebForms applications,
    so the shape is already measured. I have not checked whether we draw a
    page's fetch to the server method that answers it.
  - SQLite in a browser. It runs there, as WebAssembly. The page must hold the
    whole database in memory, which is where the 854 MB came from.
  - Stored form. Recorded as the direction: binary as the stored form, text
    exported on demand, and the page saving a copy of itself as the portable
    thing. Browser storage is only what lets a scan survive a closed tab.

  Ignore patterns

  I'm adopting your model, and it is simpler than what I built.

  - One list decides. It has .gitignore syntax and behaviour. My five layers of
    precedence collapse into it, plus two fixed things never scanned: the
    version-control folder and our own output.
  - Found ignore files are offered: Use, Add or Ignore. Two measured wrinkles
    for the import:
    - A folder can hold many ignore files at different depths, so each line is
      rewritten with its folder in front.
    - .tfignore differs in three small ways, so it is translated on import.
  - Defaults are optional prefills. One is the Visual Studio list. I'd add a
    second, generated from what the folders hold (a bin beside a project file).
    The preview matters here: the Visual Studio list leaves out 60 real source
    files of DNN.
  - Nothing is applied silently. This reverses my morning decision that the
    build-output rules apply by default.
  - The preview stays. What each line leaves out, with counts, updates as the
    list changes.

  First milestone of the eighth leg: the function now decides by one list, both

  The harness refused two commands: one chained a `git rm`, the other chained

  **Commit 1, engine only: `9d7bfdaf9`.** Files that moved:
  - `packages/engine/src/tooling/leaveOut.ts`
  - `leaveOut.test.ts`, `leaveOut.oracle.test.ts`, `leaveOut.tfvc.test.ts` (same
  folder)
  - their four Live Docs under `.mdmd/layer-4/packages/engine/src/tooling/`
  (regenerated, with Purpose and Notes rewritten)

  Nothing else under `packages/` changed, and I did not run the history's
  rebuild. Only the leave-out tests and the probes import the function.

  What the function is now:
  - `leaveOut({ list, ignoreCase, ownOutput })` reads the list as one
  `.gitignore` at the top and names the deciding line (number and text). A
  version control's folder and the tool's own output stay outside the list.
  - `fromGitignores(files, { folders, ignoreCase })` puts a nested file's folder
  in front of each line (a name alone becomes `sub/**/name`) and orders files
  shallowest first, so last-match-wins gives git's answer.
  - `fromTfignores(files)`: `\` becomes `/`; a leading separator anchors; folder
  parts are written literally. Where any file has a `!`, each exclusion is
  followed by `X/**`, because TFVC leaves out what lies beneath a folder even
  when a `!` keeps the folder itself.
  - Both translators return each line's origin (file and line) and what they
  cannot carry.
  - `proposeLines(folders, names, ignoreCase)` turns the evidence rules into
  proposals. A bare name like `bin/` is proposed only where it leaves out
  nothing the exact lines would keep.
  - The layered decider and the person/evidence settings are deleted.

  **Commit 2, page and records: `f26cede80`.** Everything is under
  `Probes/leave-out/`:
  - `choice/model.mjs`, `page.mjs`, `page.html`, `build.mjs`, `look.mjs`,
  `as-text.ts`, `choice.html`
  - the twin's outputs and 12 pictures with their 2 records
  - `git-agreement.ts`, plus two new probes, `tfvc-agreement.ts` and
  `proposals.ts`
  - `listings/one-list-git-agreement.json`, `one-list-tfvc-agreement.json`,
  `proposals.json`
  - the README's eighth-leg section

  | this repo (worktree) | 23,554 | 1 | 46 |
