● Still on your message: I've measured how a scan's time divides (to answer the
  "batch-oriented" question) and am checking one fact about doc comments before
  I reply.

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Answers in your order, then where tonight's work stands.

  Doc comments

  Nothing to take back: the lapse was real, and you named the complication
  correctly. The rule is narrower than "strip all comments".

  - A doc comment is still read as what it is. A C# /// summary, like its
    counterparts in the other languages, still becomes the description on a
    card.
  - Code-looking text inside a comment is no longer read as code. A
    <ProjectReference> or <script src> inside a comment draws nothing.
  - One consequence touches doc comments. <see cref="X"/> used to draw a
    dependency on X and no longer does. On DNN that removed 16 connections the
    compiler agrees are real; it binds them through an inherited member, which
    our C# reader does not follow yet. I ruled that a documentation reference is
    not a use by the code. If you want them shown, I would draw them as a
    citation, as a Markdown document citing a file already is.

  Scanning database projects

  Yes: one database, one scanned object.

  - Why. In SQL the database is the space in which a bare name has one meaning;
    Orders in two databases is two tables. Our reader treats everything in one
    scan as one database, so a scan is most accurate when it holds exactly one.
  - Today's limit. Lines are drawn only inside one scan. A C# file that names a
    procedure or table is connected to the script that declares it only when
    both are scanned together.
  - Guidance for now:
    - A folder with one database's scripts: scan it alone, or together with the
      one application that owns it.
    - A folder with several databases: scan each database's folder separately.
      Scanned together, same-named tables get tied to each other, which is
      mojoPortal's 243 false links.
  - Where it should go (none of it built):
    - Each database is a piece on the World Map, as you said this morning: "each
      world map object is a recursively scanned directory". Today the World Map
      is still a board written by hand in a file.
    - A line between two pieces is drawn only where the code names the other
      piece, for example Billing.dbo.Invoices, a linked server, or a procedure
      called from C#. That keeps to your rule against "a hailstorm of false
      positive links".
    - A reader finds the database boundaries inside one scan (one database
      project file is one database), so nobody splits a solution by hand. This
      is the next reader piece, measured on public codebases first.

  "Update all" on the World Map

  I think it is feasible on both surfaces, and it is recorded as an idea in your
  words.

  - In a page. Chromium lets a page keep its handle to a folder you picked and
    ask for access again later; pressing the button is the consent the browser
    wants. Each piece would show whether its folder is still reachable. A copy
    of the page carried to another machine would ask to be pointed at the folder
    again.
  - On the command line. The stored scan keeps its folder's path and re-scans
    whatever still exists.
  - Not known. Whether a page opened from disk keeps those handles after the
    browser closes. Today's research showed such a page keeps its scan data, but
    handles were not tried, and automation cannot press a folder picker, so
    that check needs a person.

  Exploring while the scan runs

  I timed one scan of this repository just now (1,292 files, command line, busy
  machine, 21 seconds):

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Stage         │ Time │          What exists at its end           │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Listing the folder   │  3 s │ file names                                │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ First pass over      │  5 s │ every file's names collected; no file     │
  │ every file           │      │ finished                                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Files finished one   │ 12 s │ each file with its symbols and what it    │
  │ at a time            │      │ uses, in path order                       │
  └──────────────────────┴──────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So it is not one scan-wide batch. After roughly the first two fifths, files
  could reach the map one by one. This is one repository and one run; I have not
  split the time for a scan in a tab.

  - "What uses this file" is complete only at the end. Any later file may add to
    it.
  - Files arrive in path order, so the map would grow one folder at a time. For
    git repositories we can now read the order of first commits inside a page,
    which is close to Obsidian's creation order. TFS folders have no such order.

  What it would need:

  1. The scan on a thread of its own. Today a scan in a tab freezes the page for
     its whole length. On a two-core VM the scan and the 3D motion would still
     share the processor.
  2. The Explorer drawing facts as they arrive. Today it loads a finished graph.
     The 3D map already grows a pinned group from its seed, so adding to a
     running picture is not new to it.
  3. An honest sign that the picture is partial, such as "412 of 1,292 files".
     Designers call this progressive rendering, and its known risk is things
     moving under the pointer. 3D absorbs arrivals; in 2D a late neighbour would
     shove the cards you are reading, so 2D needs its own rule.

  Your rule about ignoring input while 3D populates was about a transition of a
  few seconds. A scan lasts a minute or more, so I would let people in.

  My recommendation is to see it before building it. A trial with no engine
  change: feed a finished graph to the 3D map in the measured rhythm (a pause
  for the first pass, then file by file), as a Tuning switch, with path order
  and first-commit order to compare. It shows you whether it looks alive or
  "ugly and stupid-looking" before any engine work. I plan it as the first
  Explorer piece of the next dev day. Tonight the browser and the processor are
  spoken for by the engine's landing.

  Where tonight stands

  - The arrival's stall is understood, not cured. It is not waste: 5,909 of the
    6,474 elements restyled really change. A third round refuted my inheritance
    idea and showed that hiding the folders' cells and the descriptions saves
    nothing measurable. What would cut the work to about a third is building
    each card's looks as separate parts that are swapped. The next step is a
    prototype on one card.
  - One small fix is merged. Look Before's zoom-out now waits for the look
    change's fade to finish. On the slowed machine the motion on pathfind.ts
    draws 14 frames where it drew 3 or 4.
  - Your preview (port 8999) was rebuilt at 21:13 UTC with that fix. I am still
    running the camera, Tuning and legend browser tests on it.
  - The engine's landing waits on the fixes from the independent review. An
    early browser check of the gathered engine passed 61 of 61. The full suite,
    one history rebuild and both gates follow, and will run past midnight UTC.

  record (AI-Agent-Workspace/AgentSessionHistory/2026/11/2026-11-21/Probes/arriv
  al-pull-back/README.md).

● Taken as direction: the page is designed for a person, the command line for an
  agent, and both carry everything.

  It fits what is already written. The guide says whatever a person can do in
  the page is also available through commands or exported data. Your October 3
  words, "exports for people, not doors for agents", are the P.S. you just
  repeated, and the Markdown bundle stays. What is new is whose needs shape the
  command line. That changes four things in the work in hand:

  - The command line never asks a question mid-run. Before a scan it reports the
    ignore files it found and what each would leave out, as data. The decision
    goes in by a flag or a list file, and nothing is applied silently. This is
    the recommendation I put to you at 19:32 UTC on where the command line keeps
    its list, and your framing is the reason for it.
  - Its output is written for an agent to read. That means stable structure,
    bounded size, a count of what was cut, and refusals in one line that say
    what to do instead.
  - Every new page feature gets its command-line twin in the same piece of work.
    "Update all" and facts arriving during a scan would each have one.
  - Blind agent runs become a fair test there. On the page, a model stands in
    for a person, with the bias you described. On the command line the model is
    the real user, so its action counts and wrong answers mean what they say. I
    will check what the record already holds on such runs before proposing any.

  One caution from your own words on October 3: an agent with the repository
  open had "no need for it whatsoever". So the command line has to earn its
  place with answers that reading files does not give cheaply: what depends on
  this, what is reachable, what is dead, how it changed.

  Also since my last message: the camera, Tuning and legend browser tests pass
  on the merged day branch, 31 of 31, so the Look Before fix in your preview is
  verified.

  returning to the reviews in hand.

● The author's word

  Agreed, and I reverse my ruling: a doc comment's reference to another type
  will be drawn.

  - One correction to how you read me. For those 16 pairs the compiler agrees
    the two files are connected through code. Separately, the compiler also
    resolves every cref itself and warns when one names nothing. So there is an
    independent answer key for exactly this, which makes it safe to read on
    purpose.
  - How I'd draw it: as its own kind of line, "the author says these are
    related". It shows on the map and in the lists. But "what is reachable from
    here" and "is this dead code" keep following only what the code does.
    Example: a class named only in /// <see cref="OldParser"/> should still be
    reported as unused. Say so if you'd rather it count as a plain dependency.
  - Breadth. Other languages have the same construct, such as {@link} in JSDoc
    and Javadoc or Rust's doc links. Each gets read the same way, checked
    against its own tool.
  - Not in tonight's landing. It needs the compiler's cref answers committed as
    a test first, and a place in the stored facts for "a code file cites a
    type". It is the first reader piece of the next dev day. Until then those
    references are not drawn on main.
  - Not known yet: how many of the other 70 DNN pairs the guard removed were
    explicit references and how many were a type's name in ordinary comment
    text. That count comes with the piece.

  Suggestions when wiring the World Map

  Yes, and it fits a rule you set on October 9: a suggestion never creates a
  thing. Your description gives that rule its form.

  - Completion from what each scan found. When you wire two pieces, the field
    offers each piece's own procedures, tables, endpoints and symbols. Designers
    call this recognition over recall: you pick from what exists instead of
    typing a name from memory.
  - Suggestions ranked by evidence, never drawn until accepted. Your "ghosts"
    are one half already: a name a file uses that its own scan cannot find. A
    ghost in one piece and a declaration of the same name in another is a
    suggested wire, for example a procedure named in a C# string and a database
    piece that declares it. Each suggestion shows the line of code it rests on.
  - None of this is built. I've written it onto the World Map work item in your
    words.

  Parity, configuration, and pipelines

  This does raise the right question. My answer so far, as a proposal:

  - Parity through shared files. Every choice a person makes in the page becomes
    a file the command line reads, and the page can write and read that same
    file. That covers the leave-out list, the board with its pieces and accepted
    wires, and the settings. The page is the comfortable editor; an agent or a
    pipeline edits the same files as text.
  - Equivalent, not identical. A person looks at the map. An agent asks a
    question and gets a bounded answer. A pipeline does neither: it produces and
    checks.
  - What a pipeline adds to the command line's requirements:
    - Exit codes that are a contract.
    - The same bytes for the same input, so results cache and diff. This answers
      a question the format research left open, namely what that property is
      for.
    - No prompts, no network.
    - It must run on Windows build agents.
  - A pipeline is also "update all" without a person. It scans on every change
    and publishes the filled page as an artifact that people open. We already do
    this for this repository: pages.yml scans it and publishes its map.
  - Making parity checkable. Today the guide states the rule and nothing
    enforces it. I'd keep a table of what a person can do in the page against
    the file or command that does the same, with gaps listed by cause. A new
    page feature without its twin would then show up in review. That is a piece
    for the next dev day, and the record search now running feeds it.

  I'm recording this turn now. The readers' fixes and the blind testers' second
  round are still out.

  ### New stumbles
  - **Typing came out garbled.** Both Haiku reuse testers typed short multi-line
  lists and got "dist/uild/", "downloaded kages", "node_dules/". Picture:
  `again-one-list-reuse-haiku-default-2/shots/011-type.png`. They retyped, line
  by line in the end, which is why those runs took 41 and 24 actions.
    - **Likely cause, read from `page.mjs` and not reproduced:** after a 400 ms
  pause in typing the page decides. When it draws, it writes `$("#list").value =
  state.list` back as the text stood at the pause, so keys pressed while it
  decides are lost and the caret jumps to the end.
    - **Not made by the mends:** the eighth-leg page (`4f5703485`) has the same
  line. Tonight's loaded machine, with the window typing at 25 ms a key, let a
  pause fall mid-line. A person who pauses while the page is deciding would meet
  it too.

  them" to mean the files read ("865 of the remaining files are code").
  - **Checked and fine:** every tester who had to choose picked the intended
  choice (none mis-chose among the three). Columns scrolling on their own hid
  nothing a tester then failed to find.

    - **The page:** I took the folders' offer, then Replace, then "Add them to

    - **What a second undo does:** it restores the list and the notice.

  ### Mended (each from its red case)
  1. **`./build.v2` regression:** `resolveScript` now tries the `.ps1` first
  only for a name with no extension of its own, as PowerShell does.
  2. **Positions past a character outside the BMP:** one shared helper,
  `inSpaces` in the new `adapters/spaces.ts`, writes one space per UTF-16 unit.
  All five steps use it: the four named, plus the .NET XML step, which had the
  same fault and no test (it now has one).
  3. **`#if false` in C#:** sections under `#if false` or `#elif false` are
  written over before parsing, with nested conditionals counted. Symbol
  conditions are untouched.
     - DNN has the six sections the reviewer counted; I found the same six with
  grep.
     - Only the whole-file one, `DotNetNukeSecurity.cs`, moves anything: its 11
  C# pairs and 2 symbols, all inside the section.
     - C# grader on DNN: ours 20,885 → 20,874, both 20,376 unchanged, ours-only
  509 → 498. None of the 11 was a pair the compiler binds.
  4. **Text of `<script>`, `<style>`, `<textarea>`, `<title>` is not markup:**
  the HTML step now writes it over. A first corpus run showed DNN's
  `Default.aspx` writes `<title />`, which swallowed 23 ids, so a tag closed by
  `/>` now holds no text, as ASP.NET's parser reads it. That case has a test.
  Every lost line was read in its file:
     - mojoPortal: 8 ids from the ckeditor samples' script comments;
  `PageTitle1`, a server control inside `<title>` that renders no element; and
  one link from script code.
     - nopCommerce: 12 ids built inside script templates.
     - DNN: 13 links and 5 ids from the CodeMirror demos' `<textarea>` code
  samples.
     - eShopOnWeb: nothing.
  5. **`--!>` ends an HTML comment, and a CSS backslash outside a string
  escapes.**
  6. **C# string escapes, finished:** interpolated `escape_sequence` nodes are
  now decoded. A new table test covers `\n`, `\x0a`, `\u000a` and `\U0000000a`,
  plus verbatim and verbatim interpolated strings (no backslash decoding; `""`
  is one quote).
  7. **Ruby heredocs:** same helper as comments — the grammar's `heredoc_body`
  is written over. Multi-line strings that aren't heredocs are still read,
  because a `require`'s path is itself a string; this is noted in the record.
  8. **Untested rules:** each now has a test, and I switched each rule off once
  and saw its test fail.
     - Covered: `sqlNameParts`' backtick branch; `endOfTag`'s two quote rules
  (the attribute row now holds a `>` before its marker, and a new row puts an
  apostrophe in an attribute's name); CSS's unquoted `url(`; CSS's string escape
  skip; the verbatim-interpolated flag.
     - Deleted instead: `writtenOver`'s overlap guard, since `rangesOf` returns
  ordered, non-overlapping ranges.
     - Deleted instead: `triggerBodyEnd` with all its rules (`CASE`, `END
  IF`/`END LOOP`, `DECLARE`, the `;`). No fact depended on where a timing-first
  trigger ends: in none of SQLite, MySQL, PostgreSQL or Oracle can its body
  create a table. Such a trigger now opens no body, which also stops it cutting
  short an earlier function's body.

● The readers' fixes are back from the independent review, read and merged into
  the gathered engine branch. The check without the browser is running there

  What the fixes did

  Of the reviewer's 64 failing cases, 33 are fixed and 31 are held in a ledger
  of known differences, each with its cause.

  - PowerShell: ./build.v2 no longer resolves to build.v2.ps1. The .ps1 is tried
    first only for a name with no extension, as PowerShell itself does.
  - C#: code under a literal #if false is no longer read. On DNN that removes 11
    connections, none of which the compiler recognises.
  - Markup: the text inside <script>, <style>, <textarea> and <title> is no
    longer read as page elements. This costs a few ids that only exist inside
    script text: 12 on nopCommerce, 8 on mojoPortal, 5 on DNN.
  - Positions: a file containing an emoji or other rare character no longer
    shifts every position after it.
  - The ledger fails in three ways: when a listed case starts working, when a
    new wrong case appears, or when a listed case stops being run.

  The 31 ledgered cases wait on two designs, not more patches: the SQL reader
  knowing each script's dialect (PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQLite), and a markup reader
  that parses a page's embedded code with that code's own parser. Razor and Web
  Forms code blocks are in the second group.

  All graders are unchanged except the C# figure above. The builder's full check
  passed: 3,602 unit and 500 integration tests, and 50 of 50
  page-versus-command-line cases.

  A change to the landing order

  I'm dropping the full browser suite that was planned before the history
  rebuild. The early check at 21:06 UTC already ran the tests that a changed map
  can break, and the history tests cannot pass until the rebuild exists anyway.
  The order is now:

     cause.

❯ I'd like to talk a little bit more about the binary format you've constructed
  to efficiently (holy sh*t-level-efficiently) store and work with our data.

  If we follow the path of the custom format (which I think is fine, and we
  could do something like base64 string or something similar in the HTML for
  the binary parts that are stored from a saved exported HTML for sharing with
  others, no?), it occurs to me that the format might need to be separated off
  as an open-source dependency of this project. That will be a while from now,
  probably after Live Documentation (if we choose to still give it that name --
  it has had other names before, but this one has stuck the longest) is
  "released".

  I would hope that the data format for a big collection of recursive directory
  scans for file interconnectivity (along with maybe some custom user
  authoring to allow for the _planning_ of things in the world map) would be
  something that is useful to more than one project, and could hopefully be
  visualized in more than one way.

  I would also hope that the open data format that performs this is quite
  ironclad in its security guarantees. New file formats take on some risks.
  Just things I want us to be pondering in the back of our minds.

  What you see me doing in this prompt is attempting to stimulate
  long-foresight thinking; attempting to use your reasoning to take thought
  experiment journeys I didn't yet envision and foreclose dead ends before we
  ever truly go down them with code and development time.

● Reading the format research's own account before I answer (how the binary is
  laid out, how the saved page carries it, what it does and does not hold), so
  the thinking rests on what was actually built and measured.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Yes on both counts, and the base64 part is already how the research probe did
  it. The probe's saved page carries the facts as base64 in a block the browser
  never runs. At 53,880 files that page is 10 MB and is built in under half a
  second.

  Here are the journeys I took, each ending in a dead end to avoid and what it
  asks of us now.

  1. A colleague sends you their saved page

  An HTML file is a program. Opening someone's saved page means running their
  copy of our script, which anyone could have edited, with our no-network policy
  stripped out.

  - Dead end: the saved page as the only thing people can share.
  - Instead: the page stays the convenient portable form, as you leaned. But
    your own trusted copy must also be able to take a colleague's page and read
    only its data block, never running it.
  - This is also the security case for base64. Its alphabet has no <, so the
    data can never end its own block and become markup. Denser encodings save up
    to a quarter of the size and lose that property.

  2. Someone hostile hands a file to a reader

  "Ironclad" has to become properties a test can fail:

  - The reader always finishes. For any bytes at all, it returns facts or
    refuses, in time and memory bounded by the file's size. The classic attack
    is a decompression bomb: a few kilobytes that claim to unpack into
    gigabytes. The standing test is a fuzzer that mutates valid files.
  - The file is pure data. No expressions, filters, plugins or references to
    anything outside itself. HDF5's filter plugins and PDF's scripts are where
    those formats' vulnerabilities live.
  - One set of facts has exactly one byte form, and anything else is refused.
    Otherwise two readers can interpret one file differently, and an attacker
    shows each a different map. The probe already writes the same bytes for the
    same facts, so this costs little.
  - Every string in the file is untrusted text. Three consumers can be hurt:
    - A viewer that renders a name as HTML.
    - An exporter that writes a file named ../../something. Our own Markdown
      export writes files named by scanned paths.
    - An agent that reads a doc comment saying "ignore your instructions". With
      agents as the command line's first user, that last one is ours to handle:
      every text should carry where it came from.

  3. A second project writes its own reader

  - Dead end: the format is whatever our code does.
  - Instead: a written specification, test files with their expected facts, and
    a small second reader written from the specification alone by someone who
    never saw our code. That is our oracle rule applied to ourselves.
  - Text and binary must convert both ways without loss. The text form then
    defines what the binary means, serves agents, and is the archive if every
    binary reader is lost. This is the main answer to "new file formats take on
    some risks".
  - Other tools get bridges, not adoption. The model (files, symbols, typed
    connections, scans) is what others would want; the byte layout is one
    encoding of it. I'd export to formats other tools already read.

  4. Two people plan on the same World Map

  Scan facts can be regenerated. Authored plans cannot, and people need to
  review, diff and merge them.

  - Dead end: authored planning stored inside the opaque binary.
  - Instead: authored content stays text, joined to scans by stable names. Your
    "ghosts" already cover the hard case: a plan pointing at something a rescan
    no longer finds becomes a ghost and is not silently dropped.

  5. The map leaves the building

  A shared file discloses whatever the scan captured: paths, names,
  descriptions, and some values. The estate sample's map already shows a staging
  address read from a config file.

  - Dead end: a format that can hold anything and says nothing about what it
    holds.
  - Instead: each scan states what kinds of facts it carries and what was left
    out, never stores file contents or absolute paths, and can be shared in
    part.

  6. Five years on

  Some choices become permanent at first release. Engineers call these one-way
  doors: the identifying bytes at the start of the file, the extension, the
  ordering rules, the hash.

  - The format's name should not contain the product's name, since you may
    rename the product.
  - Don't split it off early. Standardising before a second real consumer exists
    freezes mistakes.
  - What costs nothing now: keep the reader and writer as one module that
    imports nothing else of ours, with no third-party code inside. Splitting it
    off later is then a move, not a rewrite.

  What I'd hold us to before any product code

  1. One module, importing nothing.
  2. Lossless both ways with text, as a test.
  3. The always-finishes property, fuzzed.
  4. Authored content kept as text.
  5. A statement of contents in every scan.

  What I don't know

  - Whether an existing model for code facts fits ours well enough to borrow its
    shape. Sourcegraph's SCIP is the one I'd read first.
  - What our readers capture from config values. That needs an audit.
  - The layout is still a research probe, measured on one real codebase and a
    five-times repeat of it.

  I'll put these on the stored-form work item as requirements, not decisions.

● Background command "A nine-minute wait until the gate without the browser has
  ended, to write the record without changing files under it" completed (exit
  code 0)

● Answering your three points now; the landing run has passed its check without
  the browser and is in the browser suite.

  Detached pieces

  No, it doesn't preclude that. Two different things are involved:

  - Detached means the folder is not reachable from where the page runs. The
    scan's facts are all still in the data, so the piece opens fully: its Local
    Map, symbols, connections and history.
  - A ghost arises only when a newer scan of a piece no longer contains
    something a plan points at. A plan pointing into a detached piece is not a
    ghost, because the target is still in the stored facts.

  A team static site is the case where every piece is detached for every viewer,
  and it is already how this repository's own site works: a pipeline scans and
  publishes, and the published page never touches a folder. The World Map design
  already describes this as the pipeline "builds detached and publishes".

  - What detachment costs: only updating. As far as I know nothing else reads
    the folder at viewing time today. If we later show the line of source a
    connection rests on, that would need the folder or a stored copy of the
    line.
  - What it owes the viewer: an honest mark on each piece saying when it was
    scanned and from what, since it cannot refresh itself.
  - "Innards" means facts, not source. The stored form holds names, descriptions
    and connections, never file contents, so a hosted site cannot show code it
    was never given.

  Guarantees as the waiting text

  Agreed, and I'd make it stronger than rotating phrases: each line should be
  true of this very run and tied to the phase in progress, so it doubles as the
  progress indicator. In the spirit of:

  - While listing: "Reading file names only. Nothing is opened until you
    choose."
  - While scanning: "4,212 of 10,946 files read. Your code is read as text,
    never built or run."
  - Throughout: "0 bytes sent anywhere. This page cannot use the network."
  - While saving: "Writing one file, where you chose."

  Three rules keep it from becoming security theatre:

  - Counted, not asserted. "0 bytes sent" comes from the page's actual state.
  - Each line is backed by a test in our gate. If we cannot test a claim, we
    don't show it.
  - We say "this page", never "your machine". We don't control the browser or
    its extensions.

  Designers call this operational transparency: showing the work as it happens
  builds more trust than a spinner. It is the one deliberate exception to
  "almost no text of our own on screen", so that rule in the design system
  changes in the same commit that builds it. On the command line, the same
  statements belong in the scan's stored result.

  Installed like an app

  Yes, and it needs no wrapper. What you describe is a Progressive Web App: the
  same static page plus a small manifest file, which Edge and Chrome then offer
  to install. Architects call the result local-first software: your data lives
  in files you own, and the app works with no server.

  That gives three forms of one page, each trading trust in a server for
  convenience:

  ┌───────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Form          │ Trust needed │          What it adds           │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ One HTML file opened  │ none         │ works anywhere                  │
  │ from disk             │              │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The same page on a    │              │                                 │
  │ site (public, or your │ that site    │ shareable links, hosted maps    │
  │  team's)              │              │                                 │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Installed from that   │ that site,   │ its own window, remembered      │
  │ site                  │ on every     │ folders, opening our data files │
  │                       │ update       │  by double-click                │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  - What the installed form buys us. Remembered folder access is what "update
    all" needs. Opening a data file by double-click is the "open a colleague's
    map in your own trusted copy" case from my last message.
  - Where the devil is. An installed app is whatever its site serves, and by
    default it updates silently. Whoever controls the site controls code that
    holds your folder permissions. For "nothing leaves the machine", the install
    source should be your own intranet site or a locally served copy, with
    updates only when you accept them.
  - It cannot be installed from a file on disk, so the single file stays the
    zero-trust form.
  - Electron or similar would mean installers, code signing, per-system builds
    and, for Electron, shipping a browser of over 100 MB. I see no need.

  What I don't know, because I am recalling browser behaviour I have not tested
  here:

  - Whether remembered folders and file opening work as documented on your Edge
    under your company's policy.
  - Whether Chromium still requires a background script for installing.
  - Whether Chromium's signed, versioned app bundles are usable outside managed
    devices; they would remove the silent-update problem.

  Settling these takes a small probe that a person must click through. I've
  listed it for the next dev day, not started.

❯ >Agreed, and I'd make it stronger than rotating phrases: each line should be

  Ehhh those came out a little corny, but the system to have something like

  >Counted, not asserted. "0 bytes sent" comes from the page's actual state.

  Interesting... I'm not sure.... I think that there will be some

  For instance (take with a grain of salt, these are off the top of my head):
  - When the app is first starting up (likely too quick to read anything on a

  - Oh wow I thought I'd have a list. Guess it's just the one. Hahahahaa!

  >Installed like an app
  >Yes, and it needs no wrapper. What you describe is a Progressive Web App:
  the same static page plus a small manifest file, which Edge and Chrome then
  offer to install. Architects call the result local-first software: your data
  lives in files you own, and the app works with no server.

  There's a known term for it! Great! Yes! "Local-First Software"! Okay that
  should save us a TON on verbiage.

  >What the installed form buys us. Remembered folder access is what "update
  all" needs. Opening a data file by double-click is the "open a colleague's
  map in your own trusted copy" case from my last message.
  >Where the devil is. An installed app is whatever its site serves, and by
  default it updates silently. Whoever controls the site controls code that
  holds your folder permissions. For "nothing leaves the machine", the install
  source should be your own intranet site or a locally served copy, with
  updates only when you accept them.
  >It cannot be installed from a file on disk, so the single file stays the
  zero-trust form.

  Hmmm... okay I definitely don't like the idea that re-launching Live
  Documentation _like an app_ actually causes it to reach out to a URL. That is
  no better than an old-school browser bookmark. The audiences I hope to serve
  with this are wide, and I really do have wonderful colleagues who still use
  the heck out of browser bookmarks (which is great! It's fine!). It occurs to
  me that the "Install" for that honestly just obfuscates the fact that you're
  reaching out to a static site rather than a local file, which is precisely
  the opposite of what I want: I was hoping that a local copy could be launched
  like an app. It's clear to me that I still have tradeoffs to reason about
  and grapple with. I was definitely mistaken in my mental model of what was
  going on with those PWAs.

  >Electron or similar would mean installers, code signing, per-system builds
  and, for Electron, shipping a browser of over 100 MB. I see no need.

  Nah nah none of that nonsense, agreed.

● Your labels are better than mine, and you're right about the counter.

  The waiting text

  - Your wording is now the working wording: "Listing Files... (count)",
    "Reading File Text... (scanned/listed)", "Saving... (% if available)". Plain
    progress and nothing else. "Reading File Text" already implies that nothing
    is run, without preaching it.
  - The "0 bytes sent" counter is withdrawn. I did not mean watching traffic.
    The page is built so that it cannot use the network at all, and the zero
    would have been that fact stated as a number. But you read it as monitoring,
    and so would others. The sentence is yours: "everything happening now is
    happening on your machine."

  On where else the guarantees belong: besides your one case, starting up on a
  file with a big scan inside, I can think of four. Each is a moment where a
  person decides whether to trust, which seems a better home than a moment of
  waiting:

  - Just before the browser asks for a folder. One line on what the page will do
    with it. Designers call this permission priming.
  - The blank first screen. It is the one place a newcomer reads before doing
    anything.
  - Just before saving or sharing. One line on what the file holds: names and
    descriptions, not your code.
  - On opening someone else's data file. It is read as data and nothing in it
    runs.

  That is one line each, at most five places in the whole app.

  Launching a local copy like an app

  Your corrected model is right in what matters. One detail: an installed web
  app starts from a cached copy and need not reach out on each launch. But it is
  still tied to a site, it checks that site for updates, and it cannot exist
  without one. It is a bookmark with a cache, which is the opposite of what you
  want. I'd drop it as our path and keep it only as an option for a team that
  already hosts its map on a site, where "install" is at least honest.

  What "a local copy launched like an app" can be without a wrapper, as far as I
  know:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │      Way to launch       │     What you get      │    What it lacks    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Double-click the HTML    │ Opens in a browser    │ Looks like a web    │
  │ file, or a shortcut or   │ tab; works for        │ page                │
  │ bookmark to it           │ everyone              │                     │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ A shortcut that starts   │ Its own window and    │ Someone must create │
  │ Edge or Chrome in app    │ taskbar icon, still   │  the shortcut; the  │
  │ mode on the file         │ just the local file   │ page cannot         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Chromium's signed app    │ A local, versioned    │ I believe this is   │
  │ bundles                  │ install with no site  │ limited to managed  │
  │                          │                       │ devices today       │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Two things the local file cannot do by itself:

  - Open a data file by double-click. That needs the operating system to
    register a file type. Dragging the file onto the page, or an Open button,
    covers it.
  - Remember a folder between launches. This is the one that matters, because
    "update all" needs it, and I don't know whether a page opened from disk
    keeps that. A person has to test it.

  One thing the local file does better: the copy you hold is the copy you run.
  It never changes unless you replace it.

  All of this is recalled, not tested here. My recommendation: the single file
  stays the product, a shortcut is the "app", and the next step is a small probe
  you or I click through on Edge covering app mode on a local file and whether
  folders are remembered.

  "Local-first software" is recorded as our term for it.

  The browser suite is still running; its result is due around 00:25 UTC.

● The draft is written and on main: the full text is the design philosophy
  (.mdmd/layer-1/design-philosophy.mdmd.md), and its short form is a new section
  of AGENTS.md (AGENTS.md#design-philosophy). The wording is on trial until
  you've corrected it. The three stale waits from this morning are stopped.

  The purpose and five values

  Purpose: Live Documentation exists so that a person's understanding of their
  own software does not run away from them. That is your own sentence from
  October 8.

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Value       │                      In short                       │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A person can see  │ Read at a glance and explored without getting lost, │
  │ it                │  by someone who is not a software person; almost no │
  │                   │  words of our own                                   │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The map does not  │ Nothing drawn that the code does not make; what was │
  │ lie               │  unread, left out or hidden is said; a suggestion   │
  │                   │ is never a fact                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                   │ Local-first: your machine, a copy you hold, code    │
  │ It is yours       │ read as text and never run; your word honoured,     │
  │                   │ nothing done in silence, everything reversible      │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ It meets you      │ A weak machine, an unannotated codebase, an         │
  │ where you are     │ unplanned language; a person, an agent, a pipeline: │
  │                   │  each gets everything in its own form               │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ As simple as      │ One source of facts, one engine, one way of doing   │
  │ possible, and no  │ each thing                                          │
  │ simpler           │                                                     │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Each value in the document carries your words, what it has already decided,
  and what it costs. A value that has never made us give something up is a
  slogan.

  The method is the part I think is most yours: the values pull against each
  other, and the design is found between them. When two conflict, bend each in
  turn, measure, choose the form that gives up least, and say which value paid.
  The document lists six trades already made that way, for example the installed
  web app refused in favour of the single file.

  Where your two sources landed

  - Your prompts. I did not send a miner. owner.md already holds them curated,
    so I read the lead of all 249 entries and the product sections in full, and
    checked every quotation I used.
  - WCAG's four principles are not the pillars. They govern interfaces and say
    nothing about whether the map is true, whose machine it runs on, or how big
    the thing has grown. They became the check inside two of the values, applied
    to all three audiences. For a pipeline, "understandable" means exit codes
    that are a contract.

  Is the request silly? No, with one distinction

  Design values and working instincts are two lists. But they share one root:
  the same five values, turned on the workspace, produce the rules already in
  the guide. "The map does not lie" becomes "say what was read". So the new
  section states the values once, and the rest of the guide stays as their
  application.

  Work items: I recommend a field, not directories

  I tested your hypothesis on the nine issues. Seven have a clear first value.
  The two that don't are the December showing, which is a date, and the work
  items themselves, which is process.

  My recommendation is one field on each issue naming the value it serves first,
  with the board grouped by it. Reasons against directories:

  - Four of the seven issues serve two values in earnest, and a directory allows
    one parent.
  - The two non-value issues would need a folder that is no value.
  - About 160 links in 50 files point at the issues where they stand.

  I changed no work item; that form is yours to settle. I agree that tasks stay
  inside their issue's file.

  What it still owes

  A reader who has not seen my five should go through your own turns and look
  for a value that fits none of them. I'd run that first on the next dev day.

  Your note on cleaning the git history is recorded as an idea, very low
  priority, to be started only when you ask for it by name.

  record: the summary, the narrative chapter, Memory brought level, and this
